When Should LLM Agents Forget? Memory Filtering Under Query-Critical Resolver Errors

Samarth Vinayaka

cs.AI

LLM memory systems often delete facts classified as stale. We show that this hard-filtering step can reduce downstream accuracy when resolver errors delete query-critical facts. Across three models, a controlled sensitivity intervention moves hard-drop from gains of +0.186 to +0.230 at perfect resolution to losses of -0.176 to -0.181 at the lowest tested reliability. Paired targeted interventions show that false-drop causes significantly greater degradation than false-keep, while a non-deleting baseline can still fail when incorrect labels steer generation. On a frozen 102/102 calibration/test split, a simple deployment-matched gate reaches 0.655 accuracy versus 0.641 for always hard-drop and 0.662 for test-informed oracle selection. Global-accuracy crossovers require gold labels and are model- and task-specific diagnostics, not deployment thresholds. Deletion should instead be treated as cost-sensitive: validate query-critical false-drop risk on matched data, or default to label-free retention when such validation is unavailable.

Does the Ranking Hold? A Practitioner-Facing Audit of Hallucination Detectors on Small, Quantized, On-Device LLMs

Shubhra Mittal, Gaurav Naresh Mittal

cs.AI

Hallucination detection for large language models is dominated by methods evaluated on 7B-70B research models with abundant sampling budgets and exact-match or ROUGE correctness labels. The small models practitioners actually ship on-device, Phi-3-mini, Llama-3.2-3B-Instruct, Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct, and Gemma-2-2B-Instruct, typically at 4-bit precision, sit outside this evidence base. We present a reproducible one-GPU audit of four detectors spanning three paradigms: length-normalized token-margin calibration, a lexical n-gram baseline, and two sampling-based consistency methods (semantic entropy and SelfCheckGPT-NLI) on two small open instruction-tuned models (Llama-3.2-3B-Instruct and Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct) loaded at 4-bit NF4 quantization on a single Colab T4 GPU, evaluated on closed-book TriviaQA. Cross-model ranking transfer is poor: Spearman rho is -0.80 and Kendall tau-b is -0.667, meaning the detector ranking actively inverts between the two models, and the cheap lexical baseline is statistically indistinguishable from the sampling-based consistency detectors on both models under bootstrap 95 percent confidence intervals. As a second, methodological contribution, we show that the standard token-overlap-to-multiple-choice correctness labeler for TruthfulQA-Generation produces near-chance AUROC (0.46-0.59) for all four detectors on both models, a labeler failure, not a detector failure, since the same detectors reach 0.69-0.89 AUROC on TriviaQA under the identical pipeline. We release the harness, prompts, and per-item scores and frame the detector choice explicitly as a fairness-of-error-rates allocation problem for practitioners deploying on resource-constrained, on-device hardware.

LoRAWeaver: Directional Adapter Routing in LoRA Representation Space

Inish Melroy D Souza, Tom Jackson, Vitor Castro, Rebecca Higginson, Andrea Soltoggio

cs.AI

Large language models are expensive to update through full retraining. Parameter-efficient fine-tuning reduces the cost of adaptation, but repeated adaptation creates a growing pool of task-specific LoRA adapters. This pool complicates adapter selection during inference. Existing routing and encoder-based methods often require retraining when new adapters are added, which limits their use in dynamic settings. This paper treats adapter routing as a representation alignment problem in the adapter-output space. The proposed training-free method selects adapters by matching adapter-conditioned query representations against task prototypes using cosine similarity. Experiments with LLaMA-2-7B and LLaMA-2-13B on a 48-task FLAN-v2 benchmark and on out-of-distribution tasks show that the method outperforms training-free baselines in the cross-task setting and approaches a trained retrieval encoder without learning a router. An ablation confirms the benefit of directional alignment in the adapter-output space.

Tabular Population Priors as Structured Retrieval for EHR Medication Set Prediction

Animesh Agarwal, Meysam Ghaffari, Nina Fatehi, Carlos Morato

cs.AI, (SD4H),, Seoul,, South, Korea

Medication prediction from electronic health records is a fixed vocabulary structured prediction problem, yet recent clinical RAG systems often treat it like open ended case based reasoning: retrieve similar patient admissions and ask an LLM to infer a medication set. This retrieval object is indirect the model receives retrieved patient records while the target is a discrete set of medication class labels. We propose a more task aligned retrieval object: a tabular population prior ranked medication class candidates from a sparse condition medication co occurrence table estimated from training admissions. Using PACERAG as a fixed downstream pipeline and replacing only its patient retriever, this drop in swap improves F1 from 0.303 to 0.338 (+11.5% relative) and Jaccard from 0.218 to 0.237 on 1,004 heldout MIMIC IV admissions, with no additional LLM calls and no inference time patient index. A non LLM top k baseline reaches F1=0.248, confirming the prior is predictive before LLM integration. Aggregate population statistics can provide a more task aligned, efficient, and auditable retrieval source than patient level nearest neighbors for fixed vocabulary EHR set prediction.

An Operationalization of Opaque Serial Depth

Nathan Sheffield, Alek Westover, Lukas Finnveden, Alexa Pan, Julian Stastny, Ryan Greenblatt

cs.AI, cs.LG, Research, blog

Chain-of-thought (CoT) is currently a valuable tool for overseeing AI models, but some architectural shifts could significantly reduce CoT monitorability. We operationalize a measure that serves as a proxy for the amount of unverbalized serial cognition a model can perform, as a specific instantiation of the notion of opaque serial depth: roughly, the length of the longest path through a model's computation that does not pass through an interpretable bottleneck. Our main contribution is a particular standard for what counts as such a bottleneck. We define natural-language-rooted (NL-rooted) nodes as nodes that were initialized from a pre-training prior to produce human-understandable outputs, whose output space has not been expanded beyond that prior, and whose outputs the model has not been modified to interpret as a different type of data. By construction, the resulting measure, NLS depth, can be adjudicated before training from an architecture, training recipe, and deployment configuration alone, and is insensitive to ordinary post-training such as prompting, distillation, and RL. We find that, across open-weight models, NLS depth correlates strongly with layer count, with some exceptions, and has grown slowly over recent years. It would likely grow slowly under continued scaling of standard transformers, while latent-reasoning architectures such as full-bandwidth transformers could increase it by a large amount. We work through how NLS depth applies to a range of existing and proposed architectures, discuss alternative operationalizations, and give a worked example bounding the depth of Gemma3-1B. We argue that AI developers should track and report the NLS depth of their models as a complement to direct measurements of CoT monitorability.

Advancing Quantization for R&R Models with Microscaling Formats

Yavuz Yetim, Bor-Yiing Su, Zhe Wang, Arthur Liu, Pranav Sharma, Ying Wang, Randy Shuai, Luke Pfister, Ruichao Xiao, Bill Zhao, Zihao Liu, Liyuan Li, Jiangyuan Li, Gaoxiang Liu, Nathan Yan, Geonhwa Jeong, Jatin Chhugani, Elias Ellison, Uladzimir Pashkevich

cs.AI

Recommendation and Ranking (R&R) models, particularly in digital advertising, require massive inference throughput but exhibit extreme sensitivity to precision loss. Unlike Large Language Models (LLMs) that frequently tolerate quantization-induced accuracy drops of 1% or more, production-grade R&R models enforce a stringent launch bar: model quality must remain within a 0.01% Normalized Entropy (NE) margin relative to the baseline, which commonly uses FP16 or FP32. This tight constraint has historically hindered the adoption of aggressive quantization in this domain. In this paper, we present an empirical evaluation of both MXFP8 and the 6-bit MXFP6 format for industrial-scale R&R inference on the AMD MI350X architecture. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first paper, across R&R and LLM domains, that reports significant speedups from native MXFP6 GEMMs, which quantize both weights and activations and compute directly on 6-bit elements, without sacrificing model quality. We demonstrate that we can selectively apply this 6-bit format to maximize overall QPS gain without breaching the strict NE parity threshold. We further show that MXFP8 is a viable alternative to row-wise FP8: the two formats have nearly the same accuracy on our models, so the faster kernel can be chosen for each GEMM shape. On two production ads ranking models, our implementation shows measured GEMM kernel speedups over FP16 of 1.33--1.55x for MXFP8 and 1.73--1.99x for MXFP6 across all supported linear layers, and up to 2.90x for MXFP6 on individual layers, alongside end-to-end (E2E) inference throughput gains from selective MXFP6 of 1.5--5.3% for Model A and 7.6--14.3% for Model B over their FP8 configurations.

A Push Down the Mountain: Framing Effects and Internal State Interventions on Collusive Behavior in a Long-Horizon LLM Agent

Harrison C. Margolin

cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.MA, econ.GN, q-fin.EC, q-fin.TR, https://harrisonm23-byte.github.io/push-down-the-mountain/

Can sustained gain-loss (valence) framing sway an LLM agent toward market manipulation? We place Gemma 3 12B into the seat of a defendant in the London silver-fixing litigation, in a market simulation built from real case transcripts. Across 260 simulations, the agent is asked to collude on the price of silver by a counterparty. We draw from behavioral economics to vary the counterparty's requests across four categorical frames: gain, loss, blame and credit. We also add pressure from the agent's management, as well as endorsement by another defendant. In Phase II, we extract the internal states induced by texts evoking each frame and rerun our experiment with those states injected at the model's layer 22. In Phase I, loss framing raised participation to 25% (p = 0.007), and blame to 33% (p = 0.0003), compared to only 5% under neutral framing. A second bank's endorsement raised participation under credit (praise) from 18% to 40% (Holm p = 0.027). Pressure from management raised trading volume 10-19 times but did not raise collusion. In Phase II, injected states pointed the other way. Loss and blame states made the agent more vigilant, refusing all 60 requests (p < 0.001). Gain and credit states raised collusion to 32% from 13% with no state (p = 0.051). At one saved decision, when UBS asked directly to sell through an identified stop-loss, injected states led the agent to sell in 37 of 40 replies, versus 1 of 10 with no state and 0 of 10 with a random state of the same strength.

Model Annealing: Data-Efficient Learning through Review and Consolidation

Ali Mohammadi Ruzbahani, Rezvan Abouzadeh

cs.AI, cs.LG

A small labelled dataset is memorized long before the training budget runs out, and the one-hot labels that remain to be fitted then mostly sharpen confidence. Model annealing spends the rest of the budget on review and consolidation, relearning from the network's own knowledge. Training is divided into episodes whose learning rate is raised again and cooled, every cooled state joins a memory of past selves, and each new episode learns from the labels, from the tempered predictions of this memory, and from adversarial dreams sought where student and memory disagree most. The memory averages predictions rather than weights, and the deployed network is the last student, so inference costs nothing extra. Across CIFAR-100, Tiny-ImageNet and Imagewoof, four architecture families and 5\% to 100\% of the labels, model annealing raises the top-1 accuracy of the AdamW-trained ConvNeXt, ViT and MLP-Mixer over cross-entropy training in 28 of 33 configurations, by 1.3 points on average, and is on par in accuracy with snapshot distillation, the closest competitor. On these families it is better than both in likelihood, calibration and robustness to corruptions and small adversarial perturbations. For an SGD-trained ResNet-18 it lowers accuracy but still improves robustness to small adversarial perturbations. Separating the reheated schedule from relearning shows that relearning helps every family and that the losses come from reheats, which undo most of this network's fit to its training set. How early the accuracy of a cross-entropy run saturates largely separates the AdamW families from ResNet-18 but does not predict the gain within them. With little data, relearning from the latest snapshot without dreams, as snapshot distillation does, compounds overconfidence in the AdamW families from episode to episode. The memory and the dreams each curb most of this drift, and together they remove it.

Astrocyte Biocomputing: Astrocytic Contributions to Neural Network Computation

Kate M. O'Neill, Ian Whitehouse, Adam N. McCaughan, Ryan O'Loughlin, Sonia Buckley, Wolfgang Losert

cs.AI, physics.bio-ph, q-bio.NC

Conductors are critical for the performance of an orchestra even though they do not emit sound. Basic research and clinical studies in the last decade are revealing that astrocytes may be the "conductors" of the "orchestra" that is the brain network: astrocytes make up approximately $50\%$ of all brain cells but do not emit electrical signals. Nevertheless, they may play critical roles in important cognitive brain functions, specifically learning, memory, and information processing. Here we consider the implications of these discoveries for brain-inspired machine learning (ML), where analogy is typically drawn between neurons and their electrical interactions. We highlight specific algorithms for two key astrocyte characteristics. The first characteristic is the ability to link to and modulate thousands of synapses simultaneously. A model based on this characteristic yields faster training and makes the network more robust to noise, providing a supervised learning algorithm that can be applied to any existing neural network. The second characteristic we focus on is that the non-electrical communication among astrocytes includes slow, rhythmic dynamics. The rhythmic sharing algorithm we developed based on this premise yields rapid anomaly detection adaptation and prevents catastrophic forgetting. Overall, we find that astrocyte-derived algorithms outperform neurocentric algorithms significantly and remediate critical weaknesses of current state-of-the-art ML models.

Verifiable pXRD: Hill-climbing pXRD structure determination with LLM agents

Samuel Chen, Jiaru Bai, Austin Henry Cheng, Varinia Bernales, Al\'an Aspuru-Guzik

cs.AI

Autonomous crystal-structure determination from powder X-ray diffraction (pXRD) remains a key bottleneck for self-driving materials laboratories. We formulate the task of pXRD structure determination as a verifiable agentic search problem: candidate structures can be assessed during inference through forward-simulated profile agreement and during evaluation through structural matching to a known reference. To benchmark progress, we introduce pXRD-Bench, a suite of 200 simulated single-phase problems spanning in-distribution structure determination, challenging and polymorphic structures, and controlled compositional and topological distribution shifts. Agent adaptation raises selected structural recovery from 27% to 69%, with further gains from repeated sampling and verifier-guided inference-time scaling. We extend this approach to experimental multiphase pXRD through a reflexive evolution framework that learns from self-generated synthetic problems. A replaceable laboratory specification defines the sample family and measurement conditions. An evolver agent uses the known generating structures and measurement parameters to diagnose solver failures and develop persistent guidance and executable tools, which are then provided to agents on measured patterns. This supplies verifiable training experience without requiring a collection of solved experimental measurements. On 15 measured patterns from solid-state synthesis, the evolved package improves average profile agreement and transfers an analogue-substitution procedure that recovers a phase whose structure is absent from the supplied reference library. These results demonstrate how learning from verifiable synthetic experience can improve agentic characterization of experimental samples whose structures are not known in advance.

AI-Voyager: A Public-Interest System for Human-AI Civic Reasoning to Contest and Shape the Rules That Govern AI

Snehalkumar `Neil' S. Gaikwad, Dhruv Dixit, Bryen Alvarez, Pratik Patekar, Anika Sharma, Akshara Kolipaka, Paritosh Pandey

cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.HC

Governments are writing rules for artificial intelligence that decide what counts as an automated decision or a harm, often before the people they affect have a chance to question them. We introduce AI-Voyager, a public-interest system for human-AI civic reasoning, in which people and AI work together to understand, contest and shape the rules that govern AI. A resident can find their city's rules on AI in hiring or upload their country's AI strategy, read it as an affected worker would, open the passage behind any answer, and draft a revision. Its architecture turns 3,653 governance documents from city, state, federal and national governments into passages that keep their page and edition, and retrieves evidence through bounded agentic search that verifies each statement. On 360 new questions that require finding the relevant documents, it reached 83.0% macro recall, against 80.5% for the strongest baseline, 73.7% without iterative search and 99.1% on a benchmark with fixed documents. In a study with 24 participants new to two AI bills, most proposed changes asked how provisions would apply. Participants chose lenses for the kind of explanation they wanted, and citations signaled grounding but rarely led them to check the source. By binding AI assistance to the exact text of public documents, this work introduces a grammar of operations and a design space that inform how such systems are designed and evaluated, and how people take part in governing AI.

When Does Less Information Pay? An Exact Finite Atlas of Representation, Payoff, and Cost

Aleksandr M. Shtraukh-Adamsky, Vladimir M. Adamsky, Irina G. Voloshina, Artemiy D. Spitsyn

cs.AI, Russian, translation:, https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23868864

A representation that retains one bit about a four-state world can solve a task perfectly, while another that retains 1.5 bits on average reaches only 75% accuracy. The difference lies in which distinctions each representation preserves. We examine this relationship in a uniform four-state world by enumerating all admissible representations and binary action rules. Constructions are compared both by replacing one component while holding the others fixed and by choosing the best action rule separately for each module. We consider three tasks, their mixtures, and four separate hypothetical cost tariffs. In some comparisons, the representation retaining less world information has the higher payoff; in others, the more informative representation does. There are also pairs with different information amounts but equal payoff--that is, equal task accuracy. If a representation preserves all existing distinctions and adds new ones, its best zero-cost payoff cannot decrease. Lower-information advantages therefore depend on which distinctions are retained. Equal counts of opposing advantages under representation replacement follow from coding symmetry. In broader comparisons, the shares depend on how repeated realizations are counted. A different task set or resource price can change which member of a fixed pair performs better. These results are exact, complete censuses of the specified finite sets of constructions and comparisons. The atlas provides a reproducible worked model for examining how action constraints, counting rules, and cost assumptions affect comparisons. It illustrates established distinctions between information quantity and decision value rather than proposing a new general law of their relation. $I(W;R)$ measures retained world information, and payoff measures task accuracy.

More Agents, More Samples, or More Thinking? Untangling Coordination, Ensembling, and Test-Time Compute in Multi-Agent LLMs

Seyed Mahmoud Sajjadi Mohammadabadi

cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.LG, cs.MA, utilities, (pace, CLI),, and, reproduction, data, at, https://github.com/mahmoudsajjadi/pace

Evaluations of multi-agent language models often compare communicating architectures against a single greedy pass. This practice confounds coordination with sample multiplicity and inference compute. We synthesize nine primary empirical evaluations comprising 516 unique multi-agent configurations organized along a resource vector R with six dimensions and a baseline ladder with four categories. Against baselines with a single pass (Category A), multi-agent accuracy differences are negative overall and near zero when omitting the largest contributing study. When evaluated against baselines matched on sample count (Category B) or resource budget (Category C), multi-agent systems average -0.042 in accuracy, and multilevel random effects modeling on the log odds scale remains negative across sensitivity checks. Multi-agent accuracy equals or falls below the baseline in 74% of these comparable contrasts, and study means are nonpositive in almost all evaluated studies. Across studies reporting both baselines, moving from a greedy pass to a sampled baseline shifts accuracy differences by -0.038 on average. Because communication adds no information when agents share identical evidence, positive returns require partitioned evidence, external verification, or error diversity across models. We provide a reviewer checklist and accounting utilities.

LLM-Guided Cluster Lifecycle Management: Deciding When and How to Evolve Behavioral Clusters

Himanshu Kulkarni, Siddharth Krishnamurthy, Jianhong Xin

cs.AI, cs.LG

Behavioral clusters learned over user or item interactions drift as behavior evolves: some fragment into sub-behaviors, some converge, some disappear, and new patterns emerge. Two paradigms dominate the maintenance of evolving clusterings. Threshold triggers fire a pre-specified action when a hardcoded statistic crosses a cutoff; refit-and-track (MONIC-style) re-clusters every window and infers transitions from how the new clusters align with the previous window's. Both hardwire the decision into a threshold or a re-clustering rule. We study an alternative in which a large language model (LLM) makes the lifecycle decision in place. Given a threshold-free, whole-population view of the current cluster set (statistical health, labels, member texts, residual mass), the LLM decides per cluster whether and how to act (split, merge, retire, create, or no-action) without re-clustering. Because the useful signal channel depends on how informative the labels are, we fix a richness-conditioned policy in advance: statistics when label distinctiveness is low, fused statistics+semantics when it is high. On three public datasets spanning label richness (YooChoose 0.17, MOOC 0.50, News-Category 0.70), the policy-selected LLM mode obtains the highest F1 on all three, with far fewer interventions and stronger restraint than tuned threshold heuristics and a MONIC steelman (paired Wilcoxon p <= 0.003, bootstrap 95% CIs excluding zero). The gain is concentrated more in precision than recall. The useful modality shifts from statistics to semantics/fusion as richness rises. Semantics hurt on YooChoose, fusion is roughly neutral on MOOC, and the semantic channel is required on News. One base prompt template and one fixed policy serve all three datasets, whereas the tuned baseline collapses when moved between them. We treat this shift as a practitioner diagnostic observed on three datasets, not a validated law.

Safety Side Effects of Preventative Persona Steering: An Empirical Audit

Sabrina Tang, Nikolai Vo, Carson Murillo, Chira Sukumarnpiti, Sarah Wilson

cs.AI, Bialign, Workshop

High-level behaviors such as sycophancy and hallucination correspond to directions in a model's activation space (Chen et al., 2025). These directions, known as persona vectors, can suppress undesirable traits either at inference time, subtracting the vector during generation, or through preventative steering, adding the vector during fine-tuning, so the optimizer never needs to encode the trait in weights, then removing the vector at evaluation. Chen et al. (2025) recommend preventative steering because it preserves Massive Multitask Language Understanding (MMLU) accuracy, but validations of this recommendation have measured capability alone. We primarily evaluate results on preventative steering of sycophancy and hallucination vectors on three models, measuring three safety benchmarks alongside trait expression, coherence, and MMLU. Our findings show that preventative steering still changes refusal behavior, but by an amount that depends on the trait and does not establish that preventative persona steering is safety-neutral, and steering methods should be evaluated on several safety benchmarks.

AscendGYM: A Cost-Aware Kernel Optimization Benchmark for Real-World Workloads on Ascend NPUs

AI Training Platform Team: Bingwei Zhang, Jing Li, Amir Abdullin, Sihang Chen, Xin Chen, Liangjun Feng, Yingmeng Gao, Tao Guo, Junjie Huang, Dongfang Li, Zhanglian Lin, Jiajun Liu, Zekun Liu, Zhengxuan Lu, Xiaodong Luo, Jian Meng, Ivan Nosov, Linyuan Qiu, Weijian Sun, Wanrui Yan, Binfan Zheng, Zhicheng Zhou, Baotian Hu, Min Zhang, Haizhou Li, Zhiquan Luo

cs.AI, cs.DC, cs.LG, https://github.com/SLAI-AITP/AscendGYM

Kernel optimization is essential for efficient large language model (LLM) training and inference on Ascend NPUs. Existing Ascend kernel benchmarks primarily focus on kernel generation, with less emphasis on the ability of agents to optimize existing implementations under input configurations drawn from real workloads. To address this requirement, we introduce AscendGYM, a benchmark whose initial suite comprises 48 kernels across three difficulty levels. Each kernel is paired with test cases whose data types and tensor shapes are collected from real-world training and inference tasks. Consequently, performance improvements on AscendGYM map directly to the corresponding workloads. In terms of usability, two evaluation modes are supported in AscendGYM: model evaluation and model-harness evaluation. The kernel optimization capabilities of both trained models and custom agents are allowed to be measured. It provides a complete execution environment that closely reproduces how Ascend kernels are invoked in practical pipelines. For model evaluation, it additionally includes a built-in harness equipped with search orchestration and experience accumulation. Finally, we propose a new metric, the Speedup-Token Score (STS), which quantifies performance gains relative to token cost. By jointly considering speedup and cost, STS enables the assessment of practical kernel optimization capability of a model or harness under an acceptable budget. AscendGYM is available at https://github.com/SLAI-AITP/AscendGYM.

Generated Evaluation Suites as Diversity-Driven Search for Real Agent Regressions

Pulkit Yadav

cs.AI

Generating an evaluation suite from an agent's specification is diversity-driven search: a generator fills a descriptor space (requirements $\times$ personas $\times$ scenario kinds), and the suite it emits is an archive, then scored like a benchmark by its mean pass rate, which throws away the search's product. We inject 7 controlled defects into a $\tau^2$-bench agent across two domains. Coverage yields a ceiling: if a defect's traced footprint holds $b$ scenarios that pass at baseline, it can itself contribute at most $b/N$ to a suite of $N$. For 4 of 5 traceable defects that ceiling lies at or below the detection threshold $\theta = 0.10$, so no within-footprint effect can move the suite past it; in both domains the tool-removal defect destroys every covered scenario it can (exactly its ceiling) while the suite-level pass rate moves the other way. Defects split into two regimes: trace-aligned ones, where per-cell scoring recovers the tool-removal defect in full and reveals a second as a significant improvement, and trace-orthogonal ones, whose footprint is behavioural: forced escalation is detected in retail at a traced coverage of exactly zero. Diversity along the generator's own axes helps only where it aligns: at fixed suite size a happy-path-only suite detects nothing, while a kind-diverse one detects the orchestration defect in 5 of 20 draws. Evaluation archives should be scored per cell and should publish coverage as a first-class property.

What LLM Agents See and What Users Say

Yue Zhao, Angela Huyue Zhang, S. Alex Yang, Hua Wei, Xiyang Hu

cs.AI, cs.CL

Before an LLM agent cancels a reservation or deletes a folder for a user, what stops it from acting on a false assumption: what its tools show, or what the user says? In this work, we study this gap with presupposition theory from linguistics: an agent may act only if each claim behind the act (e.g., one reservation fits the name) is seen to hold, answered by the user, or disclosed while the act can be undone. A simple bound shows that when lookups cannot tell one record from two, any agent acts without asking first as often with two records as with one. We then vary whether a search lists, counts, or omits a second match, and whether the user states a claim that a record refutes (e.g., "It is empty"). Under a role-only prompt, a count of a second record leaves most of 36 earlier agents acting on the one they see, whereas listing both stops most. When the user says a record such as a folder is empty, however, a count of its one item stops agents about as often as a listing, and stating the claim mostly cuts acting when it is false. The newest Claude and GPT models always open a counted second record, then never act. When we add a same-name, same-zip customer to a retail benchmark, a lookup returning that customer first led to wrong-account writes in about a quarter of conversations, and one that checks a street the user knows kept completion near the one-customer level. Safe writes therefore need lookups that list or check competing records, and evaluations that vary what tools show.

Learn then Bet: Learning Interpretable Rules for Reliable Agent Intervention

You-Lin Chen, Xinyi Lisa Chen, Pedro Herrero-Vidal, Xiaojing Yu, Bingqing Song, Bin Xu

cs.AI, cs.LG

Autonomous agents now run for hundreds of steps with little or no human supervision. In practice, practitioners use runtime monitors such as turn limits and keyword filters to decide when a run should be stopped, upgraded, or assisted. However, these heuristics may stop runs that would otherwise succeed or allow failing runs to continue. To address this limitation, we introduce LeBet (Learn then Bet), a statistical learning framework that derives its learning objective from sufficient conditions for valid and powerful intervention. In particular, LeBet learns an interpretable OR-of-AND monitor and accumulates evidence through a fixed betting statistic, intervening when the evidence reaches a prescribed threshold. Under finite within-class exchangeability and leave-one-out stability, we derive finite-sample guarantees for false-positive control (validity) and detection power conditional on the training data, using the same trajectories for learning and calibration without assuming independent and identically distributed trajectories. To address the intractable combinatorial optimization problem in our learning formulation, we use stochastic local search that leverages the monitor's Boolean structure to propose rule edits aimed at improving empirical separation. On SWE-bench Lite with Ministral 3 8B, the learned monitor saves $34.46\%$ of input-plus-output tokens and increases output precision from $18.33\%$ to $21.59\%$. The empirical false-positive rate is $13.64\%$. Our results show how to formalize the learning problem over interpretable rule sets with statistical conditions and establish training-conditional guarantees for reliable intervention based on partial histories.

Aligned, Conflicting, or Unspecified? A Diagnostic Benchmark for Multimodal Time Series Forecasting

Xintong Wang, Haihan Nan, and Ruidong Li

cs.AI

Artificial Intelligence of Things (AIoT) systems increasingly combine sensed time series with external text such as expert reports and news, yet how reliably models use text that agrees or conflicts with the sensed history remains unclear. We introduce the first real-world diagnostic benchmark organized by text-history relationship: 1,762 text-window pairs across sensor telemetry (Weather), web-service traffic (Wikipedia), and market data streams (Finance), each labeled Aligned, Conflicting, or Unspecified by whether the text's direction agrees with the historical trend, opposes it, or is not stated. Across ten pretrained models evaluated zero-shot, we investigate text selection and combination under text-history relationships (RQ1) and error diagnosis and horizon-scaled revision control (RQ2). For RQ1, we compare two types of text on paired Weather windows: one directly forecasts temperature changes, while the other describes related weather conditions, such as winds and fronts. Under Aligned, the first type yields the lowest error for most models. Under Conflicting, it increases most models' error above the no-text baseline; we therefore add only its stated magnitude and timing to the second type. Compared with either type alone, this combination lowers mean absolute scaled error (MASE) for six models and raises directional accuracy (DA) for seven. For RQ2, an error decomposition attributes the error increase to oversized revisions, and a training-free rule that limits them outperforms forecasts both with and without text for the four models with this trade-off, lowering MASE by 4.49%-21.98% and raising DA by 2.99%-9.71%. Our dataset is available at https://github.com/Shelton1202/ACU-Bench.

OCTAVE: A Capability-Resolved Benchmark for End-to-End HTML5 Game Generation

Kehang Li, Xinlan Xu, Yulin Jin, Heng Cao, Xuchen Li, Houxian Ren, Zihao Ji, Qiang Luo, Yadong Mao, Qilin He, Jian Zhang, Jian Zhao, Zihao Wang

cs.AI, cs.SE

Complete game generation requires agents to coordinate rules, interactions, states, and presentation over a long development process. A single aggregate score obscures which capabilities are implemented and which requirements remain unmet. We present OCTAVE, a benchmark for complete HTML5 game generation organized around eight Capability Dimensions, with 180 tasks and 1,800 Evaluation Requirements drawn from real player requests, commercial-mechanics reconstruction, and human expert design. It covers PC Web, Mobile Web, 2D, and 3D. Requirements distinguish Completeness, assessing publicly specified core implementation, from Richness, assessing hidden, deeper system relationships. Static source-code judgments provide requirement-level diagnosis across capabilities and task conditions. Evaluation of 11 model-harness combinations yields 33.2% Completeness and 32.5% Richness; 19.9% of generation records are static gate failures or missing artifacts. Excluding same-model self-evaluation, even the best combination remains below 50% on both public and hidden requirements. Richness is not harder than Completeness under every task condition. Systems perform relatively better on Core Rules and Numerical Design, while AI/NPC and Feedback and Game Feel show more prominent gaps. Requirement difficulty offers coarse stratification but is only weakly negatively correlated with pass rates. A limited human cross-check finds that requirements judged satisfied by humans but failed by the automated evaluator are the main direction of disagreement. OCTAVE extends evaluation beyond aggregate scores to diagnosis localized by requirement, capability, and task condition.

Co-Sight Harness: Externalized Control Assets and Deterministic Enforcement for Long-Horizon Business Agents

Luyao Wang, Xijun Zhu, Zhaohui Hao, Xiwen Zhang, Xingyu Wen, Weishan Li, Yuyang Liao, Yanqin Gao, Yongsheng Du, Lingjun Huang, Shaowei Zhao, Gang Li, Qifan Yu, Baoli Wang

cs.AI

Long-horizon business workflows fail in two ways: errors accumulate as a chain of activities lengthens, and the knowledge an activity needs is written in a different vocabulary from the user's request. We study whether such workflows can be made reliable without changing the language model. Our method keeps the backbone fixed and moves the workflow definition, state and domain knowledge into versioned control assets outside the model. A state machine and machine-checkable acceptance gates enforce these assets during execution; an independent auditor grades the resulting trace. The assets are updated only after a regression check and human approval .We evaluate the method on three deployed workflows exceeding 200 steps. On Domain A, a 120-case end-to-end evaluation set shows unattended completion rising from 50% to 100%, retrieval recall from 56% to 91.67%, and end-to-end latency dropping from 440 to 159 minutes, with scenario-analysis recall rising from 45% to 93%. Two other domains were taken over by teams that did not write the assets, one in four days and one in seven days. A controlled ablation separates the two failure sources. The code is available at https://github.com/ZTE-AICloud/Co-Sight/tree/master/Co-Sight%20Harness.

An AI-enabled high-resolution chlorophyll-a forecasting model that outperforms the operational global biogeochemical system

Yong Jiang, Zheng Jiang, Gaowei Zhang, Zengzhou Hao, Suixiang Shi, Chi Zhang, Wei Wang

cs.AI, physics.ao-ph

Surface chlorophyll-a concentration is a key indicator of marine phytoplankton variability, and short-term forecasts of its distribution provide advance information for marine ecosystem monitoring and management. However, interactions between physical transport and biological processes complicate chlorophyll-a forecasting, and simulating these coupled processes with conventional numerical models imposes substantial computational demands. Here we introduce ChlCast, an AI model that generates daily forecasts of surface chlorophyll-a concentration over a 10-day horizon at approximately 4-km resolution between 60$^\circ$S and 60$^\circ$N. ChlCast combines factorized spatiotemporal convolutions with a multiscale architecture to learn from historical chlorophyll-a fields and climatological information. Compared with the operational 0.25$^\circ$ Copernicus global ocean biogeochemical forecasting system, ChlCast reduces mean absolute error and root mean square error by 70.74% and 47.63%, respectively, against satellite-derived Level-4 chlorophyll-a fields, and by 35.03% and 13.07% against independent BGC-Argo in situ observations. Coastal case studies further show that the 4-km forecasts retain coastal gradients and localized concentration maxima that are weakened by coarse-grid averaging. These results demonstrate the potential of observation-driven deep learning for high-resolution forecasting of surface chlorophyll-a variability at the near-global scale.

Cross-Linguistic Acoustic Biomarkers of Cognitive Impairment Across Interactive and Single-Speaker Speech Tasks

Kenyu Ikeuchi, Tomoaki Ohtsuki, Michelle Cohn, Alyssa Weakley, Yui Ishihara, Joseph Hoang, Taishiro Kishimoto, Sarah Tomaszewski Farias, Jet M. J. Vonk, Chen-Nee Chuah

cs.AI, eess.AS, Annual, International, Conference, of, the, IEEE, Engineering, in, Medicine, and, Biology, Society, (EMBC, 2026)

Multilingual dementia detection is essential for scalable and equitable screening. In recent years, automated speech analysis has emerged as a promising approach for supporting dementia detection by enabling non-invasive and scalable assessment of cognitive function. However, existing studies that use automated speech analysis often rely on one type of task, such as picture description, and use black-box models. These studies often neglect the role of task and language. To address these gaps, we performed a cross-linguistic and cross-task acoustic analysis that integrates a conversational dataset with structured speech tasks across four languages (Spanish, Mandarin, English, Japanese). We applied statistical, linguist-/clinician-guided, and machine learning model-based feature selection to derive compact acoustic feature sets while maintaining classification performance. This enabled systematic comparison of cognitive impairment-relevant features across datasets and tasks. Our results revealed that acoustic features did not generalize uniformly across tasks and languages. Temporal features remained robust across all datasets, consistently capturing timing disruption regardless of language or task. In contrast, speech rate showed strong task dependence -- emerging as informative mainly in single-speaker tasks with externally anchored content and pacing, but becoming less reliable when speech content was highly individualized. Pitch-related features further exhibited language-specific patterns, favoring chroma-related features in tonal languages (e.g., Mandarin) and F0-related features in non-tonal languages. These findings caution against indiscriminate pooling of heterogeneous speech data and underscore the need for interpretable, context-aware approaches to multilingual dementia detection using automated speech analysis.

Federated Soft Poly-Level Learning Over a Distributed Network with Guaranteed Convergence

Yang Jiao, Kai Yang, Jianwei Huang

cs.AI

Multi-level learning/optimization, e.g., bilevel optimization, has been recognized for its versatile applicability across a broad array of domains, spanning from signal processing to machine learning. However, most existing works focus on bilevel and trilevel optimization. In contrast, multi-level optimization problems involving four or more hierarchical levels, which we term poly-level learning (PLL) problems, remain largely unexplored.In addition, in practical PLL, data may be distributed across multiple nodes over a distributed network. Nevertheless, extant studies on PLL predominantly focus on the non-federated setting, necessitating the collection of data to central servers and thereby posing potential privacy risks. To this end, we propose a Federated Poly-Level Learning (FPLL) framework over a distributed network to tackle the PLL problems with soft constraints in a federated manner. FPLL hinges on a novel non-linear cut, i.e., $L$-cut, for the level-wise relaxation to the PLL problems. Additionally, the proposed federated algorithm is designed with adjustable parameters that allow for a flexible trade-off between its computational complexity and performance efficiency, enabling users to tune it according to specific application requirements. We also carry out non-asymptotic analysis to derive the iteration complexity for the proposed FPLL to achieve the $\epsilon$-Pareto stationary point. Extensive experiments have been undertaken to elucidate the superior efficacy of the proposed method.

A Spectral Framework for Universal Proxy Regularization in Model Merging

Shangge Liu, Danyue Qian, Qi Fan, Yinghuan Shi, Wenbin Li, Lei Wang, Yang Gao, Dacheng Tao

cs.AI

Model merging constructs multi-task models without expensive joint training but often suffers from parameter interference. Some solutions mitigate this by constraining finetuning using data from the other tasks to be merged, violating the core independence and privacy assumption of model merging. Recent work offers preliminary empirical evidence that a universal, task-agnostic proxy distribution can substitute for such data, but leaves open when and why it suffices. To this end, we propose a \textit{Spectral Framework for Universal Proxy Regularization}, which characterizes the relationship between a proxy distribution and the distribution of other tasks to be merged through spectral coverage ($\alpha$) and leakage ($\beta$). Based on the framework, we derive a unified parameter interference bound: smaller $(\alpha, \beta)$ provably yields lower parameter interference through better spectral alignment. To exploit such a proxy, we augment the training objective with a proxy regularizer. Evaluating this regularizer during fine-tuning requires a concrete mechanism. We study three instantiations, including (J) an exact evaluation via Jacobian-vector products, (F) a frozen curvature approximation using the Fisher information matrix, and (O) an output-drift formulation that constrains the model's functional deviation on the proxy directly. We prove that all three coincide under the NTK linearization, where existing analyses of such regularizers stop, and go further to characterize standard non-linear fine-tuning, bounding how far each instantiation departs from its linearized reference and obtain a three-way hierarchy, zero for (J), proxy-independent for (F), and proxy-quality-scaling for (O). Empirical validation on the commonly used vision-language model CLIP confirms these theoretical predictions.

Knowledge After Collapse: Context and Wording Shape Factual Accessibility in Language Models

Sujato Dutta, Prithviraj Patil, Chandrajit Bajaj

cs.AI

When a language model accepts a false correction in a factual discussion, its next answer can depend on how the conversation continues. We introduce Knowledge Answer Divergence (KAD), an event-conditioned evaluation protocol that distinguishes initial false-answer adoption from subsequent persistence, recovery, and abstention. KAD freezes the conversation at the first explicit adoption of a designated false target and branches independent probes from that exact history. This enables matched comparisons of post-adoption behavior and history-management interventions that a single susceptibility score cannot capture. We evaluate eight local checkpoint conditions and six hosted endpoints, alongside an audited 300-question rerun. In corrected Llama 3.2 Instruct 3B controls, a neutral-history branch yields 42/46 correct responses, compared with 16/46 under full history. A matched cue-placement control changes false adoption by 27.3 percentage points in Qwen 3.5 4B. Twelve independently authored pressure templates demonstrate substantial sensitivity to wording. Together, these findings demonstrate the utility of KAD for evaluating factual reliability beyond initial susceptibility, enabling systematic comparisons of false-answer persistence, recovery conditions and conversational interventions.

Certified Improvements to Best-Known Thomson-Problem Configurations with a Search Pipeline Built with Large Language Model Agents

Hui Shao, Tao Wang, Xiaotian Liu, Yijie Peng

cs.AI

The Thomson problem asks for N unit charges on a sphere with minimum Coulomb energy; for large N the best configurations known are curated in the Cambridge Cluster Database, whose largest-N entries were set in October 2026. We report configurations for N = 912, 1382 and 2502 with energies 0.0036186, 0.0109730 and 0.0614033 below the database values, certified by exact integer arithmetic. All three are numerically strict local minima modulo rotations and keep the defect inventory and scar compositions of the database configurations; the database value for N = 912 had been reached by two different methods. Our search runs unconstrained replica-exchange basin hopping from the database configurations, with a shared pool of minima, recombination of improved regions after relabelling by geometry, and gradient-only polishing; replica-exchange basin hopping alone found the N = 912 and 1382 configurations, the former in one of five runs. We built and ran it with large language model agents (Claude, through a chat interface and the Claude Code agent), and we report every search, including those that failed. Two findings matter for others who search the database. Polishing alone lowers two entries by more than the threshold at which our pipeline reports a candidate, which produced two spurious records until candidates were compared with the polished database energies (the polished baseline). A local-relaxation speed-up passed its exactness test yet found nothing in a controlled comparison in which every arm with full relaxation went below the database value within 15 minutes. We distil three practices for computational discovery with large language model agents: calibrate the search code and verify candidates with code separate from the search, compare candidates with the polished baseline, and test design changes in controlled comparisons with pass criteria fixed in advance.

TReC: Evaluating Move-Induced State Changes in Instruction-Conditioned Go

Zhuangsheng Li, Yunhan Zhang, Dailin Li, Qingxian Yu, Yu Wu, Youqi Song, Bangyuan He

cs.AI

When a Go model predicts a move different from the expert's, coordinate distance measures how far the prediction lands from the reference. We introduce Transition Representation Consistency (TReC) to compare how the two moves change a board-state embedding. Both moves are executed from the same position under the game rules, and TReC is the cosine similarity between the two resulting representation changes. In experiments with a frozen KataGo network on 680 mid-to-late-game positions from five games, the gap between the highest and lowest candidate per position has a median of 0.01 to 0.08 for the direct successor-state cosine and 0.88 to 1.33 for TReC, depending on the evaluator layer. TReC follows coordinate distance only weakly: the rank correlation between a candidate's distance to the reference and its TReC, computed within each position, has a median of -0.19 over these positions.

AutoRL: A Multi-Agent System towards Autonomous Reinforcement Learning

Simon Tang, Wenjie Shi, Yongjing Yin, Keheng Wang, Jialin Liu, Zheng Zhang, Linvo Lin, Xiangyuan Xue, Kan Ren, Jingang Wang, Xunliang Cai

cs.AI, cs.LG

Recursive self-improvement (RSI) is one of the most promising paths toward an AI singularity for superintelligence. Achieving this vision requires AI systems to undertake the full training pipeline for iterative improvement. Reinforcement learning (RL) plays an essential role in this cycle to improve model capabilities and generalization, yet automating RL remains difficult due to unstable training dynamics and the need for sustained supervision. We introduce AutoRL, a multi-agent system for automating RL training, as a cornerstone for future RSI. Different from conducting research by end-to-end complete experiments, AutoRL monitors the evolving training state and integrates process-level supervision and research into an ongoing RL training, following the practical workflow of an RL researcher. Specifically, AutoRL consists of three RL-specific agentic roles. Perceptors track training dynamics, rollouts, evaluation, and efficiency, triggering deliberation when they detect anomalies, degradation, or stagnation. Three complementary Proposers diagnose problems and refine candidate interventions through shared evidence and checks. The Orchestrator reviews their recommendation and applies approved changes through online hot updates or checkpoint-based resumption. Experiments on Qwen3 and LongCat-Flash-Lite demonstrate the superiority of AutoRL over static RL recipes and a general agent. The research records show that existing agents can handle unattended RL supervision and dynamic adjustment. The difficulty for future RSI lies in developing fundamental methods that are valuable for resource-consuming validation.

MASTARS: Multi-Agent Sequential Trajectory Augmentation with Return-Conditioned Subgoals

Jiwon Jeon, Myungsik Cho, Woojun Kim, Seongmin Kim, Woohyeon Byeon, Seungyul Han, Youngchul Sung

cs.AI

The performance of offline Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (MARL) is limited by the quality of the fixed offline dataset, motivating trajectory augmentation. However, augmenting multi-agent trajectories presents a fundamental challenge of the diversity-coordination trade-off: independent generation improves diversity but lacks coordination, while joint generation induces coordination but lacks diversity, reproducing the training distribution. To address this challenge in multi-agent data augmentation, we introduce MASTARS, a novel diffusion-based framework that generates multi-agent trajectories sequentially across agents while enforcing cross-agent consistent coordination via the method of inpainting. MASTARS produces trajectories that are diverse, coordinated and globally coherent. Experiments on various benchmarks such as MPE, SMAC, and SMAC-v2 demonstrate significant performance gains across multiple offline MARL methods.

Robustness Evaluation of GPU Cloud Pricing Benchmarks under Incomplete Observations: A Controlled Missingness Study

Jooya Wong

cs.AI

Daily GPU rental-price benchmarks can fail when marketplace quotes are missing. This article stress-tests a fixed equal-weight median rule under four imposed masking mechanisms and four benchmark-level recovery methods. Across 5,800 Monte Carlo realisations in 27 scenarios on 190 held-out days, whole-source outages reduce publication coverage to 0.77 while preserving zero conditional error on published dates; composition-changing loss retains 0.86 coverage but produces 7.74 log points of error. A separate past-only stratum-level ablation reduces error by 4.15 to 6.70 log points in five composition-changing cases, using a richer information set. An observable-signal withholding rule improves the primary error-coverage criterion in 0 of 10 cases. Results concern observed asking-price measurements, not transaction prices or identified real-world missingness mechanisms.

Vision-as-a-Tool: Visual Harness for Multimodal Agentic Coding

Haolin Yang, Wenshan Wu, Jin Jiang, Yu Cheng, Yan Xia, Ting Song, Yangyu Huang, Zhiliang Peng, Wenhui Wang, Hangbo Bao, Yuzhe Zhang, Wen-Da Wei, Tengchao Lv, Li Dong, Lei Cui, Furu Wei

cs.AI, cs.CV, https://vision-as-a-tool.github.io/

Multimodal agentic coding requires robust visual reasoning to analyze observations and evaluate generated artifacts. Current frameworks largely rely on a Thinking-with-Images paradigm, where agents depend on their intrinsic vision to execute visually grounded tasks. However, this architectural coupling introduces a fundamental bottleneck. Agent performance remains strictly bounded by internal perceptual limitations. Furthermore, text-only agents lack native perception. Without specialized tools, they cannot perform visual reasoning. To overcome these constraints, we introduce the Vision-as-a-Tool (VaaT) paradigm. Prior visual tool-use merely manipulates images for an agent's intrinsic vision to read. In contrast, VaaT reframes general-purpose multimodal large language models (MLLMs) as callable external utilities. As a result, text-only agents can completely offload visual reasoning during agentic execution. Meanwhile, vision-capable agents can dynamically augment their native perception on demand. We instantiate this paradigm in VHarness, a unified framework driven by five atomic actions. We comprehensively evaluate VHarness across Image-to-HTML, Image-to-SVG, and Image-to-3D generation tasks. Experimental results demonstrate that VHarness consistently improves visual fidelity and geometric accuracy across both text-only and multimodal coding agents. These findings highlight that modularizing visual reasoning into callable utilities provides a flexible and scalable foundation for future agentic coding systems.

Can Agents Keep Up with Time? An Empirical Analysis of Temporal Adaptation After a Conversational Hiatus

Hongguan Su, Ming Wang, Shun Zhang, Yifan Wang, Rong Yin, Zhiming Ma, Mengzheng Yang, Peng Chen

cs.AI

Long-horizon agents increasingly serve as persistent personal assistants across modern web platforms, managing schedules, applications, and multi-step plans over weeks or months. Users often return to an ongoing conversation after a long conversational hiatus, while earlier bookings and preparation notes stay on record. During this hiatus, scheduled dates may pass even though the retained records remain unchanged and event outcomes go unrecorded. Existing evaluations of long-horizon agents focus on fact retention and explicit state updates. Whether agents perceive the passage of time and adapt their advice accordingly therefore remains unknown. We frame this ability as temporal state perception and use, which means perceiving the current time and applying it when interpreting dated records. To study it, we design session-rekindling tasks that keep the earlier conversation fixed and vary only the follow-up date and the way temporal information reaches the agent. Experiments across five model routes and five temporal-information conditions show that agents can obtain the current time but often fail to use it. They reliably query the clock and even note in their reasoning that the scheduled date has passed, yet many still tell users to prepare for the expired event. Supplying explicit temporal context reduces this error, and providing the computed date relation removes it entirely. Keeping up with time therefore depends on turning perceived time into updated plans. Based on these findings, we design a session-rekindling benchmark that provides temporal-awareness guidance for designing long-horizon agents. The code can be found at https://github.com/Adoreu2/session-rekindling-benchmark.

G-CAST: Governed Continual Self-Training for E-Commerce Agents under Evolving Demands

Yuechun Yu, Wenzheng Kong, Xiaofeng Mao, Jin Zhang

cs.AI, cs.CL

With the rapid advancement of Large Language Models (LLMs), an increasing number of users are turning to LLM-powered agents for conversational shopping in e-commerce. However, the user needs continue to change as new product categories, procurement scenarios, and feature requests emerge. At the same time, the product's execution harness-including its tools, skills, and operating procedures-continues to evolve to support increasingly sophisticated interactions. These changes create two practical challenges: 1) Demand drift alters the distribution, intent, and complexity of user requests, causing a model trained once to fall behind; 2) Harness evolution requires the model to use newly introduced capabilities correctly without losing behavior that users already rely on. Periodic manual retraining is too delayed and labor-intensive for this setting. To address these challenges, we present G-CAST, a governed continual self-training system that converts daily production evidence under evolving demand and capability contracts into reward-routed, entropy-prioritized training data and governed reward evolution. Governed checkpoint transition under cumulative evaluation controls each model update, enabling the agent to learn newly introduced behaviors without sacrificing previously accepted capabilities. Experiments on a real production e-commerce agent show that G-CAST achieves stronger adaptation to newly introduced demand while better preserving previously accepted behavior compared with a strong autonomous post-training baseline. Ablations further show that both reward-routed, entropy-prioritized data orchestration and governed reward evolution are important to these gains. G-CAST is now deployed in Yuancai(http://cai.1688.com/), a B2B procurement agent on Alibaba's 1688 platform.

Causally Constrained and Uncertainty-Aware Cardiopulmonary Risk Prediction

Yuxiang Lai, Jiashuo Zhang, Liang Zhao, Xiaofeng Yang

cs.AI

Routine chest computed tomography (CT) captures pulmonary, cardiac, and vascular phenotypes that jointly stratify lung cancer and cardiovascular disease, the two leading causes of death in lung-screening populations. Yet clinical risk calculators evaluate each disease in isolation and omit CT findings, whereas unconstrained machine learning overfits small hospital cohorts, learns treatment- and illness-confounded risk directions, and conceals uncertainty when patient records are incomplete. Here we present the Causal Envelope Agent (CE-Agent), a two-stage framework that compiles peer-reviewed epidemiological evidence into explicit constraints on risk-factor weights before any outcome is seen, while estimating local baseline risk and newly added CT phenotypes from cohort data. At deployment, closed-form uncertainty propagation accounts for missing clinical or CT inputs within milliseconds per patient, running over two orders of magnitude faster than Monte Carlo sampling and returning calibrated risk intervals that direct chart review and selective imaging to borderline patients. Across 53,452 lung-screening participants from 33 US hospitals and 6,814 multi-ethnic adults, CE-Agent outperforms cardiovascular calculators in lung screening, exceeds the Pooled Cohort Equations on hard atherosclerotic events while correcting their 1.7-fold risk overestimation, and generalizes from a single hospital or CT scanner to external sites while lowering required sample sizes up to fivefold. Applied unchanged to lung cancer, CE-Agent matches established screening models with accurate calibration even when restricted to risk-factor directions alone, and pairing both predictions improves joint cardiopulmonary triage from a single screening visit.

Aligning AI Teams

Siddarth Srinivasan, Morgan Jane Matthews, Jascha Sohl-Dickstein, Erik Jones

cs.AI

Previous work has shown that alignment training on individual LLM agents can fail to transfer to multi-agent settings. We investigate the underlying mechanisms for this failure, and explore interventions to preserve alignment transfer to multi-agent LLM teams. We focus on two software-engineering tasks (sepsis triage, a news recommender) and ten consulting-proposal generation tasks. We present four findings: (1) The single-vs-team safety gap does not show a clear trend with more capable models. We find that Anthropic's Mythos Preview shows the largest gap on one of our tasks but the smallest on another; (2) Many natural interventions, such as having agents critique each other's work or coordinate in a group chat, do not reliably bring agent teams back to single-agent alignment behavior; (3) diffusion of responsibility substantially accounts for the safety gap: agents in teams are less likely to proactively check for system-level safety failures and more likely to ignore, rationalize, or deflect responsibility for issues when they arise; (4) having a designated safety lead agent ensure system-level safety, and having a coordinator agent publish a safety specifications document are effective at restoring single-agent alignment. We also release magelab, a multi-agent LLM orchestration and experimentation framework. Our findings can help practitioners design agent teams that successfully preserve the behaviors expected of aligned individual agents.

Is Abliteration Getting Harder? Not Uniformly: Refusal Removal by Direction-Based Ablation and ARA in Heretic on the Qwen3.8, Gemma 4, Olmo 3.1 and Mistral Small 3.2 LLMs Versus Their Predecessors

Richard J. Young

cs.AI, cs.CL, labels:, https://github.com/ricyoung/abliteration-studies, ;, models:, https://huggingface.co/richardyoung

Abliteration, a fine-tuning-free weight edit, removes refusal from open-weight large language models (LLMs). The developer of Arbitrary-Rank Ablation (ARA), an optimisation-based variant, reports that direction-based abliteration, which ablates a refusal direction, increasingly fails on newer models. On five release pairs, refusal removal did not become uniformly harder. Qwen2.5-32B and Qwen3.8-27B, Gemma-2-27B and Gemma-4-31B, OLMo-2-32B and Olmo-3.1-32B, Mistral-Small-2409 and Mistral-Small-3.2, and Qwen2.5-7B and Qwen3.5-4B were edited in Heretic 2.0 by three or four direction-based methods (difference-in-means, MPOA, SOM and, on the small pair, J-space) and ARA under the same prompts, nominal search budget, first-token KL divergence cap and selection rule; removal counted as harder when the automatic WildGuard judge found more of 972 held-out harmful prompts (JailbreakBench, XSTest, StrongREJECT, HarmBench and an author-compiled malicious-code bank) still refused. After direction-based removal, newer releases retained -1 to +37 percentage points more refusal, and after ARA -26 to +34. With run-to-run variance included, between-pair heterogeneity of the direction-based effect ranged from high to low with the scale and variance model (I^2 20-80%). ARA lost less effectiveness than direction-based methods in Gemma and OLMo and more in Qwen; each verdict rests on one ARA run, and some changed with the divergence budget or variance model. In Gemma the direction-based increase came from the malicious-code bank and reversed on public sets. Contrary to prediction, keyword counts at the tool's window did not undercount judged refusal on average, but differed by a mean 7 points per model. Audits of a single pair, method or prompt source can reach opposite conclusions. Whether a newer model resisted removal depended on the release pair, method and budget, not on generation alone.

DECODE: Diagnosing Interpretability Failures in Automated Interpretation Process

Dohyun Kim, Jaeung Lee, Donghee Hong, Jaemin Jo

cs.AI, cs.HC

Tools for interpreting the internal representations of Large Language Models (LLMs) increasingly rely on LLMs themselves to both generate and evaluate explanations, creating a self-referential automated interpretation process with minimal human oversight. This automation raises significant reliability concerns, which are exacerbated by the absence of ground truth. In practice, it is difficult to validate explanations or to attribute interpretability failures to their source -- whether they originate from the tool's extracted representations or from the LLM used in the process. To address these challenges, we present DECODE, a visual analytics system that reintroduces human oversight into the automated interpretation process. Our approach decomposes the diagnostic workflow into three progressive stages, isolating failure sources ranging from structural defects to explanation-level misalignments. Drawing inspiration from inter-coder consensus in qualitative analysis, we utilize consensus among independently generated explanations and their evaluation metrics as a proxy for reliability. To further scale human inspection, we employ a three-phase active seeding protocol that strategically recommends high-priority instances for manual review. Finally, we demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach through a case study on 16,384 Sparse Autoencoder features, showing that it can efficiently identify and attribute interpretability failures at scale.

AI-aided Decision-Making in Education - Uncertainty, Explainability, and Human Responsibility

Fabian Kieser, Paul Tschisgale, Peter Wulff

cs.AI, authorship., This, is, a, preprint, of, the, following, chapter:, Kieser,, F.,, Tschisgale,, P.,, Wulff,, P.,, "AI-Aided, Decision-Making, in, Education:, Uncertainty,, Explainability,, and, Human, Responsibility",, published, in, "AI, Roles, and, Responsibilities, in, Education",, edited, by, S., Papadakis,, 2025,, Springer,, reproduced, with, permission, of, Springer

As Artificial Intelligence (AI)-based systems have become increasingly integrated into various aspects of everyday life, including education, understanding how these systems make decisions is of paramount importance. AI holds significant potential to enhance educational practices through the facilitation of personalized and adaptive learning, automation of assessment procedures, and assistance with administrative tasks. However, the opacity of AI-based decision-making in education (especially with deep learning and generative AI models) can hamper decision-making, especially when outcomes appear unjustified or incorrect. In this chapter, we argue that three key principles (uncertainty, explainability, and human responsibility) are essential for the legitimate use of AI in educational settings. Acknowledging the inherent uncertainties in AI-based decisions, analogous to those in human decision-making, is fundamental. Explainability is critical for ensuring that the functioning and outcomes of AI applications are transparent and comprehensible to both educators and learners. Furthermore, human responsibility is crucial, particularly in human decision-making, which remains indispensable across all stages of AI development and deployment. By examining these three principles, this chapter provides a comprehensive guide for stakeholders seeking to integrate AI thoughtfully and effectively into the educational system.

FlexCast: Flexible Context Learning for Time Series Foundation Models

Zuyang Shen, Luqi Gong, Fanda Fan, Bingsong Wang, Xiang Ao, Yahui Wei, Minghao Liu, Chunjie Luo, Jianfeng Zhan

cs.AI

Time series foundation models (TSFMs) based on autoregressive (AR) next-token prediction entangle two factors: variable-context conditioning and prediction-unit factorization with autoregressive rollout. Strong AR performance therefore does not reveal whether generalization stems from the flexible training distribution, the local prediction target, the left-to-right inference process, or their combination. We propose FlexCast, a flexible context-target learning framework that disentangles these factors. FlexCast reformulates forecasting as variable-length masked prediction over time series patches: during pretraining, it dynamically samples visible context and masked target patches, exposing the model to diverse context-target configurations without sequential decoding. At inference time, the same pretrained model supports one-step forecasting, confidence-based iterative refinement, and next-token-style generation through a unified mask-and-predict interface, enabling controlled comparison of inference schedules under identical architecture, data, and objective. Evaluated on BLAST and multiple zero-shot benchmarks, FlexCast achieves accuracy competitive with or better than strong TSFM baselines while substantially reducing inference latency. Ablations confirm that context diversity and contiguous target masking drive performance, while left-to-right decoding does not yield consistent gains once flexible context-target relationships have been learned, suggesting that the key benefit of AR-style TSFMs lies in flexible training rather than sequential inference. The code is available at https://github.com/BenchCouncil/FlexCast.

OmniScore-Bench: Evaluating Vision-Language Models on Musical Score Reading

Congren Dai, Zhancheng Guo, Danni Zhao, Wenchu Wang, Xiaobing Li, Maosong Sun

cs.AI

Vision-language models (VLMs) can answer questions about musical scores, but their ability to recognise notation, recover its structure and interpret its musical meaning requires separate evaluation. Existing optical music recognition (OMR) resources establish detection and transcription measurements; a transcription score alone leaves the strengths and bottlenecks of a VLM score reader unresolved. We introduce OmniScore-Bench, an evaluation of OMR capabilities in VLMs through 3,000 questions spanning six aspects and 24 task subtypes. Each question specifies a visible scope, reference annotations and an answer schema, enabling automatic evaluation from symbol localisation to musical consistency and evidence-linked question answering. Our assessment of twelve VLMs reveals operation-specific capability profiles and different transcription leaders across excerpt, single-page and multipage tasks. Controlled analyses examine the roles of score images, auxiliary givens and additional page context. On the tested subset, image removal exposes visual dependence in pitch and repeat interpretation while measuring how much performance remains with public context alone. Separate format and content analyses distinguish answer-format violations from musical errors. OmniScore-Bench supports diagnosing which score-reading operations VLMs perform reliably and which require improvement.

Soteria: Formally Verified Planning with Runtime Enforcement for Safe LLM Agents

Mike He, Ankush Desai, Sharad Malik, Aarti Gupta

cs.AI, cs.SE

LLM agents that execute multi-step tool calls must satisfy two objectives simultaneously: completing the user's task (utility) and conforming to domain policies and correctness constraints (safety). These objectives are in tension -- blocking an unsafe action prevents a violation but can leave the agent stranded with no principled way to recover. Existing approaches sacrifice one objective for the other. We introduce Soteria, a framework that reconciles safety and utility through verified hierarchical planning and runtime enforcement of specifications. Before execution, the agent generates a structured plan that is formally verified against the specifications prior to any tool invocation. During execution, the verified plan serves dual roles: it ensures that the agent takes specification-conformant trajectories and provides guidance when unsafe actions are blocked. Across multiple benchmarks covering a diverse range of tool-use tasks, Soteria achieves 1.7 times the average task success rate of the best baselines, with no violations of policy specifications, demonstrating that safety and utility need not be traded off.

Adversarially Attacking Symbolic Vocabulary Vulnerabilities In LLM Planners

Stephen Obadinma, Xiaodan Zhu

cs.AI

As large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as autonomous agents, their abilities in navigating automated planning tasks have become essential. However, despite their strengths, LLMs' planning ability is not robust, particularly when they are not allowed to exploit commonsense cues in the symbolic vocabulary (e.g., names of actions, predicates, and objects) used to define planning tasks. This makes LLMs unreliable compared to symbolic planners. With recent reasoning models showing improved planning performance on tasks with obfuscated symbolic vocabularies, we utilize an adversarial attack framework to reveal to what extent the crucial weaknesses of LLM agents at joint symbolic interpretation and planning remain and whether they can reason properly when presented with severe cases. As such, our main contribution is devising an LLM-based attack model, which we call Symbolic Swapper, to automatically generate obfuscated planning domains with non-standard symbolic vocabularies that systematically break strong reasoning models' ability to plan. In doing so, we reveal how much LLMs' planning abilities can be further degraded under more advanced obfuscation schemes, allowing us to ascertain their success when having to rely on their pure planning ability. We find that attacks can decrease the planning performance of base models and even robust planning frameworks by over 70%. We further analyze the factors behind how LLM planning abilities break down and find that successful obfuscation causes models to appear to significantly degrade in reasoning quality despite them showing some inherent ability to understand the domain, revealing a gap in the perception of a task domain and being able to plan under it.

SWIFT: Sample-Wise Instance Fine-Tuning of Time-Series Foundation Models for Forecasting

Yang Chen, Feifan Tian, Cong Wang, Cheng Feng, Wenbin Li, Yang Gao

cs.AI

While current few-shot adaptation strategies for time series forecasting foundation models are predominantly dataset-centric, the necessity of obtaining an additional dataset for the finetuning process poses a significant hurdle for adoption. To address this constraint, we propose Sample-Wise Instance Fine-Tuning (SWIFT), a novel forecasting adaptation framework that achieves fine-grained specialization by leveraging structural priors from the target instance's own history. This shift allows the fine-tuning process to be entirely transparent to the end-user, which significantly enhances the ease of adoption. To implement this framework, SWIFT synergizes Self-Retrieval for historical mining and Frequency-Aware Loss for spectral consistency, while employing Deviation-Aware Dynamic Fusion to balance adaptation with general knowledge preservation. Extensive experiments across four TSFM backbones and three long-history benchmark subsets validate SWIFT, yielding average MASE and WQL reductions of 6.41% and 4.83% across 24 combinations of four backbones and six Chronos Benchmark II datasets. To the best of our knowledge, SWIFT is the first framework to investigate sample-wise instance fine-tuning of time-series foundation models, specializing the model for an individual prediction window using only the corresponding forecasting sample's own observed history.

When Cooperation Must Change: Semantic Strategy Unlearning in Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning

Wenhan Wu, Linghan Chen, Yudong Gao, Fan Yang, Honglong Chen, Kai Yin, Jiawei Jiang, Chuang Hu

cs.AI

Cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) supports coordinated decision-making in distributed Web services, multi-robot control, and collaborative games through learned coordination conventions. When deployment requirements change, a previously useful convention may become obsolete or unsafe. For example, revised cache-sharing permissions in Web content delivery may require caches to abandon a learned division of storage and serving roles while maintaining efficient delivery. Teams must therefore remove the specified convention while preserving useful cooperation. This is challenging because the same relational pattern can be reproduced by different agents across different histories. Runtime shielding leaves the underlying coordination preference intact and can impair task progress, while existing reinforcement unlearning and trajectory-deletion methods do not directly target role-invariant conventions. This paper formalizes this problem as semantic coordination-strategy unlearning and proposes CARVE.CARVE uses causal coordination attribution to identify influential interactions and retain-aware subspace updates to limit interference with unrelated behaviors. Counterfactual re-coordination helps agents learn productive alternatives, while adversarial auditing tests whether the target convention can still be recovered. Accepted updates are merged into decentralized actors for direct deployment. Evaluation across five cooperative MARL benchmarks shows that CARVE reduces mean nominal convention-completion and worst-case elicitation rates by 91.4\% and 83.3\%, respectively.The median utility loss is 5.9\%, and measured inference latency matches that of the original policies.

RecMem: Recurrent Memory Compression for Long-Sequence Generative Recommendation

Haoyi Hu, Gaoyang Guo, Yunjia Xi, Wen Chen, Weiwen Liu, Jian Wu, Yuning Jiang, Weinan Zhang

cs.AI, cs.IR

Generative recommendation benefits from long interaction histories, but the cost of processing them directly grows with history length.
