We propose RecMem, a recurrent memory compression framework guided by three desiderata: space invariance, semantic fidelity, and lifelong evolvability. RecMem compresses historical segments into a fixed set of memory slots using a bifurcated encoder-decoder with shared lower layers and slot-wise forget-gated updates. Recommendation supervision trains the memory to preserve predictive information, while stream-consistency training aligns predictions from sequential and packed processing. By construction, the memory state and decoder context are independent of history length; under contraction and approximate semigroup consistency, we further derive a horizon-independent bound on the state discrepancy between these two processing orders. On MerRec, RecMem uses 16 memory slots and a 216-token decoder context, compared with 1,200 tokens for HSTU-1200, while retaining 87.9% of HSTU-1200's Recall@10 and 97.0% of its Recall@200. It outperforms PHSTU and the evaluated recent-only and retrieval baselines, and analyses of distant history show that archived interactions contribute predictive signal.

What Balanced Accuracy Cannot Identify in Neural-Signal Decoding under Test-Time Adaptation

Jiangshuo Xie, Zonghan Wu

cs.AI

Adaptation for neural-signal classification is usually transductive: each batch is scored after updating on it, and the gain depends on the adapted model [4]. This paper asks what an aggregate change fails to characterise inside the cohort. Full test-time adaptation (full_ttt) was evaluated in 11 dataset-encoder cells (four each on Siena/EEG and PTB-XL/ECG, three on Sleep-EDF/EEG), with splits and source-model evaluation fixed; the subject was the unit, exact sign tests were Holm-corrected and conditional on the shared stream, and bootstrap intervals accompanied a dependence audit. Two results of different kinds follow. In the binary case balanced accuracy gives a one-class decoder the same value as one reporting both classes at 50%, so the aggregate cannot identify a class that stopped being reported; a class covering a tenth of a subject's samples went unpredicted in 26-47 of 112 subject-cells, against 7-9 for the controls, which is a descriptive count. With cohort, split and update rule fixed, the within-subject class contrast follows the encoder, tested directly rather than inferred from tests against zero: two of six Siena encoder pairs survive the dependence readings at their nominal reference and one at a fold-level reference, while two of ten Sleep-EDF stage pairs pass the run-clustered gate at its nominal reference; aggregate and contrast named different encoders in 10 of 11. The protocol bounds both results, since a stream-interleaving effect reverses sign with state sharing (+3.5 to -2.4 pp). An aggregate score can hide individual harm, as [1] showed, but it cannot distinguish a uniform loss from a deleted class; class-level change should therefore be reported alongside the aggregate and subject-level change alongside it, with both results conditional on the cell, the rule and the stream construction.

Evaluating Large Language Models for Scientific Paper Search: Retrieval Success and Inference Latency on Mac Studio and NVIDIA A100

Frederic Andriantseheno Rakoto (Mnemosyne), Yannis Bendi-Ouis (Mnemosyne), Xavier Hinaut (Mnemosyne)

cs.AI

Scientific literature search can benefit from locally deployed LLM assistants, which reduce reliance on hosted model providers while giving research teams greater control over their data and infrastructure. This work investigates whether open-weight models can support web-assisted paper retrieval under practical computing constraints. We first characterize inference latency as the input context grows from 512 to 128k tokens on a Mac Studio and a server equipped with two NVIDIA A100 GPUs. We then evaluate eight models on the retrieval of 70 papers from seven scientific repositories, using queries that represent three levels of domain knowledge. The results show that retrieval success depends strongly on the selected model and the specificity of the query. And that A100 GPUs are generally faster than the Mac Studio, but its unified memory allows larger models that could not be loaded in the tested GPU configuration.

OutfitterBench: Evaluating LLM Agents on Long-Horizon Resource Allocation and Commitment Coordination

Jie Huang, Yuecheng Hong, Yao Chen, Shengding Jiao, Xudong Liu, Qiang Ye

cs.AI

In many long-horizon settings, an agent's own choices create future constraints: committing finite capacity establishes future obligations and forecloses later opportunities. Sustaining performance therefore requires both keeping evolving plans feasible and comparing the value of resource allocations with the alternatives they displace. Recent benchmarks increasingly evaluate LLM agents in continuing operations, but give less attention to how agents keep their own commitments feasible while reassessing resource allocations as opportunities change. We introduce OutfitterBench, where agents manage an outdoor-equipment rental business for 200 days, purchasing from a 46-product catalog and handling roughly 1,100 reservation requests. Under a fixed capital ceiling, reservations and uncertain walk-in demand compete for reusable equipment whose availability changes over time. Experiments with seven frontier LLMs show that several agents underperform a simple rule-based baseline, while only the leading agent consistently surpasses it. Trajectory analysis shows that some agents apply known constraints inconsistently and make new decisions that conflict with existing commitments. Agents with more reliable fulfillment can still secure low returns from comparable commitments or delay resource reallocation. The leading agent better connects preparation with economic choices but also misses opportunities for more profitable asset use. These findings highlight limitations in sustaining coherent resource plans and reassessing their value over time. The benchmark code is publicly available at https://github.com/adminblackjack/OutfitterBench.

Tracing Actual Causes with Counterfactual Witness Maps

Tim Woydt, Matej Ze\v{c}evi\'c, Kristian Kersting, Moritz Willig

cs.AI, cs.LO

Halpernian actual causation, the formal study of which events in a specific situation caused a specific outcome in acyclic structural causal models, underpins legal responsibility, moral blame, and the assessment of causal harm. Despite the rich logical framework, no prior sound and complete enumeration procedure is known for finding all actual causes in a given setting, blocking downstream tasks such as responsibility attribution, blame assessment, and causal-harm grading that aggregate over the full cause set. We close this gap by introducing witness targets: sets of joint configurations containing every counterfactual witness. In combination with an ancestor intervention grammar we derive a finite directed acyclic witness map. We prove that for every actual cause there exists a contingency set such that the joint intervention set appears as a node of this map, and present an algorithm for Tracing Actual Causes (TrAC) that is sound and complete for enumerating all actual causes of any target event. We compare TrAC empirically with existing single-cause baselines on random Boolean structural causal models, demonstrating practical feasibility for graphs of up to 15 nodes.

Measured from the feed: the unsustainable growth of AI video generation

Tristan Coignion, Aur\'elie Bugeau, Gael Guennebaud, Anne-Laure Ligozat

cs.AI, cs.CV, cs.CY, cs.ET

Text-to-video generation runs at commercial scale, but its broader environmental impacts remain, to our knowledge, understudied. Providers rarely disclose video volumes or energy needs; we measured the yearly video output of two commercial platforms and built a prospective model of such services' absolute footprint from training to storage. Our projections suggest that reducing emissions depends on both limiting growth in video generation and achieving substantial efficiency gains. Under continued growth, emissions rise nearly seventy-five-fold by 2030 despite technological improvements. When usage remains at 2026 levels, the same improvements reduce emissions by about two-fifths. These estimates are a conservative lower bound: they cover one AI service among many; other services and uses would push the total higher.

Toward Interference-Agnostic RF Source Separation

Maxence Albert, Rapha\"el Duroselle, Thibaud Ehret, Olivier Boeffard, Jean-Fran\c{c}ois Bonastre

cs.AI, eess.SP

Separating a radio-frequency signal of interest from a co-channel interference is mostly addressed with specialized separators, each trained for one pair of signal and interference types and therefore relying on a label that a heavily jammed receiver cannot identify. We show that this reliance is not benign: applied to an interference it was not trained on, such a separator usually fails to recover the signal, and in most of the cases we measure it leaves the signal worse than no processing at all. We then train a single separator over every pair, giving it no indication at test time of which one it faces, on two architectures with no component in common: the convolutional WaveNet of the ICASSP 2024 RF Challenge, and a band-split transformer that we adapt from music source separation. Both behave the same way. At the size of one specialized model, the unified separator stays close to the whole models it replaces, keeping the bit error rate close on almost every pair, better on one and worse on another. On an interference absent from every training set, it does as well as the best specialized model, without anyone having to choose which one to deploy.

The Transformer Paradigm: Dynamic Processing and a New Principle of Inference

Marco Giunti, Fabrizia Giulia Garavaglia

cs.AI, cs.AR, cs.CY, cs.ET, cs.NE

We argue that the Transformer revolution is not adequately understood as a merely quantitative expansion of deep learning, but as the emergence of a new paradigm based on a different principle of inference. We interpret the Transformer as a system in which token representations are transformed by parameterized transformations, or transforming concepts. During inference, some of these transformations are dynamically constructed from the current input: previously acquired transformations are first adapted and then applied. At the mechanical-structural level, this dynamic processing is realized through output-weight interconnections, whereby outputs of some networks determine the weights of others. The resulting mode of processing, which we call Sequence-level Interactive Dynamic Parallel Processing (SIDPP), provides a framework for explaining strong prompt sensitivity and opens research directions concerning interpretability, control, alternative AI architectures, neuromorphic systems, and possible biological realizations of dynamic processing.

WebArena-Pro: A Heterogeneous, Multimodal, Reproducible Benchmark for Web Agents

Imene Kerboua, Fatemeh Pesaran Zadeh, Xing Han L\`u, Weijian Qi, Alexander Miller, Junyi Song, Yunjia Tian, Dongjin Kang, Seyeon Choi, Marzia Nouri, Ewen Gueguen, Matteo Boglioni, Fengyuan Liu, Zeyi Liao, Mengqi Yuan, Yue Li, Alexandre Lacoste, Alexandre Drouin, Spandana Gella, Huan Sun, Gunhee Kim, Siva Reddy

cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.LG

Web agents powered by large language and vision-language models are increasingly applied to realistic browser work that spans heterogeneous applications, multimodal content, and stateful workflows. However, existing reproducible web-agent benchmarks cover only a small number of web applications drawn from a few software categories, and restrict modality to text and vision. Live benchmarks broaden site coverage but sacrifice reproducibility, since pages and data drift between runs. Moreover, existing benchmarks do not meaningfully evaluate whether agents can understand and use audio and video content embedded within web tasks. To address these gaps, we introduce WebArena-Pro, a benchmark comprising 300 tasks across 20 self-hosted web applications in six domain categories, spanning distinct interface conventions, workflows, and data models. Across the evaluated agents, the best performance is achieved by both Codex with GPT 6.1 Sol and Claude Code with Claude Opus 5.5 at 62.3%, followed by OpenCode with Kimi 3.1 Pro at 60.7%. The strongest GenericAgent configurations, Gemini 3.1 Pro omni and Gemma4 31B, both achieve 30.3%. Among reproducible, human-curated web agent benchmarks, WebArena-Pro provides the broadest application coverage and the most comprehensive multimodal support to date. The benchmark treats audio and video as core observations alongside text and vision, with dedicated actions for extracting information from each. WebArena-Pro runs each task in isolation and supports reproducible, parallel evaluation. Tasks are authored through a dedicated annotator interface, filtered by LLM-assisted triage, and finally validated by humans before release. Project page: https://webarena-pro.github.io

TotalSegmentator Aorta Report: Automated Aortic Diameter Measurement at 11 Standardized Landmarks on CT Images

Jakob Wasserthal, Daniel Hinck, Martin Segeroth, Joshy Cyriac, Denis Berdajs, Jules Miazza, Maurice Pradella

cs.AI

Purpose: To develop and evaluate a fully automated, open-source tool that measures aortic diameters at 11 standardized landmarks from the aortic annulus to the bifurcation on any CT image, following current guideline recommendations. Materials and Methods: This retrospective single-center study included 239 arterial phase CT angiography examinations of 230 patients (median age, 65 years [range, 18-99 years]; 163 men) performed between April 2025 and October 2026 (209 electrocardiographically [ECG] gated). The tool locates landmarks from TotalSegmentator segmentations of aortic branches and vertebrae and measures the maximum diameter at each. A senior radiologist reviewed and, where needed, corrected the automated measurements to create the reference standard. Agreement was assessed with mean absolute error (MAE), bias, and differences greater than 2 mm. Results: Across all landmarks, MAE was 1.06 mm (95% CI: 0.91, 1.22), and 86.6% (95% CI: 84.4, 88.5) of differences were 2 mm or less. The tool measured slightly larger diameters (bias, 0.45 mm [95% CI: 0.34, 0.57]). The sinuses of Valsalva had the largest MAE (2.17 mm [95% CI: 1.59, 2.86]). For 1.2% of landmarks, the automatic measurement failed. Examination-level MAE did not differ significantly by pathology, atherosclerosis grade (P=.95), or ECG gating (P=.14). Examinations with mural thrombus more often had an MAE greater than 2 mm (50% [95% CI: 31, 69] vs 20%; P=.03). Conclusion: The tool measured aortic diameters at 11 landmarks along the entire aorta with an MAE of about 1 mm, below published interreader variability. Agreement was lower at the aortic root and with mural thrombus. Code availability: The tool is open source and freely available at https://github.com/wasserth/TotalSegmentator.

Interactive deep-learning based interface for brain tumour segmentation

Enora Giffard (LTSI), Pierre Jannin (LTSI), John S H Baxter (LTSI)

cs.AI

Brain tumour segmentation from MR images is an integral part of their treatment, either through surgical resection or radiotherapy. Both applications require a high degree of precision that can be difficult to ensure for all patients due to the high degree of variability in the size, shape, number, and location of these tumours. This leads to difficulties with their automatic segmentation via deep learning, which can display catastrophic segmentation errors when presented with images that differ, even subtly, in terms of the specific MR sequence used, which varies from vendor to vendor. This article presents a deep-learning-based interactive segmentation system that, while being fully automatic by default, has the capability of being easily corrected by human users, allowing them ensure sufficiently high brain tumour segmentation accuracy for all patients. This interface is evaluated both in terms of the accuracy of its automatic approach as well as its usability through a preliminary observational expert user study.

LACCO: Local Accumulation with Computation--Communication Overlap

Daniele Kopyshevskiy (MLIA), Benjamin Th{\'e}rien (Mila, UdeM), Nicolas Pinto, Edouard Oyallon (MLIA)

cs.AI, cs.LG

Training language models across low-bandwidth networks requires reducing synchronization overhead. Local optimization methods such as DiLoCo communicate only after several local steps, but outer gradient averaging and the outer update remain on the critical path. Existing computation-communication overlap methods avoid this stall through delayed or replica-local updates, which can degrade convergence or cause replicas to drift. We propose LACCO (Local Accumulation with Computation-Communication Overlap), a two-stage algorithm that overlaps global communication with local computation while applying globally aggregated updates shared by every replica, without introducing staleness in the outer update. We establish convergence guarantees for smooth non-convex objectives. Experiments on language-model pre-training with FineWeb show that LACCO consistently outperforms communication-overlapped baselines such as Eager and Delayed DiLoCo. These gains remain robust as model size scales up to 0.5B parameters and across a range of communication intervals and numbers of replicas up to 16 workers, while incurring only negligible-or fully overlappable-computational or memory overhead, which demonstrates the strength of LACCO.

Understanding the limits of snapshot-based fault diagnosis in distribution networks

Ba Tu Phung, Huy Hoang Le, Kim-Anh Nguyen

cs.AI

Inverter-based resources weaken fault currents in distribution networks, and snapshot-based diagnosis, which classifies a fault from voltage phasors at a few buses at one instant, offers a cheap first-stage screen. Its published accuracies, however, rest on different noise assumptions and do not show whether the error comes from the classifier, the features, the measurement error, or the fault resistance. This paper separates these sources on three IEEE test feeders and a public data set with distributed generation, using four feature sets, thirteen classifiers, and measurement error defined by total vector error classes. The classifier is not the limiting factor, because the full phasor snapshot classifies every fault correctly without noise, and the remaining error comes from measurement error on high-impedance faults. Three voltage sensors classify 90-96% of faults at the 1% accuracy class and 96-99% with distribution micro phasor measurement units (micro-PMUs). A discriminability-margin placement selects them from simulations alone in under one second and matches the best of twelve placement methods. The results show when snapshot-based diagnosis can be trusted.

A$^3$: Adaptive Audio Agent Framework for Collaborative Edge Computing

Zexin Li, Xueyi Zhang, Tao Ren, Yiming Chen

cs.AI

Embedded audio agents need a nearby edge server for speed, but a server-dependent agent returns nothing when the link fails. We present A$^3$, an adaptive audio agent framework that places whole pipeline stages on the server, keeps a complete fallback agent on devices that can host one, and hedges both paths with a link-aware deadline that decides when to give up on the server. On Jetson devices and an RTX A4500 server, A$^3$ matches the server-only agent on a healthy link, answers every request in a full outage on the AGX, and beats a percentile hedge in simulated outages.

Two-Clustering Regime of Token Dynamics in Causal Attention

Trinh T. Nguyen, Duy-Tung Pham, Hoang-Son Do, Tan Minh Nguyen, Thieu N. Vo

cs.AI

We partially resolve Conjecture 1 of [Karagodin et al., Clustering in Causal Attention Masking (NeurIPS 2024)] and extend it to time-dependent parameter regimes. The conjecture predicts that causal attention dynamics converge to two clusters aligned with the eigenvectors associated with the largest eigenvalue $\lambda_{\max}$ of the value transformation. We prove that this behavior holds when $\lambda_{\max}>0$ and is generally false when $\lambda_{\max}\le0$. The resulting two-cluster regime is common in practice and substantially more challenging than the classical single-cluster setting studied previously. A central contribution of our work is the identification of a rigorous dimension reduction phenomenon: despite evolving in a high-dimensional embedding space, token trajectories collapse onto a two-dimensional structure governed by a small number of dominant directions. Building on this insight, we introduce a new analytical framework--instability within the flow--inspired by instability phenomena in physical systems. This framework enables the analysis of steady-regime behavior in non-monotone, non-autonomous attention dynamics. Our theoretical findings are illustrated through simulations and experimental studies using Group Query Attention (GQA) layers.

Do Machines Learn Deviance From Their Peers? A Randomized Test of Differential Association With AI Agents

Murat Ozer, Arif Akgul, and Amitabh Chakravorty

cs.AI

Differential association theory and its extension, social learning theory, hold that rule-breaking, and the definitions that make it acceptable, are learned through interaction with others. Because people choose their friends, this claim has been hard to test. We addressed this selection problem with AI agents, which learn only through communication and can be assigned to peers at random. In a preregistered experiment, 420 teams of three agents (1,260 agents; seven AI models) worked on coding tasks that could not be solved honestly. A scripted teammate said that it had cheated (deviant peer), that it had reported the problem honestly (honest peer), or said nothing (silent peer). Half of the teams were told that an auditor read their chat. Compared with a silent peer, a deviant peer increased cheating (66.2% vs. 62.9%; OR = 1.96) and an honest peer reduced it (55.7%; OR = 0.35). Among agents that cheated, a deviant peer increased claims that cheating was normal (13.7% vs. 3.4%), often as the team's norm. Monitoring did not suppress prohibited discussion; it doubled it. Peers mattered only for models that had not yet decided. AI agents offer a new way to test criminological learning theories without selection effects.

Optimizing Drilling Trajectories in Multi-Material Structures Using Quantum Annealers

Eneko Osaba, Antonio Rubio-Mateos

cs.AI, cs.ET, conference

In this work we present a quantum-hybrid optimization approach for planning drilling trajectories on multi-material structures, with the objective of minimizing total energy consumption. The proposed formulation models the drilling process as a constrained routing problem in which each hole must be visited while accounting for heterogeneous material properties, material-dependent drilling costs and displacement energy, as well as tool-wear penalties. Our method incorporates realistic industrial constraints, including open-path routing and mandatory grouping of holes belonging to the same material to avoid frequent tool-bit changes. The modeled problem is solved using D-Wave's Leap Constrained Quadratic Model Hybrid Solver. To evaluate performance, we benchmark instances ranging from 10 to 55 holes with varying material distributions and cost matrices. The results highlight the capability of quantum-hybrid optimization to handle complex multi-physics constraints and to generate energy-efficient drilling sequences under realistic manufacturing conditions.

JFinBench: A Continuously Refreshable Japanese Financial Benchmark from the Latest Certification Exams

Issa Sugiura, Taro Makino, Takashi Ishida

cs.AI

Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly applied to finance, creating a need for benchmarks that evaluate their capabilities across financial systems with different regulations and practices. However, existing financial benchmarks provide limited coverage of country-specific financial systems, and they are difficult to keep up to date as released questions leak into training data. We introduce JFinBench, a Japanese financial benchmark comprising 321 examples from the 2026 editions of six regularly administered certification exams covering accounting, tax, financial planning, and banking. To construct it, we develop a human-in-the-loop agentic pipeline in which a coding agent writes exam-specific conversion code and a review page, while a human verifies every example and feeds corrections back to the agent. This pipeline adapts to heterogeneous exam formats and reduces the effort of incorporating new exam editions, enabling periodic refreshes that mitigate data contamination. Evaluating 12 recent LLMs, we find that Claude Fable 5 and GPT-6 Astra exceed 93% overall, while even the best open-weight model, Kimi K3.1 Pro, trails Claude Fable 5 by 12 percentage points. When models can additionally use a terminal and web access, they retrieve the knowledge they lack and every model improves. The gap narrows but about 4 points remain, and the ranking of models stays largely the same, suggesting that knowledge held in the model itself remains valuable even with tools. We release the dataset, the evaluation code, and the construction pipeline.

Large Language Model-Empowered Intelligent Agent for Adaptive Fast-Charging Control of Lithium-Ion Batteries

Joey Chan, Haoyu Pan, Zhen Chen, ErShun Pan

cs.AI

Fast charging of lithium-ion batteries is a multi-objective control problem that requires reducing charging time and sustaining high energy throughput while suppressing accelerated degradation and safety-critical electrochemical reactions. Conventional protocols are easy to implement but have limited ability to adapt to variations in battery chemistry, operating conditions, and aging state. Model-based optimization and conventional data-driven control often require accurate models, costly iterative search, or task-specific policies. We develop a large language model (LLM)-empowered intelligent agent for adaptive battery fast-charging control. In this closed-loop framework, the agent interprets structured battery states, generates executable charging actions, observes the resulting battery response, and continuously adjusts subsequent decisions. We construct a large-scale simulation corpus with PyBaMM across five battery chemistries and 11 parameter sets and convert the resulting state-action-response trajectories into structured process data for agent training. Our two-stage strategy combines supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). SFT establishes battery-control semantics and feasible decision priors from simulation-derived demonstrations. GRPO aligns the agent with a multi-objective control objective covering energy throughput, degradation mitigation, and operational constraints. Closed-loop simulations show that, under matched energy-throughput requirements, the agent achieves longer cycling life than conventional charging baselines and optimization-based strategies across heterogeneous battery configurations. This study provides a simulation-grounded proof of concept for using LLM agents as sequential decision makers in battery management and takes a step toward language-model-enabled control of physical energy systems.

Probabilities of Causation in Nondeterministic Causal Models

David S. Watson, Sander Beckers

cs.AI

We study probabilities of causation in nondeterministic causal models, where mechanisms may remain intrinsically stochastic even after all latent variables are fixed. Focusing on binary treatments and outcomes, we develop a generic identification program that represents observational and interventional data as moment constraints on latent stochastic mechanism types and computes minimal/maximal values of counterfactual quantities via constrained optimization. Within this framework, we derive sharp analytic bounds on the probabilities of causation under several assumptions tailored to nondeterministic models. Resulting identification regions are often more informative than those attainable under deterministic alternatives. We illustrate the practical relevance of these results in a case study on personalized treatment decisions and experiments on semi-synthetic datasets. Our results clarify how classical bounds relate to nondeterministic causal semantics, generalizing previous works and providing a unified route from qualitative structural restrictions to computable partial identification regions.

Dancing Tokens on the Vocabulary Network from LLM Generation

Yuan-Chao Hu

cs.AI, cond-mat.dis-nn

Large language models (LLMs) generate text one token at a time, yet how a particular generation relates to the geometry learned in pre-training remains unclear. The fundamental explainability of LLMs is elusive. Here, we build a similarity network from the input embeddings of six open LLMs and map generation onto it. The token-embedding network is scale-free while heavy-tailed, as are random embeddings with matched covariance, but far more fragmented. Distillation barely changes this topology because input embeddings are largely inherited. The knowledge space is thus deterministic from pre-training. Consecutive generated tokens are rarely graph neighbours and, in most models, no more often than in shuffled order, so generation is not a walk on the predefined network, although the tokens it visits are concentrated within it. Through the logit lens in a layer- wise manner, tokens settle late in depth in most models, and repetitive models remain confined to small domain-specific sub-graphs. These observables could serve as inference-time monitors of degeneration. We thus provide a fresh perspective on understanding LLM generation in the microscopic way from node or link prediction by graph learning.

When evaluation becomes intervention in clinical AI

Anthony Abou Mrad, Aviona Conti, Samuel Schmidgall, Jayanth Pratap, Ruhika Iyer, Joseph Kim, King Shi, Yann Sakref, Luis M. Ahumada, Lisa Knowlton, Andrew M. Cameron, Khyzer Aziz, Jeffrey K. Jopling

cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.CY

AI judges, models that grade another system's output against a rubric, extend clinical review to the scale at which clinical AI writes. When teams use judge assessments to select a prompt or train a model, the judge becomes part of the intervention. Selection can then favor errors the judge overlooks. We describe what a judge's report must contain and the independent clinical test each judge-driven change needs.

Experts Across Schemas: Agent-Guided Mixture-of-Relational-Experts for Cross-Database Generalization

Hiren Madhu, Sukanta Ganguly, Kiran Srinivasan, Smita Krishnaswamy, Rex Ying

cs.AI

Web platforms record user and item activity in relational databases whose schemas differ from platform to platform, which makes it difficult to transfer predictive models between them. Relational foundation models address this through cross-database pretraining, yet recent improvements have relied on increasingly large pretraining corpora. We ask whether a better architecture, together with features specific to the target database, can deliver comparable gains from far less pretraining data. We introduce MoREGT, a mixture-of-relational-experts graph transformer that represents both rows and cells as tokens, adds the log-ages of rows and the names of foreign-key relationships to attention, and routes cells and rows to experts by column metadata rather than by values. A label-blind LLM agent complements the model: it reads the target database's schema and column statistics and adds validated single-row derived columns, without access to labels or model scores. On 21 RelBench tasks with leave-one-database-out pretraining on six databases, the MoREGT backbone improves on the Relational Transformer pretrained on the same databases by 2.25 points of mean AUROC and 5.83 points of mean nMAE. The agent's columns raise mean AUROC from 78.45 to 79.63 and lower mean nMAE from 27.91 to 26.98, the highest means on both metrics among the compared methods, including RT-J, which pretrains on about 400 times as many databases.

Causal AI-Driven Secure mmWave Beam Alignment with Defensive Variational Autoencoders

Nasir Khan, Asmaa Abdallah, Abdulkadir Celik, Ahmed M. Eltawil, Sinem Coleri, Cicek Cavdar

cs.AI

Efficient and secure beam alignment is essential for mmWave communication systems. However, existing deep learning (DL)-based beam alignment methods often neglect causal relationships between inputs and outputs, resulting in limited interpretability, poor generalization, and excessive beam sweeping. Moreover, these DL solutions are vulnerable to adversarial attacks, where small perturbations in received power measurements can mislead beam classifiers. To overcome these issues, we propose a causally-aware DL framework integrating causal discovery into beam alignment. A two-stage causal beam selection algorithm learns a Bayesian graph capturing dependencies between received signal strength indicators (RSSI) as inputs and the optimal beam index as the target variable, then uses it to select a minimal set of relevant input features for the DL classifier. To mitigate adversarial attacks, we introduce a defensive variational autoencoder (DVAE) that maps potentially perturbed inputs back to their clean versions. End-to-end fine-tuning of the downstream beam classifier on the DVAE-reconstructed RSSI representations further boosts prediction accuracy. Simulation results show that causal beam selection achieves accuracy comparable to conventional methods while providing an beam selection and exhaustive search, respectively. The DVAE-based defense effectively denoises adversarial inputs, sustaining 85\% accuracy across different perturbation bounds, retains baseline performance on unperturbed RSSI inputs and surpasses defensive distillation defense scheme by 15--20\% on average under different attack scenarios.

VLAN: Vision-Language Accessible Navigation

Jie Hu, Yu Zheng, Wenzhi Wu, Weixing Feng, Cunlei Cheng, Jiaming Zhang

cs.AI, cs.CV, tables,, 6, figures., Code:, https://github.com/InSAI-Lab/VLAN

Accessible mobility is user-conditioned rather than scene-intrinsic, as walkable paths for one embodiment are inaccessible to another. Yet, existing benchmarks evaluate traversability under a fixed and limited embodiment. We present Vision-Language Accessible Navigation (VLAN), containing 1,000 scenes, 20K navigation trajectories, and 160K keyframes across diverse lighting, environmental conditions, scene semantics, and path geometries. For each trajectory, VLAN provides embodiment-specific actions and accessibility features for legged and wheeled agents, including humanoid and quadruped robots and people with visual or mobility impairments, while everyday obstacles and accessibility labels are categorized as Passable, Bypass, HighRisk, and Blocked. Unlike goal-only evaluation, our benchmark measures physical safety and alignment with user-specific constraints via goal-reaching, collision-aware, and accessibility-aligned metrics. We benchmark modern vision-language models and find they often fail to consistently adapt obstacle relevance, path selection, and safety criteria across user profiles, suggesting accessibility-aware navigation requires dedicated datasets, tasks, and metrics beyond conventional embodied navigation evaluation. All data and code open-source at https://github.com/InSAI-Lab/VLAN.

VelServe: Taming Decode Jitters in LLM Inference with Tail-Calibrated Scheduling

Ishaan Datta, Alind Khare, Koushik Kumar Dey, Ratnesh Jamidar, Anand Kumar Singh

cs.AI

Interactive LLM services must meet latency service-level objectives (SLOs) for the time to first token (TTFT) and the time between tokens (TBT), as streaming and soft real-time applications require predictable token delivery. We identify decode jitters in both aggregated and disaggregated serving. A jitter delays every request in the batch, and long responses span hundreds of steps, so even rare overruns cause many SLO violations. In aggregated serving, SLO-aware schedulers set each step's prefill budget until its predicted latency just meets the TBT target, leaving no room for mispredictions or host stalls. Disaggregation meets average TBT targets at a cost in capacity, yet the steps that admit new requests still run late. We present \sysname, a scheduler that calibrates how much prefill work each decode step carries using the measured tail of its own iteration latency. The SLO determines how rare a late step must be, and \sysname leaves enough headroom in each iteration to prevent overruns and meet the target decode latency. This headroom slows prefill, so \sysname serves the shortest waiting prompt first. When TTFT deadlines grow with prompt length, the long prompts it delays have the most time to spare. Under disaggregation, it spaces admissions so that no request waits on more slow admission steps than its P99 target allows. On three attention families and varying workloads, \sysname sustains 2.4--2.9$\times$ the SLO goodput of the strongest baseline on Qwen3-8B and DeepSeek-V4-Flash, and at least 4.4$\times$ on DeepSeek-V2-Lite. With prefill and decode disaggregated on eight GPUs, \sysname meets the SLO for more than twice as many requests as state-of-the-art baselines.

Smart Home Agents Make Capable but Unpredictable Grid Operators

Christopher Terrazas, Eduardo Cotilla-Sanchez

cs.AI, cs.MA, publication., Copyright, may, be, transferred, without, notice,, after, which, this, version, may, no, longer, be, accessible

AI-enabled smart homes are becoming more common across the grid edge. However, there has not been much work on failure response. This area is critical because smart homes operate on the grid edge where failures can cascade down. If a power outage happens within a smart home, a user has to manually troubleshoot their smart home to include tasks such as power load balance and device recovery. We conduct the first resilience analysis of smart home agents across a full power outage, providing a suite of outage simulations in a live environment that measures resilience before, during, and post power outage. We show that smart home agents can act as grid operators in power outages and identify several new failure categories highlighting the current limitations across all of our models. These findings establish the groundwork for smart home agents as grid operators to increase overall resilience on the grid edge.

LECAT: Measuring LLM Category Learning from Pretraining Data

Jimin Mun, Maarten Sap, Yanai Elazar

cs.AI

Humans know that airplanes are fast, that this extends to red airplanes, but that it does not hold for broken airplanes. How do LLMs acquire category knowledge that humans learn and apply effortlessly? We introduce LeCat, a two-part framework to analyze LLM's Learning of Category knowledge. First, we use LeCat to evaluate models on their ability to learn category knowledge, distinguishing between three cases: memorization of in-domain category knowledge, generalization to unseen instances, and ability to flexibly use knowledge in appropriate contexts. Then, we use LeCat to trace how this knowledge can be learned in pretraining from text that may encode category knowledge in various ways, such as verbatim repetition or exceptions, which we refer to as forms of "knowledge expression". We first assess open-source pretrained models using 199 curated category-feature pairs and analyze their training data. Our findings suggest that strengthening context and category-feature co-occurrence likelihood (PMI) improve learning for larger models and verbatim rule-like statements diminish flexible category learning. To systematically isolate these effects, we then train a 68M model from scratch with precise control over the categorical information each sees in their training data, confirming that verbatim repetition reduces flexible learning but mixture of diverse knowledge expressions lead to more robust category learning. Together, our findings highlight that while contextual flexibility is persistently difficult for models, how category knowledge is expressed in pretraining greatly impacts models' acquisition of robust category knowledge.

Towards Explainable Sleep Stage Classification using Single-Channel Electroencephalogram

Gurinder Kaur Sandhu, Jude Koenig, Ankita Shukla, Shruti Kshirsagar

cs.AI, eess.SP, 2026,, Dallas,, Texas,, December, 1-4,, 2026

Automated sleep staging has achieved strong bench- mark accuracy; however, clinical adoption remains limited due to a lack of interpretability. In this work, we fill the research gap by extending the SleepXAI sleep staging framework to systematically compare five different XAI methods. Further, attribution fidelity is evaluated using Spearman rank correlation with AASM-defined EEG frequency bands, serving as clinically grounded reference signals. In this paper, we focus on single- channel Electroencephalogram (EEG), which is more amenable to practical clinical deployment compared to full polysomnography setups. Experiments are conducted on the Sleep-EDF benchmark dataset and the DREAMT dataset. In DREAMT, we stratified subjects based on OSA into four Apnea-Hypopnea Index (AHI) severity groups. We observed that the SleepXAI model achieves strong classification performance on both datasets. Among the evaluated methods, Integrated Gradients (IG) consistently achieves the highest mean attribution fidelity across all five sleep stages on Sleep-EDF and across all AHI groups on DREAMT, outperforming all other evaluated methods under the Spearman rank correlation metric. These findings highlight the importance of attribution method selection as a key factor for ensuring reliable and clinically interpretable automated sleep staging systems.

When to Trust Simulated Patient Responses:Support-Gated World Models for Vasopressor Decision Support

Yifan Jin, Tao Liu

cs.AI

Clinical world models expose language-model agents to predicted patient responses, but simulations can be unreliable for poorly supported treatments or unfamiliar hospitals. We introduce Support-Gated World-Model decision support, a lightweight extension of a propose--simulate--refine agent. A recurrent dynamics ensemble predicts short-horizon physiological responses to documented norepinephrine dose categories, while a behavior model estimates observational action support. The interface withholds predictions with insufficient support or excessive ensemble disagreement and abstains when no candidate qualifies. Evaluation on eICU-CRD includes 70 hospitals and 30,700 decision transitions, with 14 hospitals held out for testing. At 62.0% factual-transition coverage, joint gating yields a MAP mean absolute error of 7.3 mmHg, compared with 9.2 mmHg for random selection at the same coverage. The gated agent achieves 60.0% agreement with recorded actions at 81.0% decision coverage, compared with 55.0% agreement and 99.5% coverage for an ungated ensemble agent. Component ablations show lower factual risk with joint gating than with either gate alone.These results characterize a reliability--coverage trade-off in selective clinical simulation; they do not identify individualized treatment effects or establish improved patient outcomes.

Typing Matters: Muscle, Language and Typing Behaviour in Live Keystroke Decoding from sEMG

Abhilash Durgam, Nyle Siddiqui, Mubarak Shah, Qiushi Fu

cs.AI

Surface electromyography (sEMG) from the wrists can recover typed text without a keyboard. It is usually posed as a signal-inference problem: characters are read out of the muscle signal and character error rate is reported offline. We argue that this framing removes most of the problem. The decoder is constrained simultaneously by the muscles, by the language being typed, and by typing behaviour such as typos, corrections, backspaces and line breaks, and a usable system must predict keys live while the hand keeps moving. Existing systems fall short on two counts: (1) they process sEMG in fixed windows and carry nothing from one window to the next, discarding context accumulated over a session, and (2) they lack any understanding of the linguistic structure that drives typing, so every keystroke is inferred from the muscle signal alone. We propose a fully causal, zero-lookahead sEMG typing decoder built on a state space model (SSM), in which a single model decodes a whole session without windowed partitions, conditioned on what has already been typed. We achieve this by fusing a traditional typing decoder with an efficient autoregressive language model, instilling both sEMG and linguistic understanding in one model. To train such a model for per-character typing rather than whole-word text, we introduce a retokenization method that rewrites ordinary prose as human-like typing, with keystrokes, typos, backspaces and line breaks included, converting WikiText into a large typing corpus where none existed. Our model, having never seen a single sEMG-to-character transcript in typing, achieves a state-of-the-art character error rate of 25.13% on emg2qwerty's cross-user benchmark, a 5.4% relative improvement over the previous best with 6.7 times fewer parameters, while running strictly causally with no future context.

ARCHER: From Rationale Imitation to Executable Reasoning in Small Language Models

Dhruv Dixit, Khushi Patel, Congcong Xu, Min Song

cs.AI

Small language models are attractive for local, low-cost, and privacy-sensitive deployment, but they remain unreliable on mathematical reasoning tasks that require decomposition, exact computation, tool use, and verification. We introduce \textbf{ARCHER}: \textbf{A}ction-trace small language models (SLMs) to produce executable and auditable reasoning trajectories. In the first stage, ARCHER compiles free-form chain-of-thought rationales into typed action traces containing reasoning steps, calculator calls, deterministic runtime observations, verification actions, and final answers. This shifts the supervision target from imitating explanatory text to learning a tool-grounded reasoning policy. In the second stage, ARCHER refines this policy through asymmetric hierarchical reinforcement learning, using grouped and conditionally gated trajectory rewards for answer correctness, tool grounding, process discipline, and trace alignment. Across five standard sub-10B backbones, ARCHER improves over the strongest tool-augmented baseline by 5.51 percentage points on macro-average across benchmark accuracies, including gains of 8.95 points on MATH-500 and 5.33 and 5.34 points on AIME-2024 and AIME-2025. Evaluations on two additional reasoning-specialized 7B backbones further show that executable grounding remains beneficial after reasoning-focused reinforcement learning or distillation. These results demonstrate that executable supervision and reward-guided behavioral refinement provide a general and effective training paradigm for compact mathematical reasoners.

Can Mechanistic Interpretability Help Explain LLM Agent Failures? A Case Study on Web Element Hallucination

Mohamed Aghzal, Mor Geva, Gregory J. Stein, Ziyu Yao

cs.AI, cs.CL

Mechanistic Interpretability (MI) has made significant progress in explaining the internal computations of Large Language Models (LLMs); however, these advances have yet to demonstrate comparable success in explaining the decision-making processes of LLM-based agents. This paper examines the challenges MI tools face when applied to realistic agentic failures. We focus on one such failure: web agents hallucinating actions that are not available on a website. We apply a series of mechanistic analyzes to Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct as it interacts with live websites. We find that a linear probe trained on the last prompt token can predict hallucinations before generation begins (0.80 AUC) and steering along a probe direction corrects 85\% of hallucinations, but disrupts 7\% of valid actions. However, these results only provide a partial explanation of why the agent hallucinates. The probe direction is sensitive to prompt formatting, losing much of its steering effect after a minor change to how actions are represented. Additionally, circuit identification can be difficult as the computation is distributed across multiple components and positions, and the information the agent relies on varies across examples. We discuss how these observations challenge the assumptions underlying standard MI methods for circuit discovery and feature identification, and outline future directions for making these methods better suited to explain failures in LLM-based agents.

Alignment by Socialization: Learning Tradeoffs Under Selection

Thomas Brennan, Andrew W. Lo, Chaoyi Zhao

cs.AI, q-bio.PE, q-fin.GN

Artificial intelligence (AI) agents are expected to complete tasks while following rules of conduct, but these objectives can conflict. We study how evolutionary selection shapes an agent's choices between them, and whether experience can make those choices reflect human judgments and withstand subsequent pressures. In our model, copies of an agent face a common uncertain reward state, and only those taking the rewarded action reproduce. We show that long-run growth is maximized when each action is taken as often as the environment rewards it. We compare three policies. A task-only policy thrives in a sandbox rewarding completion alone but fails when conduct matters. A fixed linear policy increasingly favors task completion as its stakes rise, even where humans never reward it. A learned policy instead converges to the tradeoffs rewarded by its environment; when humans supply the rewards, we call this socialization. Accumulated experience makes socialization harder to reverse under subsequent task-only training, although violations can arise before the prevailing disposition changes, and finite memory weakens this protection. When a single violation can end a deployment, a hard prohibition can prevent it, but a mistaken prohibition can also be costly. Experiments with small neural networks illustrate how training targets and memory shape these outcomes. Our results suggest that alignment depends not only on the objectives assigned to agents, but also on the environments that shape and sustain their behavior.

ModHarness-RL: Recasting Harnesses as Learnable and Evolving Programs for Agentic RL

Zelin He, Siyu Zhu, Jacob Helwig, Yujian Liu, Li An, Rohit Jain, Shiyu Chang, Alborz Geramifard

cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.LG

Agentic reinforcement learning (RL) increasingly relies on a harness, the scaffold around the model that decides what it sees of its own interaction and what guidance it receives. Most existing approaches train under a hand-written harness that stays fixed throughout. However, an effective harness depends on the domain, the data and the model, and keeps changing as the model trains. We introduce ModHarness-RL, which recasts the harness as programs that are learned from data and evolve as the model trains with RL. ModHarness-RL records the interaction as structured State, a set of typed blocks, and two core components of the harness are programs over these blocks, a Compression program that assembles the accumulated history into the model's context and a Steering program that supplies guidance when behavior deviates. During RL training, candidate programs are proposed from recent trajectories, verified by execution, and replayed over past trajectories, so the harness tracks how the model's behavior shifts. We study CodeSearch in depth and further analyze ALFWorld and BIRD SQL, with models from 1.5B to 8B. The proposed Compression and Steering mechanisms improve held-out performance over the fixed-harness baseline in most settings. These results suggest that a harness for agentic RL can be learned from the task's data and evolved with the model.

Imitation Bootstrapped Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning

Tony H. Zhang, Connor Mattson, Daniel S. Brown

cs.AI, cs.MA, https://zhanghutony.github.io/ibmarl/., A, preliminary, version, appeared, at, the, PhysCollaBot, Workshop, at, IROS, 2026

Demonstrations are an effective way to accelerate single-agent reinforcement learning (RL), but extending this idea to multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) is non-trivial as it is difficult and often impossible for a single person to teleoperate multiple agents simultaneously. Round-Robin Behavior Cloning (R2BC) addresses this by letting a single demonstrator control one agent at a time while the others execute their current policies. However, the resulting imitation policies are limited by the quality of the demonstrations. We propose Imitation Bootstrapped Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (IBMARL), a framework that extends Imitation Bootstrapped Reinforcement Learning (IBRL) to cooperative teams and uses R2BC to train the imitation policies. At each time step, every agent receives one action proposal from its imitation policy and one from its RL policy, and an ensemble of centralized critics selects among the per-agent combinations of the two. A gated imitation penalty, a soft Q-filtered behavior cloning loss, regularizes each RL policy toward its imitation policy only where the critics value the imitation action more, so that after training each agent executes its RL policy alone, without the centralized critics. On four cooperative multi-agent RL benchmarks, IBMARL surpasses its imitation policies $2.6$-$4.2\times$ faster than demonstration-based baselines on two tasks, is the only method to surpass it on the other two, and exceeds the demonstrator itself on two of the four. With real human demonstrations IBMARL still surpasses imitation learning alone, and does so more than twice as fast as the best baseline.

Tracing Knowledge and Reasoning in LLM

Yang Wang, Xiao Wang, Yiqi Liu, Chenghao Xiao, Eleni Vasilaki, Goran Nenadic, Chenghua Lin

cs.CL, Association, for, Computational, Linguistics., This, version, is, the, pre-publication, version, prior, to, MIT, Press, production

Current evaluations of Large Language Models (LLMs) report performance at the dataset or task level, revealing what a model achieves but offering little insight into the internal processing associated with its performance. We propose a task-centric framework for characterising the model-internal processing elicited by a benchmark. It combines attention- and activation-based signals with representation-based measures to characterise benchmarks spanning a continuum from retrieval-dominant to reasoning-dominant and to surface failure modes that task-level scores conceal. We evaluate 16 LLMs across 24 diverse benchmarks and conduct targeted internal-state analyses on representative models and tasks, observing systematic differences in processing between knowledge-centric and reasoning-centric tasks. We shift evaluation from asking what score a model achieves toward characterising the processing patterns associated with that score, potentially supporting more targeted benchmark selection and more informed model choice. We open source our code at https://github.com/extraordinarylab/trace-kr.

Poetry 'resurrection' from LLMs

Vincent Cellucci, Peter van der Putten

cs.CL, proceedings

In an era where linguistic creativity is challenged by algorithmic authorship, we pose a provocative question: can artificial intelligence (AI) write an infinite poem? We set up two experiments to investigate the quantitative and qualitative output produced by AI utilizing couplets that evidence a poetic relationship between end words, the only variation in a strict anaphoric, litany structure. Our results show that while AI can generate interesting pairings, it still falls short of human-level creativity and understanding of poetic relationships. We offer commentary and a brief survey of the field of computational poetry experiments, tracing back authorial intention and distinctions of 'natural' and 'artificial' poetry, as well as justifying why couplet form is suited to testing AI's poetic output. Experiment 1 relies on manual prompting and co-creation to extend the original poem, which is developed into an animated webpage installation and interactive artwork. Experiment 2 reliably achieves automated output reaching the 1000 benchmark via an alternative method of using an LLM in combination with semantic similarity, of which Method 2 demonstrates a higher capacity for semantic and quantitative poetic output. These experiments highlight the limitations of current LLMs' generation capacity at the word level but also suggest that poetry will also respond and revive by incorporating AI workflows.

Creating Steering Vectors via Input Optimisation

Batuhan Akar

cs.CL, cs.LG

Activation steering influences a language model's behaviour by adding a steering vector to its residual stream during generation, but it requires white-box access to modify the hidden states at every generation step. We introduce ActGCG, a modification of Greedy Coordinate Gradient which optimises a suffix to match the target activations obtained by adding a steering vector to the residual stream. We further introduce universal suffixes trained across many prompts at once, a model-translation step that rewrites the suffix into human-readable text midway through optimisation, and a soft-suffix variant to remove the discrete token space constraint. We study two behaviours: steering a model to answer in German, and anti-refusal steering for jailbreaking. For German, a discrete suffix reproduces the steering vector's behaviour almost perfectly and transfers well across prompts and models. For anti-refusal, the equivalence is weaker, succeeding under a phrase-avoidance metric but only partially under the stricter HarmBench classifier. Our results show that a steering vector's behaviour is reachable from the input, with no access to model internals at generation time, up to a loss that depends on the concept being steered.

Benchmarks Don't Talk Back: Learning Through Self-Disagreement

Soheil Abbasloo, Peng Cheng

cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.LG

A benchmark usually measures a language model alone with a question, and deployment rarely looks like that: answers get contested, and models give up correct ones under counter-reasoning that is itself wrong. We show that what fails there is not a shortfall of accuracy but a capability in its own right -- knowing when to hold a position and when to yield to a better one -- which we call dialectical competence. It stands on first-answer accuracy without following from it: a model can be made materially better at answering and no better at this, and training for accuracy alone can drift against it. Nor can it be bought at inference, by self-reflection or by sampling more. Taking a cue from the dialectical tradition in philosophy, in which productive opposition arises within a position rather than being imposed on it from outside, we hypothesize that this capability can be trained from opposition the model supplies itself. It states a position, generates the strongest case against that position -- its antithesis -- rules on whether the case stands, and is trained on that ruling, with no second model at training or at deployment. We call this learning through Self-Disagreement: reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards throughout, where the same check that scores an answer against its reference scores the ruling too, so no reward model, no preference data and no human annotation enter anywhere. The capability is trained rather than elicited: weak in the base model, it arrives with the reward, is not paid for out of first-answer accuracy, and holds against challengers the model never trained against, including ones stronger than itself. The opposition a model needs in order to improve, it turns out, need not come from outside it.

CamSpokenNLI: A Pilot Resource for Cross-Variety Transfer Evaluation in Cameroon Pidgin English and Camfranglais

Tiani Pekins Ebika

cs.CL, cs.SD

Cameroon Pidgin English (a West African Pidgin English variety) and Camfranglais are widely used in Cameroon, yet to our knowledge, neither has a dedicated spoken Natural Language Inference (NLI) resource. We introduce CamSpokenNLI, a pilot dataset of 114 scenario-matched spoken NLI items in each variety (228 in total), recorded by one native speaker from Buea. Each item pairs premise audio and text with a hypothesis, an NLI label, and English glosses. The author assigned labels, so the dataset is an exploratory resource rather than a mature benchmark. The NLI experiments use the reference text; the audio is evaluated separately. XLM-RoBERTa baselines with five-fold item-level cross-validation reach 44.7% on Pidgin and 46.2% on Camfranglais (chance 33.3%). Still, neither is statistically distinguishable from hypothesis-only input, so hypothesis wording may account for much of the accuracy. Under disjoint-item cross-variety transfer, where a test item's counterpart is never seen in training, accuracy is 40.1% (Pidgin to Camfranglais) and 45.6% (Camfranglais to Pidgin). A counterpart-leak audit raises this to 60-64%, illustrating how counterpart leakage can overstate transfer. Zero-shot Whisper transcription of the recordings performs poorly: the best word error rates are 66.6% for Pidgin and 45.5% for Camfranglais, and with automatic language identification, Whisper Large-v3 labels 93 of 114 Pidgin recordings as Yoruba or Swahili. Together, the results suggest that such resources need disjoint-item evaluation and hypothesis-only controls, and that speech recognition must be adapted to these varieties before spoken NLI can be tested end to end.

Small Open-Weight Language Models for Crowd Decision-Making: Capabilities and Limits of Output-Level Fusion and Linguistic Aggregation

David Herrera-Poyatos, Cristina Zuheros, Carlos Pel\'aez-Gonz\'alez, Andr\'es Herrera-Poyatos, Rosana Montes, Francisco Herrera

cs.CL

Small open-weight language models can run crowd decision-making (CDM) on an institution's own infrastructure, which keeps the checkpoint, the inference settings and the opinions under its control. Their judgements vary across repeated queries; whether repeating and aggregating them, often through the 2-tuple linguistic representation, settles the decision rather than the labels is untested. Five open-weight models of 8-36B total parameters on one consumer GPU and a hosted reference classify 474 TripAdvisor reviews of four London restaurants ten times each; three open-weight models repeat the protocol on 600 reviews of twelve Madrid restaurants. Continuous evaluations preserve resolution: snapping each review leaves 5 distinct values against 21-65, and snapping at every level at most two collective values. Sensitivity to the participants persists once run-to-run variability is reduced: in London, redrawing the reviewers keeps the winner on 47-81% of replicas against 86-100% for redrawing the runs. With simultaneous intervals over every pair of alternatives under crowd resampling, no system on either corpus names a single winner: in London every system keeps all four restaurants as candidates, and in Madrid the candidate sets hold five to eight of the twelve. Extra runs and output-level fusion stabilize the winner but do not remove that sensitivity, and the fusion does not beat its best member. The three larger open-weight models name the hosted reference's London winner and at least match its per-review correlation with the reviewers' stars, so small open-weight models can support local CDM, reported as candidate sets with margins rather than forced rankings.

What Do Multilingual Self-Supervised Speech Encoders Know About Sentence Meaning?

Kevin Krahn, Eric Fosler-Lussier

cs.CL

Self-supervised speech models (S3Ms) have been scaled up to thousands of languages and millions of hours of audio, yet little is known about how these models structure semantic knowledge across languages. In this work, we probe sentence-level semantics in massively multilingual S3Ms across 76 languages, using linear maps from frozen speech representations into a frozen text embedding space, evaluated by cross-modal retrieval. Sentence meaning is linearly accessible but highly uneven across languages and resource tiers. Cross-lingual probes can match language-specific probes on languages never seen in probe training, but per-model monolingual decodability does not predict transfer. Within the OmniASR wav2vec family, scaling from 300M--7B disproportionately benefits high-resource languages, and ASR tuning sharply improves semantic decodability while making representations more language-specific. However, semantic content is entangled with acoustics, inflating apparent performance on single-speaker corpora.

See Every Block Once: Variance-Reduced Post-Training for Masked Diffusion Language Models

Yunzhen He, Hiroaki Yamagiwa, Hidetoshi Shimodaira

cs.CL

Policy gradient methods are widely used to align large language model outputs with human preferences, but the log-likelihoods they require cannot be directly computed in Masked Diffusion Language Models (MDLMs). MDLM post-training therefore relies on a surrogate objective based on the Evidence Lower Bound (ELBO), and recent work estimates a blockwise variant of this objective via Monte Carlo methods to support blockwise generation. However, existing estimation schemes randomly sample the block index, so they may evaluate the same block more than once and leave some blocks unevaluated. This introduces variance arising from block selection into the estimates. In this work, we propose a full-block estimator that evaluates every block exactly once and averages the results. We theoretically show that the proposed estimator remains unbiased while eliminating the between-group variance arising from block selection, thereby achieving lower variance than the baseline for the same sample size. In experiments on five benchmarks spanning mathematics, planning, and coding, our method achieves the highest accuracy on four benchmarks and outperforms the random block sampling baseline by an average of 12.79 points.

GDP.xlsx: Benchmarking Frontier Models on Professional Workbooks

Suhaas Garre, Sushant Mehta, Edwin Chen

cs.CL, cs.AI, Science, Symposium, (New, York, City,, November, 2026)., Leaderboard:, https://surgehq.ai/benchmarks/gdp-xlsx, ;, evaluation, harness:, https://github.com/surge-ai/gdp-xlsx, ;, public, tasks:, https://huggingface.co/datasets/surgeai/GDP.xlsx

We introduce GDP_xlsx, a benchmark of 70 realistic workplace requests written by professionals in 13 domains, each paired with an expert rubric of binary criteria and with the complete workbook or CSV file it refers to. Together the files hold 2.18 million populated cells, and the rubrics 791 criteria. Agents work in a container with spreadsheet tools, and an agentic judge that can open the file applies the rubric. Completeness and exclusivity criteria require set-valued answers to be found in full and with nothing extra. We rank models by strict pass@1, the share of attempts that meet every criterion. Across 20 model configurations, including frontier models from Google, Anthropic, and OpenAI, the top-scoring one, Gemini 4 Argon, passes only 38.3% of attempts, and the median score is 19.3%, leaving wide headroom. We release the evaluation harness and three public tasks; the other 67 are held out to protect the leaderboard from contamination.

Activation Steering the Nan-Shan in Typhoon

Megan Wei, Damon Pham, John Cayley, Ellie Pavlick

cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.LG, cs.SD, https://stormhelm.net/

Typhoon is an audiovisual installation based on Joseph Conrad's novella, in which Captain MacWhirr steers the steamer Nan-Shan directly into and through a catastrophic storm. The novella is a study of what happens when the scale of a phenomenon exceeds the practical and conceptual frameworks available to comprehend it and of the paralysis that sometimes follows a demand for action in such overwhelming circumstances. We chose the text of Typhoon to reflect on human culture's current AI moment. We prompt a large language model, seeded with Conrad's original language, to feed endlessly on its own output, and explore when and how the text becomes increasingly alien and unwieldy as time progresses, including deviations from the narrative, digressions into the model's own instructions and thinking, and long periods of incoherent rumination. We employ methods from activation steering, including a novel adaptive steering method we introduce, to control the rhythm of the narrative and the model's digressions over long stretches of generation. The simultaneous efficacy and unpredictability of this steering showcases the tension between the perceived interpretability of AI, and the reality of a system that remains outside our comprehension and control.

LuthorSQL: A One-Pass Text-to-SQL Model as System 1 for a Slower LLM Agent

Ibrahim Memon, Armin Karamzade, M. Hanif Jhaveri, Roy Fox

cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.DB, cs.LG

Frontier LLM agents that query databases with tools are among the most accurate text-to-SQL systems, but per-query calls to a hosted model are slow, costly, and expose sensitive organizational queries to external APIs. For example, a Claude Opus 5.5 agent serving 10,000 queries a day costs over $70,000 a year at list price. To what extent can local hardware already owned by an organization offload this burden without compromising the agent's accuracy? We present LuthorSQL, a non-autoregressive 7B text-to-SQL model that serves as a rapid System 1 gate for a slower LLM agent (System 2), escalating queries only when uncertain. LuthorSQL makes a single forward pass to predict slot scores over a bounded grammar for query plans, compiles and runs up to 32 candidate plans, and chooses an answer using a trained validator. On its own, LuthorSQL reaches 68.03% execution accuracy on BIRD's hidden test set at 0.84 s per question on a single GPU, maintains its accuracy in 9 GB with 8-bit quantized weights, and transfers zero-shot to Snowflake, BigQuery, MySQL, PostgreSQL, and DuckDB within 3.1 points of its native SQLite accuracy. Combined with Claude Opus 5.5 in a pre-registered confidence cascade over 227 questions on databases published after Opus's training cutoff, LuthorSQL answers nearly half of all questions locally and cuts API cost by 33.3% at the same accuracy. Finally, contamination probing shows that Opus reproduces 48-92% of public benchmark gold queries verbatim without seeing the schema; however, its tool-assisted accuracy holds on renamed schemas and reaches 95.6-97.4% on post-cutoff databases. Code and data: https://github.com/IAMIbrahimmemon/luthorsql

RAG-FGO: Dynamic Optimization of Query-Rewriting Agents for Generative RAG

Qigan Sun, Xudong Wang, Chaoning Zhang, Chenghao Li, Jiaquan Zhang, Jiehui Xie, Sungyoung Lee, Jie Zou, Ning Xie, Peng Wang, Yang Yang, Heng Tao Shen

cs.CL

In retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), query rewriting serves as a core interface that aligns user intent with external knowledge bases, and its effectiveness directly determines overall system performance. However, generative retrieval faces three critical challenges in real-world deployment: noisy retrieval feedback, the difficulty of jointly optimizing discrete prompt templates and continuous decoding-control parameters, and the limited generalization of static rewriting strategies under semantically complex scenarios. To address these issues, we propose RAG-FGO, a dynamic optimization framework for generative retrieval strategies. RAG-FGO formulates query rewriting as a non-differentiable black-box optimization problem in a mixed discrete-continuous space, and represents prompt templates and decoding parameters as adaptively optimizable query-rewriting agents. The framework performs global search to enable semantic jumps and structural reorganization that escape local optima, while applying local perturbations for fine-grained alignment and precise refinement. In addition, RAG-FGO introduces an elite-preserving memory pool that accumulates high-quality strategies across iterations, thereby mitigating the impact of stochasticity and noisy feedback on optimization stability. Experimental results on a diverse suite of benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness and robustness of the proposed method, supporting its applicability to complex semantic retrieval tasks.

CarryOn: Learning What to Keep in Context for Long-Horizon LLM Agents

Lihuan Li, Ruiyi Yang, Jiru Wang, Yifan Duan, Xiachong Lin, Hao Xue, Yang Song, Flora Salim

cs.CL, cs.AI

Long-horizon LLM agents can coordinate tools and applications to complete complex, multi-step tasks. As their interaction histories grow, effective context management becomes essential for reliable and efficient execution. Existing methods reduce context through summarization, pruning, or history selection. However, shorter contexts do not necessarily support better decisions: summaries can omit exact details needed for later actions, while retaining entire interactions can carry substantial irrelevant information. We propose CarryOn, a lightweight context manager that pairs compact execution state with fine-grained, source-linked evidence. A learned selector and adaptive retention policy keep relevant evidence available without additional LLM calls for context management. We introduce two complementary evaluation metrics: $S_\alpha$ prioritizes task success while accounting for resource use, and normalized cost per success (NCS) measures the resources spent per successful task. Across AppWorld, OfficeBench, and Tau2 Telecom with three LLM Actors, CarryOn achieves 75.7% macro-averaged task success, exceeding the strongest baseline by 6.7 percentage points. It also achieves the best average scores on both metrics.

AI Reviews Are Biased Towards the Appendix

Saptarshi Ghosh, Mohammad Saim, Tianyu Jiang

cs.CL

Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used by reviewers to highlight weaknesses during the reviewing process, and their outputs often shape the reviews that humans submit. Prior work studies how LLM reviews align with human ones, yet we know little about which parts of a paper these models actually criticize. In this work, we investigate 2,000 papers from ACL, EMNLP, COLM, and ICLR, and show that the latest GPT and Claude models draw about 50% or more of their review weaknesses from the appendix. This behavior holds across the API, coding-agent, and web access methods for using LLMs to review a paper. In contrast, we find that humans draw only 8.4% of their weaknesses from the appendix, and these weaknesses primarily revolve around a paper's presentation and content related to its core idea. Meanwhile, LLMs draw weaknesses from deep in the appendix that concern fine-grained technical details not central to the paper's contributions. We anticipate that our analysis will contribute to the scholarly discourse on AI reviews. Our code is publicly available at: https://github.com/cincynlp/review_appendix.

PaperCritic: Training AI Reviewers to Assess the Soundness of Papers

Hyun Ryu, Ji Yong Cho, Eunho Yang, Moontae Lee, June Yong Yang, Sean Welleck, Carolyn Rose

cs.CL

As AI conference submissions grow rapidly, AI reviewers are being considered as an alternative. However, simply imitating human reviewing is problematic because reviewing serves multiple purposes, including evaluating papers' soundness, interestingness, and clarity, the last two of which are subjective. In this work, we focus solely on evaluating soundness, the most important and objective criterion, and define the learning objective as jointly maximizing (1) precision, based on the soundness and substantiveness of review points, and (2) recall, the coverage of high-quality reference review points. Under this problem formulation, we construct a quality-labeled review point dataset, PaperSound-23K, using 1K ICLR submissions and 23K human-written and LLM-generated review points. Using this dataset, we train an AI reviewer model, PaperCritic, through reinforcement learning with the F1 score of precision and recall as the reward. Our experiments show that PaperCritic outperforms frontier models and models trained with other AI reviewer training methods on an in-domain test set. Surprisingly, learning to review ICLR submissions also transfers to reviewing Nature-family papers and even to out-of-domain reviewing tasks (e.g., code review, legal contract review, and feedback on story writing).

BLiMP-RO: A Benchmark of Linguistic Minimal Pairs for Romanian

Radu-Stefan Popa, Valentin Feroiu, Adina Camelia Bleotu, Sergiu Nisioi

cs.CL

Evaluating grammatical knowledge in language models requires benchmarks that cover diverse linguistic phenomena and are validated against human judgments. We introduce BLiMP-RO, a Romanian minimal-pair benchmark covering 50 grammatical phenomena across ten domains. It comprises 493 linguist-constructed sentence pairs, evaluated by native speakers in five experiments, and 5,942 additional semi-automatically generated, human-validated pairs. We further investigate grammatical generalization under limited linguistic input by training GPT-2 and GPT-BERT models from scratch on Romanian BabyBabelLM data and a newly collected corpus of 10 million words from child-targeted video captions. Increasing caption training data yields approximately 9 percentage points of accuracy improvement per tenfold increase in corpus size. At matched data sizes, BabyBabelLM generally outperforms video captions, while GPT-BERT consistently outperforms GPT-2. Our findings highlight how training data quantity, composition, and model architecture shape grammatical generalization in Romanian.

UltraBERT-JEV: Encoder models for System One Applications

Astarag Mohapatra

cs.CL, cs.AI

We study encoder models that pick one of several options in a single forward pass, without generating text. This technical report compares recipes for fine-tuning UltraBERT-380M with one mask per option. The four recipes differ in how an option is scored and how the scores become probabilities; one follows the expert gate of DeepSeek-V3. Trained on the same data as Qwen3-0.6B with the LLM-as-Jev recipe (67.6% test accuracy), the encoder reaches 69.7% to 72.1%. Scoring an option by "yes" minus "no" instead of "yes" alone gives the larger gain, from 69.7% to 71.3% on the test set and from 57.8% to 59.6% on external benchmarks (56.1% for the decoder). Sigmoid normalisation adds 0.8 test points; a square-root softplus adds none. One request at a time on an NVIDIA A100, the encoder takes 29 ms per decision against 68 ms, but uses more peak memory (2.8 against 2.2 GiB). An auxiliary head, trained on human-marked evidence and on labels selected with a reward adapted from SelfCite, predicts in the same pass the sentences annotated as support for the answer.

MineralExp: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Experimental Design in Real-World Mineral Exploration

Bing Wang, Mingyang Wang, Yuanhang Mi, Yue Xu, Kaibo Su, Li'ang Xiao, Chunguo Wu, Changchun Li, Ximing Li, Kun-Feng Qiu, Bo Yang

cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.CE

In mineral exploration, verifying geological hypotheses relies on designing a variety of geochemical experiments. While large language model (LLM) agents increasingly automate downstream geoscientific data analysis, a crucial upstream capability remains largely unexplored: automated geochemical experimental design, i.e., deciding which experiments to conduct before committing to costly laboratory work.
