Evaluating this capability of LLM agents is difficult for two reasons. First, geochemical experiments exhibit high methodological diversity and subjectivity: to test the same hypothesis, different geologists may choose different minerals and instruments, so no single protocol can serve as the reference answer. Second, open-ended experimental plans resist representation as structured labels, which makes objective scoring difficult. To address these challenges, we introduce MineralExp, a benchmark that reformulates geochemical experimental design as a hypothesis-based experiment selection problem, i.e., a multi-label classification problem. We abstract concrete instrument names and procedural details into a shared taxonomy of 19 experimental types across five mineral-system processes: Source, Transport, Deposition, Modification, and Preservation. This taxonomy yields deterministic multi-label targets while accommodating diverse experimental protocols. Constructed from published ore-deposit literature and verified by domain experts, MineralExp comprises 277 cases and 1,572 reference experimental plans. Given a geological hypothesis and initial context, an LLM agent selects the relevant tasks and generates concrete experimental plans, which are scored by multi-label classification metrics and an LLM-as-a-Judge metric. The experimental results show that current agents generally achieve high recall but low precision, tending to propose more experiments than are necessary to test the hypothesis.

Can You Trust Your Finance Agent? Drift, Diagnosis and Auditable Intervention on One Shipped Financial Research Agent

Cosimo Galeone, Anna Ettorre, Ginevra Mariani, Minsu Park, Giuseppe Ettorre, Daniele Ligorio

cs.CL, cs.AI, 2026,, Milan

Can you trust a finance agent you did not build? We take the reference agent shipped with the Vals Finance Agent Benchmark v2, leave it untuned, and ask four questions a leaderboard score does not answer. Is the score stable? Seven identical runs on 18 questions pass between 105 and 123 of 149 rubric criteria, and the bill varies by up to 17%. When the agent fails, who owns the fix? We classify 20 failed trajectories, from three runs of nine held-out questions, by the layer whose owner would remedy them, under a taxonomy frozen in advance: 13 belong to the agent's instructions, 3 to its tools, 4 to the model, none to its data. Can that diagnosis be trusted? Four blind readers from three model families, given only the traces, name the same fault as our LLM analyzer in 94% of cases, the rate at which they agree with one another (93%). Can a change be trusted? An optimization loop reads the traces, makes one targeted change per round and keeps it only if it clears a fixed threshold. Under a cost objective its final configuration costs 0.34x the baseline on the held-out questions (95% CI [0.22, 0.58]) at accuracy within noise. Under an accuracy objective it gains 14 points on the development questions and 1.5 on the held-out ones (CI [-5.3, +7.2]). Judged against the measured run-to-run noise, under a rule written before the test was opened, the first is a real saving and the second is not. This is a case study of one agent and nine test questions, not a general result.

In the Eye of the Beholder: Human Label Variation in Textual and Visual Entailment

Monica Riedler, Jasmin Orth, Raffaella Bernardi, Barbara Plank

cs.CL

Human Label Variation (HLV) is increasingly recognised as an informative signal that reflects meaningful differences in how people interpret the same input. While HLV has been studied extensively in Natural Language Inference (NLI), much less is known about HLV in Visual Entailment (VE; Xie et al., 2019), where the textual premise is replaced by an image, keeping the hypothesis and label space fixed. We introduce VariNLI-VE, a dataset for studying how HLV changes between paired NLI and VE instances. We re-annotate a subset of originally neutral SNLI (Bowman et al., 2015) instances and their visual counterparts, with the same annotators evaluating both the textual and visual versions. Each annotation includes a label, confidence score, and explanation, yielding 2,250 annotations. Labels and explanations are collected jointly, providing ecologically valid explanations. Because the image may reveal information not stated in the text, one might expect disagreement to decrease in VE. Instead, we find no clear overall reduction: disagreement decreases for some instances while it increases for others. Explanations reveal further variation, as annotators can retain the same label while changing their rationale. When evaluating vision-language models (VLMs) on the paired data, we find lower accuracy, weaker alignment with human label distributions, and generally higher uncertainty in VE than in NLI.

Toward Universal Translatability: On the Statistical Ontology of Generative AI

Katia Schwerzmann

cs.CL

This paper argues that the problem of translation functions both as a point of origin and as a normative horizon for contemporary generative AI. In current discourse around ''world models,'' their attainment is framed as the achievement of internal consistency, cross-modal alignment, and physical grounding. Taken together, these demands converge on an ideal of the model as a universal translation machine capable of establishing lossless correspondences across linguistic, visual, and physical representation spaces. I show that this ideal presupposes a metaphysical commitment that I call a statistical ontology: the belief that reality has an underlying, computable statistical structure that can be recovered through sufficient data and modeling, rather than being shaped and accessed through situated human interpretation and labor. Tracing this commitment back to Warren Weaver's 1949 Memorandum on machine translation, I outline how translation was historically recast as code deciphering and, later, as the alignment of probability distributions across structured outputs. Finally, I contrast this paradigm with approaches in Translation Studies that foreground opacity, power asymmetries, and the translator's positionality. I argue that generative AI's statistical ontology is pre-Kantian, pre-critical and obscures the infrastructures and human labor that make machine translatability---as alignment---possible in the first place.

FEND: Far-End Detection in Outbound Voice AI Calls

Abhishek Kumar, Suraj Agrawal, Aashraya Sachdeva

cs.CL, eess.AS

When a conversational Voice AI agent places an outbound call, the far end may be a live person, a voicemail greeting, an interactive voice response (IVR) system, or increasingly another AI system answering on the callee's behalf. Standard Answering Machine Detection (AMD) reports only whether a human or a machine picked up, a design meant for human call centres, where one machine is as uninteresting as another. That single bit does not tell a Voice AI agent what to do, since leaving a voicemail message, navigating an IVR and holding a short exchange with another AI are three different actions and a two-valued label cannot distinguish them. In 1,000 randomly sampled calls from our own outbound deployment, a live person answered 48.1%, a voicemail system 44.6%, an AI 6.9% and an IVR 0.4%. Our proposed framework, FEND, reads the far end's audio and its streaming transcript and runs one detector for each automated class (voicemail, IVR, AI callee), with human the default when nothing fires, returning a single label within 20 seconds. On a public benchmark the voicemail components reach 100% accuracy without ever misclassifying a live human, where Asterisk's AMD misclassifies 30 to 66% of them. For the AI callee, acoustic features do not transfer across recording conditions, but speech timing does, separating AI from human at 0.952 ROC-AUC, because synthesised speech leaves far shorter gaps between words. On production traffic, separating a live person from any automated far end reaches F1 0.979, and only 2.9% of live people are given an automated label, the error that matters most for an agent acting on the result.

Agent-as-a-Judge

Runyang You, Hongru Cai, Caiqi Zhang, Qiancheng Xu, Meng Liu, Tiezheng Yu, Yongqi Li, Wenjie Li

cs.CL, https://github.com/ModalityDance/Awesome-Agent-as-a-Judge

Artificial intelligence has progressed from task-specific systems toward general models spanning perception, generation, reasoning, and autonomous interaction, making reliable evaluation essential for comparison, deployment, and post-training feedback. At scale, modern evaluation pipelines increasingly rely on LLM-as-a-Judge to produce scores, preferences, and critiques; in its fixed-context setting, the judge relies on the supplied evaluand and evaluation materials. Yet a reliable verdict may require evidence beyond the supplied evaluand and context---either because critical environmental states remain unrecorded in the static execution log, or because controversial factual claims require external verification. Passive judges cannot obtain that missing evidence before deciding, especially when evaluating agent trajectories, executable artifacts, or domain-specific reports. Recent work therefore develops active evaluators that seek missing evidence and update their grounds for judgment before deciding. We bring these advances together through Agent-as-a-Judge, focusing on how evaluators construct and control checking processes within the broader LLM-based judging landscape. We define the scope through evaluation-directed checks and the use of their results, covering both structured workflows and observation-driven control. We organize the design space by multi-agent collaboration, tool integration, memory, and planning, and show how these mechanisms supply the checks and evidence on which a verdict rests. We consolidate published scores and reproduce missing entries for key agentic judges; the comparison reveals uneven agentic gains and motivates dedicated agentic evaluation. We conclude by characterizing application demands and open challenges in cost, reliability, safety, and governance that condition trusted deployment of agentic judges at scale.

VEPO: Mitigating Multilingual Hallucinations in LVLMs via Visual Evidence-guided Preference Optimization

Chengpeng Fu, Xiaocheng Feng, Wenshuai Huo, Yichong Huang, Baohang Li, Duyu Tang, Ting Liu

cs.CL

Large vision-language models (LVLMs) achieve strong multimodal performance, yet they remain prone to object hallucination---generating answers that are linguistically fluent but visually ungrounded. This issue is especially severe in low-resource languages, where weaker cross-modal alignment causes models to rely more on language priors than on image evidence. Existing remedies either require costly multilingual annotations or external translation/reward models, or intervene at inference time in ways that can distort the output language. We propose VEPO(Visual Evidence-guided Preference Optimization), a training-time method that transfers reliable visual grounding from English to other languages without external models. VEPO first localizes key visual evidence using English queries via mid-layer cross-modal attention, then constructs preference pairs that share the same correct answer but differ in evidence sufficiency (full image vs.\ key-region masked image). Direct preference optimization on these pairs encourages low-resource inputs to prefer answers supported by complete visual evidence. Experiments on multilingual POPE and MME with Chinese, French, and Hindi show consistent hallucination reduction and perception gains, with the largest improvements on the lowest-resource language. Analyses further confirm that VEPO sharpens question-relevant attention and that evidence-guided masking outperforms random masking.

Contagious Fingerprints: When LLMs Inherit Another Model's Identity

Zailong Tian

cs.CL

The fingerprint by which a classifier recognizes the LLM behind a text is usually treated as a fixed property of the model. We show that it is contagious: a model that continues a conversation begun by another model inherits that model's fingerprint. This undermines audits of API relays, which can serve the first turns of a conversation with the model a customer pays for and silently hand the rest to a cheaper one. After such a handoff, detection of the substitute by a text classifier drops from 0.97 to 0.72 (DeepSeek replacing Claude Sonnet) and from 0.87 to 0.54 for a same-vendor downgrade (Haiku), and retraining on handoff data repairs only substitutes seen in training. Preregistered experiments trace the contagion to imitation of the history's style. History also moves a model away from its own fingerprint: active probes are not camouflaged, but when calibrated on fresh conversations they flag same history} and tests the audited reply by its rank among them. ------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Folding Language Models into Themselves

Ziyang Zhang, Yubin Jing, Yuyao Li, Haofan Wang, Yichen Gong

cs.CL, https://github.com/myboker/concertina

Knowledge distillation usually trains a compact student from scratch, which needs a large amount of data even though the teacher's weights already encode most of what the student has to learn. In this work, we explore building the student directly from the teacher's weights. We propose Concertina, which folds every group of N adjacent teacher layers into one shared block that is executed N times. Each loop step carries a low-rank correction initialized from the layer it replaces, and a per-token gate mixes these corrections, to let every token recover the behavior of the original layers from a single shared block. We further design a distillation data recipe that balances general, mathematical, academic and targeted multiple-choice data, to recover all capabilities of the teacher together rather than one at the expense of another. On Qwen3-4B, a 2.27B Concertina model distilled on 22B tokens retains about 90% of the teacher on commonsense and mathematical reasoning and 84% on average across eight benchmarks. It outperforms same-size dense and layer-sharing baselines, as well as Qwen3-1.7B, which is trained on over a thousand times more data. These results suggest that a compact model need not be retrained at scale, since folding the teacher into itself and distilling it on the right data recovers most of its capabilities. Code is available at https://github.com/myboker/concertina.

A Multi-Layered AI Pipeline for BPMN Generation from Natural Language

Alexandru Rancea, Ionut Anghel, Tudor Cioara, Ovidiu Anchidin, Ancuta Nemes

cs.CL

The automated transformation of unstructured natural language process descriptions into Business Process Model and Notation (BPMN) diagrams remains a challenging task, due to linguistic ambiguity and the difficulty to detect non-adjacent control-flow relationships. This paper proposes a solution for generating BPMN models from natural language descriptions through a multi-layered pipeline that integrates transformer-based architectures, Large Language Models and ontology population. The proposed method starts with natural language descriptions and separates textual processing, control-flow relation extraction, and structural validation into dedicated components. Entity extraction is performed using a fine-tuned RoBERTa-based transformer model while a control-flow engine captures non-adjacent relationships through LLM-based inference. To improve generalization under limited available data, the approach uses a dynamic few-shot strategy with con- trolled template input construction, enabling improved generalization under limited data availability. Additionally, an ontology population mechanism is introduced to represent the generated process models as semantic knowledge structures. For evaluation purposes we have used from the literature and industry reference processes. Additionally, we have validated the proposed method on a real-world heart disease patients transitional care process, capturing practical constraints and multi-actor collaboration, and serving as an expert-defined benchmark scenario. Experimental results demonstrate strong performance in the relation extraction task, achieving an accuracy of 90.7%, as well as maintaining structural alignment between the generated and reference BPMN models across multiple evaluation scenarios scenarios.

NER-D: A cross-domain benchmark for German NER

Marc Brinner, Philipp Cimiano, Karolina Hanna Czok, Regina Stodden, Viju Sudhi, Sina Zarrie{\ss}

cs.CL

We present NER-D, a cross-domain benchmark for German NER comprising 13 datasets spanning 9 domains, including General/Politics, Wikipedia, Legal, Medical, Historical, Financial, Agriculture and Archaeology. We examine the performance of both proprietary LLMs and state-of-the-art small open-source models using normal prompting and further examine the performance gains achievable via in-context learning and LoRA fine-tuning. Our results show that without any sort of optimization a proprietary model (GPT-5.4 mini) outperforms the smaller open-source models on 6 out of 13 datasets, but LoRA fine-tuning enables the smaller models to substantially outperform the proprietary baseline. We further analyze the influence of the domain and the tagset used in each task on the performance achieved in the different evaluation settings. Our benchmark will be available as a resource for the development of future German NER methods on both general and specialized domains.\footnote{We make our code, data, and models publicly available upon acceptance of this paper.

MamaReason: Unfinished Reasoning and Answer Recovery in Clinical Language Models

Thanni Adewuyi, Adepoju Jeremiah Moyondafoluwa, Dorcas Amapu, Dieko Whenu, Sylvia Essessien, Sandra Oko-Oba, Pelumi Onafadeji, Lily Aimas-Love, Samuel Omolaiye, Abiodun Adereni

cs.CL

Answer continuation often returns a diagnosis already stated in unfinished clinical reasoning. We study this behavior in MamaReason, adapted from dense Gemma 4 31B, on MamaBench's 217 paired maternal and child-health presentations. An explicit-commitment extractor identifies a diagnosis in 75 of 94 failed RL first passes and 75 of 93 failed SFT first passes. The continuation matches that diagnosis in 67/75 RL cases and 58/75 SFT cases, using diagnosis strings alone. Reading these commitments without additional generation recovers 30 of the 40 RL pairs restored by continuation under the archived correctness labels. Recurrence controls distinguish routine mentions from repeated answer selection. An offline second-commitment trigger measures the tradeoff between earlier readout and paired accuracy. The resulting decomposition separates diagnostic content already present in a trace, completion of an accepted answer, and paired diagnostic correctness. For clinical language-model evaluation, these are distinct outcomes that become visible when the same reasoning trace is examined through multiple readouts.

GT-FENG: General Theory of Factual Evaluation and Normative Grounding in Language Models *Author(s)

Jiayu Feng

cs.CL

Whether a language model's answer is correct often depends on context: the same value can be right under one jurisdiction, date, or legal definition and wrong under another. We introduce GT-FENG (General Theory of Factual Evaluation and Normative Grounding), the first general framework to unify contextual/temporal correctness and rule-based error attribution across law, policy, and other rule-governed domains. GT-FENG checks answers against the applicable rules and identifies documented rules that match incorrect answers. It shows that when multiple rules give the same value, the answer alone cannot tell which rule the model used. In Medicaid income-limit answers from two models, documenting the asked state's own records reduces the number of wrong answers that match only other states' values by 68-78%, and more than half of stable wrong answers then match two or more documented sources. GT-FENG provides a foundation for evaluating language models in rule-governed domains and for future benchmarks and evaluation toolkits. We release GT-FENG as an open-source Python package, gtfeng.

Learning Word-Level Explanations of Text Reuse in Latin Literature with Little Annotation

Julian Schelb, Michael Wittweiler, Marie Revellio

cs.CL, https://github.com/julianschelb/retexo, Demo:, https://julianschelb.github.io/retexo/demo/

Detecting an intertextual reference is only the first step; philologists also need to know how a later author reworked the source. We explain a reuse pair word by word: each word of the reusing passage is first aligned to its source word, then labeled with the operation that transformed it, such as a copy, a change of inflection, or a substitution. Such word-level operations are expensive to annotate, and only a few corpora annotated at this level exist for text reuse in Latin literature. We describe a procedure that needs only a limited number of annotations: synthetic pairs, built by applying known operations to Latin text, train a first model, and active learning chooses the pairs an annotator corrects. Among several architectures, a model that links and labels together performs best: a pointer network that, for every reuse word, chooses a source word and names the operation in the same step. We show that with active learning, 200 pairs chosen by uncertainty are enough for the model to match one trained on every annotated pair of the 1,490 references of the Loci Similes benchmark. We release our code, word-level predictions for all references, and an interactive demo.

Evaluating Human Sensitivity to Gender Biased AI-Generated Content through Human Assessment of Google's Autocomplete Function

Tamara Serrano, Nikoleta Pantelidou, Raquel Montero, Natalia Moskvina, Paolo Morosi, Evelina Leivada

cs.CL

Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly present, but their effects on human users remain underexplored. We tested whether AI-attributed information influences views regarding gender-related statements, a domain where biases are pervasive. Spanish-speaking participants recruited through Prolific evaluated biased statements derived from Google's autocomplete function and human-authored, non-biased gender-related facts. Participants were either informed that some of the material was AI-generated or received no information about its origin. AI attribution did not affect reaction times or agreement judgments. This result suggests that AI-generated content does not automatically raise credibility concerns and may be evaluated with less scrutiny compared to human-produced content.

Gamer: A Generalist Vision-Language-Action Foundation Model for Long-Horizon Real-Time Game Agent

Run Luo, Kuan Zhang, Qi Deng, Yukun Chen, Zhihao Yang, Yufei Zhang, Renrong Hu, Yi Zhang, Guiyu Ma, Tianyu Fan, Longze Chen, Hao Sun, Jiaming Li, Jiaming Yang, Xiaohao Liu, Tao Tan, Minzheng Wang, Hangtian Jia, Shuai Yuan, Lvdi Wang, Xiao Liu, Chao Ma, Yuting Zou, Yinhe Zheng, Xiaobo Xia, Dawei Jiang, Shaoqing Ren

cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.CV

We introduce Gamer, a generalist agent framework built for long-horizon tasks in complex interactive environments. Its end-to-end architecture combines perception, memory management, and multi-step reasoning. A shared training and deployment pipeline supports interaction with both conventional 2D graphical interfaces and complex 3D simulated worlds. We first continue pre-training on a dataset constructed from tens of thousands of hours of device-level keyboard and mouse interaction. These trajectories span simulated games and embodied environments, allowing the model to learn environmental dynamics across both physical and virtual domains. Sparse-thinking instruction tuning then substantially reduces redundant computation while preserving the reasoning needed for complex decisions. We train the policy for long-horizon interaction with a stable multi-turn reinforcement learning method that addresses finite context windows, accumulated rollout errors, and sparse rewards. For real-time interaction, a cloud-edge inference engine serves numerous simulated game environments concurrently, supports unbounded-context execution, and keeps online inference latency low. Across multiple challenging 3D open-world games, Gamer demonstrates solid generalization and operational efficiency comparable to skilled players. It also achieves competitive results on a range of conventional 2D game benchmarks. These results suggest that even repetitive keyboard and mouse trajectories can support substantial gains from scaling data and learning algorithms. Training on these trajectories helps Gamer learn complex skills more steadily in environments with both physical and digital properties. This approach offers a path toward generalist agents that can interact across digital and physical worlds.

LitArc-Bench: Disentangling Text-Specific Reader-Response Dynamics from Position and Lexical Affect

Mingming Su, Yushan Wei, Sen Song, Xinche Zhang

cs.CL

When model predictions track an unfolding human affect trajectory, alignment may reflect narrative position, affective words, or text-specific reader-response dynamics. We introduce LitArc-Bench to separate these explanations: 39 Chinese-language literary excerpts segmented into seven checkpoints, with 430 questionnaires from 81 adults in two classroom groups (9,020 VAD ratings). We compare three future-blind model runs with leave-one-text-out position priors, lexical affect, and human-human references. Position alone reaches r = .470 for arousal, making raw alignment insufficient. Yet all six V/A transition advantages over a joint position-lexicon control have text-bootstrap intervals above zero. Cross-fitted models match the correct human text better than 38 mismatched texts in all nine model-dimension cells (p < .0001); a text-invariant negative control returns chance (p = 1). On human transitions at least 0.5 points against the position trend, models recover 69-76% of valence directions and 53-68% for arousal and dominance. Under crossed participant-text resampling, all nine identity intervals remain above zero; joint-control advantages are robust for valence and directional but less certain for arousal. High shape agreement still coexists with scale bias. LitArc-Bench identifies which components of literary reader response are recoverable beyond shallow explanations and why shape, calibration, and human variability require separate evaluation.

Do Verifier Judgments Survive Abstraction? Measuring Verification Transport for LLM Agent Evaluation

Disha Sheshanarayana, Sai Linisha Reddy Goli

cs.CL, cs.AI, Reliable, Agent, Development

LLM-based verifiers are increasingly used to evaluate agent executions, but their reliability is typically measured under a single representation of the trajectory. In deployed agent systems, the same execution may be exposed through composed tools, delegation boundaries, or compacted histories, changing what execution evidence is directly observable to the verifier. It therefore remains unclear whether correct verifier judgments persist across such abstraction boundaries. We introduce Verification Transport, a reliability test that measures judgment stability under abstraction-induced changes in execution observability. We construct matched native, abstracted, and restored views of the same frozen executions while holding the task, realized trajectory, outcome, ground truth, evaluation criterion, and verifier fixed. Across four agent-evaluation benchmarks, seven verifier backbones, four verification protocols, and three abstraction boundaries, we find substantial transport failure, reaching about 31% under macro abstraction and delegation. Verification protocols exhibit distinct failure modes: direct verification more often produces incorrect verdicts, whereas evidence-grounded and deliberative protocols tend to abstain under limited observability. This suggests that lower transport failure rates do not necessarily imply greater reliability. Restoring the corresponding execution provenance recovers 85.5% of transport failures, while recovery is lower when provenance is lossy or unavailable. These results show that native-view accuracy alone is insufficient to characterize verifier reliability when execution evidence crosses abstraction boundaries.

Interactive Program Induction with Language Model Priors

Robin Shing Moon Chan, Ryan Cotterell

cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.LG

Program induction refers to the process in which a learner aims to recover a program from a few given input-output examples. This paradigm broadens programming access by letting users demonstrate the rule-based behavior they want to apply to unseen data, instead of having to implement it themselves. However, recovering a program from finite input-output examples is fundamentally ambiguous, as a finite set of input-output examples is theoretically consistent with infinitely many programs whose output diverges on unseen inputs. We view this problem from a Bayesian perspective, where, given a prior $p^0$ over programs, identifying the target program means concentrating posterior probability mass on it by observing additional input-output examples. In the interactive program induction setting, the learner chooses maximally informative inputs and queries an oracle, such as the user, for their outputs. We derive a greedy query-selection algorithm and show that it terminates after $\mathcal{O}(\frac{1}{\gamma}\log 1/p^0(f^\star))$ informative oracle queries, and returns the target program $f^\star$ up to approximate equivalence when no other class outweighs it under the prior. The algorithm uses a language model as the prior under a PAC-style relaxation of program equivalence, where the query-selection objective is estimated using importance sampling. On the CodeARC benchmark, the algorithm improves accuracy over the LM only agent by up to 22 percentage points while observing up to 10.5 fewer input-output examples.

Agentic induction of common data elements and information extraction from sarcoma case report abstracts

Daniel Reichenpfader (1, 2), Kwaku Appiagyei (3), Manovah Nadarajah (4), Ahmed Reda Bahr (5), Irina Filchenko (6), Fabio Dennst\"adt (7, 8, 9) ((1) Institute for Patient-Centered Digital Health, Bern University of Applied Sciences, Bern, Switzerland, (2) Faculty of Medicine, University of Geneva, Geneva, Switzerland, (3) F. Hoffmann-La Roche AG, Basel, Switzerland, (4) Kantonsspital Aarau, Aarau, Switzerland, (5) University of Alexandria, Alexandria, Egypt, (6) Department of Neurology, Inselspital, Bern University Hospital, University of Bern, Bern, Switzerland, (7) Data Semantics, Decision Making Institute (DSDMI), St. Gallen, Switzerland, (8) Department of Radiation Oncology, Inselspital, Bern University Hospital, University of Bern, Bern, Switzerland, (9) Department of Digital Medicine, Inselspital, Bern University Hospital, University of Bern, Bern, Switzerland)

cs.CL

Common data elements (CDEs) support clinical data comparability but are usually defined manually. We investigated whether large language model agents could derive a CDE schema from sarcoma case report abstracts and apply it at scale. An agent proposed 72,909 candidate CDEs from 24,588 PubMed abstracts; agentic consolidation yielded 1,570 CDEs. We retained 53 supported by at least 50 abstracts and attempted evidence-linked extraction across the corpus. Processing succeeded for all retained CDEs in 79.2% of abstracts, including responses indicating non-mention. Two human reviewers approved 83.0% of retained CDEs for relevance, 67.9% for clarity, and 56.6% for correctness. In 100 sampled abstracts, exact agreement with the value extracted by the LLM was 94.9% among 1,116 pairs with a non-empty answer (abstract-level bootstrap 95% CI, 93.0-96.4%). Errors concentrated in anatomical and temporal fields. These findings support agent-assisted schema drafting followed by expert curation before large-scale extraction.

ParseBench-2: Severity-Aware Evaluation of Document Parsing

Pierre-Lo\"ic Doulcet, Boyang Zhang, Sebasti\'an G. Acosta, Sacha Bron, Daniel B. Ospina, Antonio Jimeno Yepes, Nutchapol Dendumrongsup, Simon Suo

cs.CV

Document-parsing metrics can give high scores to outputs that change important facts and penalize changes that preserve meaning. People can often judge these consequences more easily than they can design a metric to measure them. We introduce a method for developing parsing metrics from severity policies that specify how each error type should affect the score. We express these policies through test scenarios that specify how a metric should behave. We then optimize the metric implementations against these fixed expectations, using coding agents to refine comparison rules, error detectors and weights under human review. This makes stated human priorities an explicit objective of metric design. We apply this method in ParseBench-2, a comprehensive document-parsing benchmark that combines broad capability coverage with metrics calibrated to error severity. It covers 4,317 pages across six dimensions of parsing quality: text, formatting, tables, charts, visual grounding, and forms. We use detailed annotations and targeted difficult cases to check for changed facts, broken structural relationships, and missing content. We take the lower of a parsing score that measures content recovery and a severity cap that penalizes detected errors, so recovering most of a page cannot hide a serious error. Across six severity test suites, our metrics more closely match the expert judgments expressed by the scenario targets than existing metrics and penalize at most 3.4% of the tested benign variations. We conduct a comprehensive evaluation spanning frontier vision-language models, specialized document parsers and open-weight systems, comparing their overall rankings, performance across parsing capabilities and tradeoffs between accuracy and cost.

Real-Time Diffusion Relighting as a Neural Sensor: Closing the Low-Light Domain Gap for Robotic Perception

Shengjie Xu, Alexander Gao, Anshul Rai, Dae Yeol Lee, Guan-Ming Su, Ming C. Lin

cs.CV, Intelligent, Robots, and, Systems, (IROS), 2026., Project, page:, https://shengjie-xu.com/conferences/2026_IROS_neural-sensor/, Systems, (IROS),, Pittsburgh,, PA,, USA,, 2026,, pp., 12703-12710

Low-light conditions severely degrade robotic perception: object detectors trained on daylight imagery are prone to failure under dark and adverse weather conditions that fall outside of their training distribution. Furthermore, from an efficiency standpoint, existing Low-Light Image Enhancement (LLIE) methods are designed for offline processing and are therefore impractical to integrate into live control loops. To address these issues, we propose to embed a Generative Neural Sensor within the control loop that treats illumination as a generative prior rather than a signal processing problem, enabling task-relevant semantic recovery. We further propose an Real-Time Perceptual Architecture that decouples high-latency semantic recovery from low-latency robotic control. Rather than blocking the control loop, our system runs a large-scale image-to-image diffusion model in a background thread, and is activated only when a drop in detection confidence signals a perceptual uncertainty event, significantly reducing computational overhead. Our method sets a new state-of-the-art performance on the ExDark low-light benchmark with 87.1% mAP, and under adverse weather conditions, significantly boosts detection rates over competing methods. On a navigation task in a closed-loop autonomous driving simulator (CARLA), we achieve a 100% lap completion rate, establishing a simulation-based proof-of-concept for integrating high-latency generative models with standard robotic control autonomy stacks.

Adversarial Tracking: Attacks on Image Sensors for Object Tracking in Autonomous Vehicles

Han Wu, Alex Chan, Ekin Can Erkus, Tousif Rahman, Rishad Shafik

cs.CV, cs.AI

Object tracking systems estimate the motion of surrounding vehicles by linking detected objects across video frames. In particular, Tracking-By-Detection (TBD) systems combine object detectors with motion prediction and data association to maintain object identities over time. However, neural network-based detectors are vulnerable to adversarial attacks that perturb input images, and their impact on downstream trajectory estimation is not fully characterized by detection accuracy alone. Therefore, this paper evaluates image-specific and image-agnostic attacks on YOLOv3 and YOLOv4 detectors paired with SORT, Deep SORT, Strong SORT, and OC-SORT tracking systems. The evaluation uses CARLA simulator and measures tracking quality through Multiple Object Tracking Accuracy (MOTA), Higher Order Tracking Accuracy (HOTA), and Identification F1 (IDF1). Under image-specific attacks, HOTA for YOLOv3-based trackers declines from 53.45-56.19% to 14.85-17.03%, while MOTA reaches -772.60% in the worst case. YOLOv4-based trackers exhibit greater robustness, while HOTA still declines to 25.06-26.86%. In addition, image-agnostic attacks can reduce the tracking accuracy using a single Universal Adversarial Perturbation (UAP), without generating a separate perturbation for individual frames.

Rethinking Visual Reasoning in Text-to-Image Reward Modeling

Shiye Su, Xiaohan Wang, Ludwig Schmidt, Serena Yeung-Levy

cs.CV, cs.AI

Reward models are a core component of modern text-to-image systems, serving as the objective for inference-time selection and RL-based post-training. A key prerequisite is visual understanding: to judge prompt adherence and ground aesthetic preferences, a reward model must bind attributes, resolve relations, and count reliably. We find that widely-used reward models perform poorly on targeted visual reasoning evaluations. Even when starting from strong vision-language backbones, preference fine-tuning erodes these capabilities, and continual-learning baselines fail to mitigate a steep tradeoff between visual reasoning and preference accuracy. We propose a data-centric intervention that injects visual reasoning supervision into preference learning by flipping the pairing axis. To complement conventional human preference data (paired images under a shared prompt), we convert existing visual reasoning datasets into preference pairs that share the same image but differ in prompt, and train on the mixture. This yields a Pareto improvement in both visual understanding and preference prediction. Across inference-time best-of-n selection and RL post-training, the resulting reward model improves text-to-image generation on challenging compositional prompts (GenEval) by an average of 4.0% and 5.5% respectively, outperforming HPSv3 which was trained on 6x more preference pairs.

Where to Predict in Retinal OCT? Anatomy-Guided Target Selection for I-JEPA

Yunchu Feng, Wai Tak Lau, Kaveri A. Thakoor

cs.CV, NeurIPS, 2026, (non-archival)

Self-supervised learning aims to learn meaningful representations from unlabeled data, potentially allowing models to capture subtle anatomical variations across patients and reduce the amount of labeled data needed. Image-based Joint-Embedding Predictive Architecture (I-JEPA) learns by predicting target-region representations from visible context rather than reconstructing pixels. However, its uniform target placement does not account for the anatomical structure of retinal OCT, where retinal tissue occupies only part of each B-scan. We investigate anatomy-guided target selection using image intensity and predicted retinal segmentation while retaining the I-JEPA architecture and latent prediction objective. We compare anatomy-guided rectangular targets, anatomy-shaped targets and coverage-constrained placement with uniform masking, and evaluate the learned representations using linear probing for glaucoma classification on FairVision. We found that rectangular targets guided by image intensity achieved the highest linear-probe AUC in our comparisons. We show that anatomically meaningful target selection is promising for retinal OCT representation learning, while greater anatomical specificity did not consistently improve performance.

Training-Free Acceleration of Multi-View 3D Transformers via Regime-Aware Attention Approximation

Muhammed Izzet Sezer, Sevval Belkis Dikkaya

cs.CV, cs.AI

Multi-view 3D transformers such as VGGT reconstruct a scene from a set of unposed images in a single forward pass, predicting camera poses, depth and 3D points, but the cost of their global attention grows quadratically with the num- ber of images. Accelerators borrowed from large language models assume that each token attends to only a few oth- ers; on these models they lose accuracy or gain little speed. We study the global attention of three multi-view transform- ers on seven datasets and find that every layer plays one of three roles: it averages over the whole scene, matches points across many images, or anchors on the first image, whose coordinates the outputs use. Building on this anal- ysis, we propose Regime-AWare ATTention Approximation (RAWATTA), a training-free method that runs attention in 8- bit precision and keeps 16-bit precision only in the layers where a worst-case calibration check requires it. Its fast set- ting, RAWATTA-Fast, also approximates attention in a few averaging and anchoring layers, while matching layers and the first image keep all their keys. On a 12 GB GPU at 50 im- ages, RAWATTA runs VGGT faster than the released code, mostly through our CUDA engine, and loses at most about one point of pose accuracy (AUC@3) on any dataset; RAWATTA-Fast is slightly faster and less accurate. Both fit 200 images, where the released code runs out of memory at 100.

Provable Cross-Modal Transfer of Hierarchical Concept Structure in CLIP Embeddings

Nghia Nguyen, Tianjiao Ding, Lachlan Ewen MacDonald, Ren\'e Vidal

cs.CV, cs.LG

Contrastive vision-language models such as CLIP are trained to align images and text, not to represent taxonomies. Yet empirical studies show that CLIP embeddings exhibit hierarchical structure aligned with the hierarchy of semantic concepts. A theoretical characterization of why such hierarchy emerges from a non-hierarchical objective remains open. In this paper, we formalize this phenomenon as Hierarchical Angular Separation (HAS), a geometric property whereby each non-leaf concept embedding is more similar to its descendants than to off-branch concepts. Empirically, we show that HAS holds approximately in raw images and randomly initialized CLIP image embeddings, while in text embeddings it holds approximately only after contrastive learning. To theoretically explain this asymmetry, we analyze an unconstrained features model in which the image embeddings are fixed and only the text embeddings are trained. At the global minimizer, the text-side HAS violation percentage at each node is at most the image-side HAS violation and near-violation percentage. The key mechanism is the hierarchical co-occurrence structure of image-text pairs, which biases text embeddings toward root-centered image prototypes. For a linear contrastive loss, we derive a closed-form solution that makes this mechanism explicit. We extend the analysis to the InfoNCE loss and establish analogous guarantees under sufficient regularization. Synthetic experiments validate the theory and show that HAS is transferred even beyond the regimes covered by our analysis.

Open-World Scene Deocclusion

K.M.S.J. Kumarasinghe, Neil A. Dodgson, Fang-Lue Zhang

cs.CV

Recovering the complete appearance of occluded objects from a single image is a fundamental problem for scene understanding and image editing. Real-world scenes are challenging due to clutter, complex spatial occlusions, and diverse object categories. Existing methods often struggle when occlusion involves background surfaces or objects that are not explicitly detected, and fail to resolve the cyclic occlusion dependencies that frequently occur in natural scenes. We introduce a training-free, open-world scene deocclusion framework that recovers the full amodal appearance of detected or user-selected objects from a single RGB image, decomposing the scene into depth-ordered RGBA layers that are geometrically plausible, semantically faithful, and visually consistent with the visible scene. Extensive experiments show that our approach improves scene-level deocclusion, producing coherent and realistic reconstructions in real-world settings. Code will be available on the project page: https://sashjk.github.io/owsd/

RaFL: An Empirical Study of Random Filter Losses for Image Reconstruction

Ruibo Wang, Fengbei Liu, Aleksei Tiulpin, Mert R. Sabuncu, Heejong Kim

cs.CV, cs.LG

Pixel losses train image reconstruction networks toward a point statistic of the target distribution, often averaging away fine textures under uncertainty. Perceptual losses alleviate this issue using pretrained features, but those features are inherently domain-specific. We study a random filter loss (RaFL) that compares prediction and target through filters with random weights, avoiding both pretrained features and task-specific loss engineering. RaFL combines random convolutions, random Fourier features, and random wavelet filters, automatically balancing their contributions through gradient-norm matching, eliminating manual loss-weight tuning and grid search. We systematically investigate three design choices: filter family, weight resampling schedule, and weight initialization. Based on validation PSNR and stability across random seeds, we select the configuration that uses all three filter families, redraws filters every optimization step, and employs Gaussian initialization. We evaluate RaFL on three 2D and two 3D image reconstruction tasks. RaFL outperforms a pixel loss on three tasks and matches it on two, and demonstrates stable performance across random seeds on all five tasks. RaFL adds less than 2% to the backbone's forward FLOPs and no inference cost, requiring $182\times$ fewer loss FLOPs than a VGG19 perceptual loss. These findings suggest that RaFL is a simple, tuning-free, and domain-agnostic loss objective for 2D and 3D image reconstruction.

QCAP-VQA: Question-Conditioned Capped Prior-Mass Supervision for Medical Visual Question Answering

Yining Zhao, Catarina Moreira, Mukesh Prasad, Ali Braytee

cs.CV

Medical visual question answering models can answer correctly without attending to the anatomy that the question names. Automatic region priors allow spatial supervision without expert masks, but they are noisy, and it is unclear how strongly they should shape attention and how their effect should be verified. We present QCAP-VQA, which supervises a subset of cross-attention heads with a question-conditioned prior built from ranked segmentation proposals. Its capped prior-mass objective penalizes insufficient attention inside the prior only below a threshold. We evaluate attention against SLAKE structure boxes that play no role in training, separating attention on the named anatomy from how attention shifts between questions about the same image. QCAP-VQA matches the accuracy of a baseline without spatial supervision (84.26% vs. 84.23%), and every evaluated prior-supervised model places more attention on the annotated structures. Over a 64-fold range of regularization strength, the capped objective keeps prior mass between 0.67 and 0.77, whereas a pooled region-mass objective drives it to 0.97 and loses accuracy. The cost lies in question conditioning: as the constraint widens or strengthens, attention shifts agree less with the expert-defined contrast. Across 13 configurations, prior-referenced and expert-referenced evaluation rank the models in largely opposite order (Spearman correlation of -0.87), and the capped models it favors track the prior's own errors. Because clinical use of medical visual question answering requires answers grounded in the queried anatomy, supervision from automatic priors should be judged against independent anatomical references.

HarmonyText: Harmonious Scene Text Editing via Reverse-to-Refined Pair Synthesis and Region-Decoupled Frequency Learning

Ziqi Yi, Huiguo He, Guitao Xu, Peirong Zhang, Cheng Da, Xinyao Yu, Zhanpeng Wang, Changqian Yu, Huan Yang, Kun Gai, Lianwen Jin

cs.CV

Natural language-guided, mask-free scene text editing aims to modify text in real-world images using only textual instructions, yet remains challenged by unreliable pseudo-GT supervision, over-smoothed text generation, and the absence of GT-free evaluation. Existing methods often rely on imperfect pseudo-targets with background discrepancies, while standard MSE-based velocity regression tends to suppress high-frequency details, leading to blurred text boundaries and weakened local textures. To address these issues, we propose HarmonyText, a mask-free scene text editing framework with region- and frequency-aware supervision. HarmonyText introduces Region-Decoupling Optimization (RDO), which aligns edited text regions with pseudo-targets while anchoring preserved regions to the source image, and Cascaded High-Frequency Texture Guidance (CHFTG), which applies cascaded wavelet-domain constraints to preserve sharp boundaries and fine-grained textures of textual content. We further propose Reverse-to-Refined Pair Synthesis (RePair) to construct STE25K, a high-quality 25K paired dataset, and introduce a GT-free evaluation protocol with Color Distribution Divergence and a Harmony Assessment Model. Extensive experiments show that HarmonyText outperforms existing methods in text editing accuracy, background fidelity, color consistency, and perceptual harmony.

Stage-wise Lineage-aware Dropout for Sparse-View 3D Gaussian Splatting

Yan Xing, Zhi Chen, Fanglue Zhang, Junhong Zhao, Bing Xue, Mengjie Zhang

cs.CV

Sparse-view 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) is prone to overfitting because only a limited number of images are available to constrain scene geometry. Dropout is one regularization strategy for alleviating this problem. We introduce the concept of a lineage family to describe a set of Gaussian primitives derived from the same founder through cloning or splitting operations. The founder Gaussians refer to all Gaussian primitives generated during initialization. Descendants within the same family often jointly contribute to the refinement of adjacent local regions. However, dropping individual Gaussians can be less effective due to the compensation effect from neighboring Gaussians for the dropped ones. To address this issue, we propose Stage-wise Lineage-aware Dropout(SLD), which applies lineage-family dropout during the structural regularization stage and switches to individual dropout during the later refinement stage. We further propose Lineage-Balanced Densification(LBD), which operates during densification by constructing an expanded candidate pool and reallocating the densification budget according to the mean gradient scores and existing descendant sizes of different families. Experiments on the LLFF, MipNeRF360, and Blender datasets show that the proposed framework achieves competitive performance in sparse-view reconstruction

VACO: Vertex-Aligned Decoding for Dense Hand Contact Estimation from a Single Image

Binhong Ye, Jin-Chuan Shi, Tao Liu, Xin Wang, Hao Chen, Chunhua Shen

cs.CV

Dense hand contact estimation from a single RGB image is a foundational primitive for dexterous manipulation and 3D interaction analysis. Direct predictors typically decode the entire per-vertex contact map from one shared query, leaving individual vertices ungrounded in the image and smearing contact across adjacent, non-touching surfaces. We present VACO, a vertex-aligned decoder in which each MANO vertex samples local image features at its projected location. A hand-reconstruction backbone, jointly trained for reconstruction and contact, predicts these locations at inference without an auxiliary reconstructor. Training uses noise-perturbed ground-truth projections and end-to-end feature adaptation to improve robustness and contact-sensitive features. A single VACO checkpoint sets a new state of the art on five benchmarks spanning hand-object, hand-human, hand-scene, and bimanual interactions. It improves F1 by +20.7 pp over the strongest prior direct predictor on Hi4D and generalizes to the held-out HOT3D and HOCap, with gains of +5.5 and +2.7 pp. The resulting contact predictions also improve downstream HOI reconstruction.

Efficient Genotype Classification from 3D MRI in ADPKD via Density-Aware Point Sampling and E(3)-invariance

Kwanghee Lee, Hyeongsub Lee, Youngwoo Kim, Hyunji Jeong

cs.CV, cs.LG

Early detection of genetic kidney diseases, especially autosomal dominant polycystic kidney disease (ADPKD), is crucial because certain genotypes such as truncating PKD1 mutations cause rapid kidney growth and early progression to end-stage kidney disease. MRI-based genotype inference is a potential low-cost complement to genetic testing. However, although many deep learning methods have been developed for classifying various MR images, applying them to ADPKD remains challenging: (1) abdominal MR images are generally too large to be handled by deep learning models designed for smaller images like brain MRI, and (2) the amount of labeled data for ADPKD genetic mutations is insufficient to train deep learning models. In order to address these challenges, we propose E3PN, an efficient 3D MRI-based ADPKD genotype classification network that relies on point sampling and E(3)-invariance. We utilize a point representation of 3D MRI, which consumes less GPU memory than a voxel representation. For further memory efficiency, 3D points are sampled from a mixture distribution over the two kidneys in each image. Since E(3)-invariance has been shown to improve data efficiency, we also incorporate it into our model through coordinate canonicalization. On our ADPKD dataset, E3PN trains 163 times faster and uses 103x less GPU memory than a 3D ResNet, while also improving F1 from 0.165 to 0.226 over both baselines. These results establish a first benchmark for five-way ADPKD genotype classification from 3D MRI and show that point sampling and coordinate canonicalization yield two-orders-of-magnitude efficiency gains with a modest but consistent improvement in F1.

Creative Product Design under Brand Image Constraints

Takeshi Onishi, Hiroya Makino, Takahiro Yamaguchi, Hiroyuki Sakai

cs.CV, cs.AI, cs.GR

Industrial design requires creative visual exploration while satisfying constraints that are often product-specific and implicit. Existing text-to-image (T2I) approaches can generate creative designs through concept blending or multi-agent self-enhancement, but they provide limited mechanisms for ensuring that generated designs remain consistent with such constraints. We focus on brand image as a representative design constraint and propose a framework for brand-aware redesign generation. Given a product name, the framework constructs a brand image survey report that summarizes product and market information, derives a creative direction from the report, and integrates this direction into a self-enhancement loop in which generated images are critiqued and prompts are revised. We evaluate the framework on 50 redesign tasks across five product categories, comparing it with Naive T2I, IP-Adapter/IT-Blender, and unconstrained self-enhancement under the same T2I backbone. By a VLM-as-Judge protocol to measure creativity and brand identifiability, the proposed framework achieves a more favorable trade-off between the two objectives, with particularly clear gains in highly constrained categories such as automotive and motorcycle, where the plausible design space is relatively limited compared with that of other product categories. These results suggest that dynamically eliciting and textualizing brand image can guide creative product design generation under brand image constraints.

Learning Anisotropic Prediction Targets for Flow Models

Rayhan Zirvi, Hansheng Chen, Gordon Wetzstein, Leonidas Guibas

cs.CV, cs.LG

Flow-based generative models are trained on a hand-chosen prediction target, such as the velocity, the noise, or the clean data. This choice strongly affects training and sample quality. We introduce Anisotropic Flow Models (AnisoFlow), which replaces a fixed prediction target with a fully learnable one. AnisoFlow learns to scale the noise component of the target anisotropically, assigning a separate nonnegative weight to each direction in a jointly learned basis. A closed-form map recovers the velocity from the network's prediction, leaving the architecture, flow-matching loss, and sampler unchanged. Since recovery is exact, training optimizes the target purely to make it easier for the network to predict. We theoretically analyze the resulting prediction error and predict that the preferred noise weight in each direction scales near-linearly with data variance, in agreement with the weights learned empirically in pixel space. AnisoFlow improves FID from 1.90 to 1.55 over the JiT baseline on ImageNet 256$\times$256, by learning only the prediction target, without representation alignment or architectural changes. In pixel-space text-to-image generation, AnisoFlow improves image quality and, through better-conditioned velocity recovery, largely removes patch-seam artifacts under reduced-precision inference.

Towards Seconds-level Video Representation: Quadratically Gated Feature Grid with Adaptive, Prediction-free Sampling

Tie-Yun Li, Hao-Ran Wang, Chao Wang, Zhiyong Yin, Yiming Xu, Zhenwei Cai, Li Li, Rongzhi Lin, Feng-Lei Fan

cs.CV, cs.AI, https://github.com/MillenRosen/Q-GAS

Implicit neural video representations are notoriously slow to build, which does not cross the basic practical threshold for most industrial applications. Here, we present Q-GAS, a coordinate-queryable video representation that couples multi-resolution explicit 3D feature grids with a compact SwiGLU-style quadratic-gating (QG) decoder and an error-aware, prediction-free sampler. The explicit grids avoid hash-collision coefficient sharing at the cost of modestly higher storage yet greatly boost the expressive capacity; the QG decoder introduces low-rank local second-order interactions to improve the nonlinear feature response. During fitting, a global error map periodically refreshes a high-error pool, and each batch mixes pool samples with uniform samples, which is free from the auxiliary model to predict difficult samples, thereby further escalating the efficiency. On UVG Beauty/Bosphorus/Jockey, Q-GAS fits and quantizes under 50 s on one RTX PRO 6000, decodes at over 370 FPS with mixed precision and about 146 FPS in FP32. In our PyTorch implementation, Q-GAS trains and quantizes 5.5-7.2 times faster than the evaluated Instant-NGP baseline and 19-28 times faster than RNeRV. Under a 90 s budget, it improves PSNR/MS-SSIM/LPIPS over the evaluated NeRV-family pipelines at comparable BPP over the measured overlap. We have shared our code at https://github.com/MillenRosen/Q-GAS for readers' free use.

Can Large Language Models Optimize Pretrained Neural Network Weights? A Multi-Phase Empirical Evaluation of Gradient-Free Editing in YOLO26 Object Detection

Mohamed Abdallah Salem

cs.CV, cs.AI

Direct weight editing offers a gradient-free alternative to fine-tuning pretrained detectors, but its generalization and the value of large language model (LLM) guidance remain uncertain. We evaluate five experimental phases using YOLO26 models and MS COCO, comparing deterministic scaling, random perturbations, differential evolution, CMA-ES, structured interventions, real local-LLM proposals, failure-aware guidance, and hybrid LLM-CMA-ES against a matched random-policy control. Optimization uses native Ultralytics mAP50-95 on 1,000 COCO Val2017 images; selected candidates are assessed on a disjoint, previously examined 1,500-image subset, with COCO API AP50-95 reported separately where available. Increasing the CMA-ES budget from 8 to 250 evaluations raised development gains from +0.11 to +0.28 percentage points, while selection changes remained negative (-0.03 to -0.12 points); the 250-evaluation result uses one seed. The hybrid LLM approach showed no statistically established advantage over random-policy guidance (p = 0.612, 0.369, and 0.694 at budgets 8, 25, and 50). Five-seed YOLO26m replication yielded a COCO API AP difference of +0.004 points (95% CI: -0.026 to +0.034). Of 225 archived LLM proposals, 53 satisfy the final strict schema; early tensor-level execution cannot be fully verified. Genuine LLM-mediated weight editing is feasible, but the tested approaches did not demonstrate consistent transfer beyond the development subset or a measurable LLM advantage.

SceneAgent: A Multi-Agent Framework for Portable Game Scene Generation

Xingyi Li, Jiahao Yang, Yiqin Zhu, Jingwen Ye, Yang Sun, Weidong Zhang

cs.CV, cs.AI

Moving a scene generator to a new game engine requires more than exporting its final output: planning, construction tools, and stored experience may also depend on the original backend. We present SceneAgent, a multi-agent framework for reusing the construction process and its experience across backends. A canonical SceneContext maintains the evolving world, while backend adapters isolate coordinate conventions, asset bindings, and export. Structured handoffs let fresh Builder sessions query this persistent state without inheriting the full construction transcript. Across generations, human-approved traces are distilled into asset-ID-free Layout skills and semantic Content skills that can be retrieved and recombined. We introduce GameSceneBench, comprising 69 indoor and outdoor prompts. Under a common asset and evaluation protocol, SceneAgent obtains human tie-adjusted preference scores of 78.2-89.4% against five baselines. Ablations show that reusable skills and the multi-agent protocol primarily improve prompt alignment and scene completeness, with additional runtime. Deployment in a second engine preserves the shared construction protocol and skill library while replacing backend integration components. Together, these results support separating persistent scene state, task-local reasoning, and reusable experience for portable scene construction.

Beyond Endpoints: B\'ezier Paths for Vision Foundation Model Adaptation

Chenzhi Zheng, Xi Xiao, Chih-Ting Liao, Qizhen Lan, Jingwen Lin, Tianyang Wang, Hao Xu, Xin Cao

cs.CV

Frozen vision foundation models can be adapted by learning a few prompt tokens shared across all images. When prompts are updated as data shift or tasks change, the distribution of image features moves from one endpoint to another. Prior theory asks which endpoints prompts can reach; we ask whether they can travel between them along the shortest path. Because shared prompts compete with each image's own content for attention, they often cannot. We quantify this restriction as the extra transport cost that shared attention imposes over the shortest path. In an exactly solvable attention model, the reachable features form a cone; unrolling it into a plane gives the exact minimum action for every pair of endpoints and explicit optimal prompts on short arcs. The analysis reveals a sharp threshold in content normalization, which measures how much attention each image's own tokens absorb: for same-sign endpoints, at most two distinct levels allow prompts to follow the shortest path, whereas three or more force a strictly positive extra cost unless the endpoints differ only by rescaling. This gap persists with more prompts, small content perturbations, and aligned multihead outputs. On ViT-B/16 with VTAB-1k, a quadratic B\'ezier prompt path that minimizes feature action reduces held-out transport action relative to linear and cross-entropy-trained paths between the same endpoints and improves intermediate and online accuracy under unlabeled distribution shift, while cross-entropy-trained paths retain higher minimum accuracy when labeled calibration data are available. Reachable endpoints do not guarantee a reachable shortest path: how prompts move matters, not only where they end. Project page: https://xixiaouab.github.io/Beyond-Endpoints/.

Signal Scaffolding: Unlocking the Potential of Lightweight Models for Self-Supervised Monocular Depth Estimation

Zhijie Wang, Li Zhang, Yujie Chen, Shibiao Deng, Xin Gao, Houzhi Zhou, Jiangshuo Zhang

cs.CV

With the rising demand for edge devices, lightweight self-supervised monocular depth estimation methods have continued to evolve; however, existing methods generally suffer from poor accuracy. We find that the root cause may lies in the failure of conventional training regimes to fully exploit the inherent potential of lightweight models. To address this, we propose a novel three-phase training strategy for lightweight self-supervised monocular depth estimation, termed Signal Scaffolding (SS). Without introducing any additional dataset or parameters, we apply SS to ReForgeDepth, a lightweight network of our own design. Experiments on the KITTI and Cityscapes datasets demonstrate that our method achieves state-of-the-art performance in the accuracy-efficiency trade-off and running in real time on edge devices. These results suggest that lightweight models can be improved further within their existing capacity, and that the central question may be less about how parameters are allocated than about how to unlock this latent potential through training.

V-JEPA-LM: Video-Language Models That Read Dynamics Beyond Static, Text-Aligned Representations

Keuntae Kim, Dongjun Kim, Yong Suk Choi

cs.CV, cs.AI

World-model video encoders such as V-JEPA learn how scenes change (direction, speed, order, state) from video alone. To answer questions, they are joined to a language model trained on captions and question-answer pairs. But each caption or question names only a few attributes of a clip, so the model learns to retrieve those attributes rather than to read the clip, and the dynamics the encoder carries go unused: on the same items, a linear probe reads speed from frozen V-JEPA features at 87% while the VLM answers at chance. We show where dynamics lives in a frozen encoder: in a subspace shared across videos for predictive encoders such as V-JEPA, but not as a shared component of frame-wise, text-aligned encoders such as SigLIP. We then build counterfactual supervision that makes the language model read it: pixel transformations turn public videos into answer-balanced variants whose answers are set by the transformation and differ only in the change. Trained in a single stage on 4.53M public samples with 32 visual tokens per frame, where open video LLMs use several stages, 9M to 172M samples and 64 to 256 tokens per frame on average, the resulting V-JEPA-LM models (1B to 8B, Qwen3 and Llama) lead every size class in average score across eleven video benchmarks; our 4B model scores 72.1, against 61.1 for the best open 4B and 64.8 for the best open 8B model. A controlled encoder x supervision study shows that the encoder decides what can be read and the supervision what is read: at a matched budget, counterfactual supervision adds 4.5 points on average and 38 on the speed questions of natural benchmarks. Code and models: https://github.com/kkt94/Llama-V-JEPA-LM.

PADSNet: Physics-Aware Dual-Stream Network with Biomechanical Priors for Laryngeal Lesion Classification

Zhenzhen You, Weifeng Yu, Zhenghao Shi, Shuangli Du, Minghua Zhao, Yingcheng Zhao, Xinhong Hei, Demin Kong, Xiaoyong Ren, Jin Hou

cs.CV

Precise classification of laryngeal lesions is clinically crucial yet highly challenging, as inter-class visual differences are often subtle. While abnormalities in vocal fold vibratory dynamics provide complementary discriminative information, existing deep learning models predominantly rely on visual appearance and lack explicit mechanisms for modeling clinically meaningful vibratory dynamics. To address this, we propose PADSNet, a Physics-Aware Dual-Stream Network for laryngeal lesion classification that integrates visual representations with explicit biomechanical reasoning. Specifically, a Wavelet-Attention Enhancement Module (WAEM) is designed to bridge the spectral-semantic gap, enabling complementary learning of spatial texture and dynamic frequency representations. Meanwhile, a Knowledge Computing Module (KCM) extracts interpretable biomechanical indices from temporal glottal distance waveforms, including indicators of mobility, symmetry, glottal closure, and vibration stability. These descriptors are subsequently incorporated into a Physics-Aware Gating Module (PAGM) as biomechanical priors to adaptively recalibrate visual feature toward pathology-relevant vibratory patterns. Extensive experiments on a clinical dataset demonstrate that PADSNet achieves an accuracy of 0.8452, an F1-score of 0.8090, a sensitivity of 0.7953, and a specificity of 0.8866. These results indicate that explicitly integrating biomechanical priors with deep visual representations provides a more accurate, robust, and clinically interpretable framework for automated laryngeal lesion classification.

DoubleTake: Focused Verification of Visual Evidence for Long-Document Understanding

Kejun Xue, Ang Li, Jinghua Tang, Xincheng Wang, Yongbin Gao, Yanghua Xiao, Zhijun Fang

cs.CV

In long-document question answering, a vision-language model can answer incorrectly even when the retrieved pages contain sufficient evidence. Rereading those pages with the original question gives the model little guidance about which fact to check. We introduce DoubleTake, a training-free framework for focused verification of visual evidence. An initial reading produces a candidate answer and a separate evidence record that guides retrieval for a second reading. A planner compares the two candidates and their supporting evidence to formulate a factual question. A verifier answers it from the source images without seeing either candidate or evidence record, and a reasoner uses this observation to resolve the original question. With a frozen Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct backbone, DoubleTake achieves the highest overall accuracy among the evaluated methods on each of four benchmarks. With the same pages and number of model calls, asking a focused factual question yields higher accuracy on all four benchmarks than asking the original question again. Our code is available at https://github.com/small-code-cat/double_take.

Speed-Variation Distillation for Generative Modeling

Sukit Seripanitkarn, Phonphrm Thawatdamrongkit, Supasorn Suwajanakorn

cs.CV, https://speed-variance-modeling.github.io

While diffusion models excel at image generation, their denoising trajectories pass through corrupted intermediate states far from the image manifold. We ask whether generation can instead proceed through recognizable images throughout the entire trajectory. We introduce a generative framework built around a variation-reducing degradation process that contracts diverse images toward a shared prototype while preserving recognizable intermediate states. Because such manifold traversal lacks a simple analytical form, we construct the trajectories using generative-prior-based image morphing. We then distill the reverse process into a diversification operator that transforms the prototype into increasingly varied samples. To resolve the one-to-many ambiguity of the reverse process, we associate each trajectory with its initial noise latent and apply perturbations to improve robustness against accumulated errors. At inference, every step yields a recognizable image, allowing early termination and enabling a controllable speed--variation trade-off.

DySENSE: Dynamic Spatiotemporal ENergy Synthesis and Emulation with Satellite Imagery

Kailai Sun, Zijie Gao, Mingyi He, Can Rong, Alok Prakash, Baoshen Guo, Shenhao Wang, Jinhua Zhao

cs.CV, cs.AI

Cities account for over two-thirds of the world's energy and more than 70% of global carbon dioxide emissions. City energy is essential for achieving the 2050 net-zero target and the UN's Sustainable Development Goals 7 and 11. Although existing studies have made progress, they are limited by data and methodological gaps. Existing studies are largely predictive for existing cities rather than generative for future cities; fine-grained, aligned monthly dynamic energy data are scarce; and existing GenAI models struggle to generate dynamic urban functional monthly energy. We present DySENSE, a joint multimodal GenAI framework that synthesizes static urban satellite imagery together with spatially aligned, dynamic monthly building energy consumption maps. We decouple long-term static urban morphology from short-term dynamic energy consumption. By conditioning on static urban land use, DySENSE can generate realistic, diverse and new static urban imagery with aligned energy maps. By conditioning on dynamic monthly and climate prompts, DySENSE can jointly generate dynamic, monthly energy consumption maps while keeping the urban morphology static. Experiments across four cities (New York, Washington, Norfolk, Busan) demonstrated that DySENSE can generate annotated high-fidelity synthetic energy data using less than 25% labelled data, boosting downstream prediction performance by 8% mIoU, and achieving strong energy dynamic consistency. Compared to existing methods, DySENSE significantly improved prediction performance (8.7% accuracy) and reduced prediction error (9%-18% CVRMSE, ASHRAE). DySENSE offers an effective foundation for energy-efficient cities, peak-load analysis, building performance simulation and dynamic demand response for city, energy, and building science. The data and code: https://huggingface.co/datasets/skl24/MUSE2 and https://github.com/kailaisun/DySENSE

Photogrammetric 3D Model-Based Animal Behavior Classification

Olaf Hellwich, Moritz Heine, Patrik Reiske, Sole Traverso, Katharina Hohlbaum, Henning Sprekeler, Christa Th\"one-Reineke, Lars Lewejohann, Roman Bhuiyan

Video-based behavior estimation is often implemented end-to-end from image sequences. We advocate a two-stage alternative: reconstruct 3D body and object geometry before classifying behavior.
