See and Detect: Image-Driven Open-Vocabulary Multi-View 3D Object Detection

Yating Xu, Na Zhao, Chenhan Jiang, Qingwen Zhang, Jiankang Deng, Jifei Song

cs.CV

Recent open-vocabulary multi-view indoor 3D detection methods typically follow a point-cloud-driven pipeline, where pseudo 3D boxes are generated from reconstructed or estimated geometry and semantic labels are inferred from projected regions. However, such a design makes box generation dependent on the completeness of point clouds and restricts semantic learning to local appearance, causing inferior 3D object localization and open-vocabulary classification. In this paper, we present See and Detect, an image-driven framework that uses multi-view images as the primary evidence for 3D box and semantic label generation. For box generation, we first discover object candidates from individual images, enabling objects missing in point clouds to be recovered from visual evidence. However, single-view predictions are often noisy and redundant. To address this, we propose multi-view consensus box consolidation to fuse spatially consistent predictions across views, and box quality assessment to reduce the impact of boxes with partial object coverage. For semantic label generation, we introduce context-aware semantic learning, where semantic labels are queried from full image context, enabling more accurate semantic prediction. Extensive experiments on ScanNet and ARKitScenes demonstrate that our method achieves state-of-the-art open-vocabulary multi-view 3D detection performance while running over 500$\times$ faster than the previous state-of-the-art method.

More Genes, Faster Inference: Efficient Large-Panel Spatial Transcriptomics Prediction from Histology

Songming Zhang, Yuxiao Luo, Shifu Luo, Siran Chen, Shuquan Su, Jinyan Li

cs.CV, cs.AI, q-bio.QM

Spatial transcriptomics (ST) prediction from histology aims to infer spatially resolved gene expression profiles directly from tissue images. This is a challenging problem that is currently often addressed with complicated model architectures, additional inputs, and complicated training procedures, while remaining limited to fixed, small gene panels. This raises a natural question: Is there an efficient method for large-panel ST prediction? Here, we present GeneFlow, a simpler yet effective framework for large-scale spatial gene expression prediction that unifies flow-matching generative modeling with structured multimodal fusion. Specifically, GeneFlow first encodes both histological morphology and cross-gene interactions in spatial spots through histology and gene tokenizers. The model directly predicts spatial gene expression, rather than an intermediate velocity field, while being trained with a velocity-based flow-matching objective. We show that, even without relying on heavy generative pipelines, GeneFlow achieves state-of-the-art performance across multiple ST benchmarks, with up to 56.8% gains in 1,000-gene prediction. Extensive experiments further show that GeneFlow produces biologically meaningful spot profiles while reducing computational cost by orders of magnitude, thereby improving efficiency and scalability, particularly in clinical settings. We believe GeneFlow provides an opportunity for leveraging widely available histology images for downstream biological discovery. Code is available at: https://github.com/ZZhangsm/GeneFlow

AmbiSteer: Selectively Eliciting Ambiguity-Aware Responses in Vision-Language Models

Monica Riedler, Samuele Antonelli, Barbara Plank, Raffaella Bernardi

cs.CV, cs.CL

Vision-language models (VLMs) often exhibit overconfidence, providing firm answers even when visual evidence is insufficient. A prominent case is referential ambiguity in Visual Question Answering (VQA), where multiple entities in an image are plausible referents of a question. Addressing such cases requires recognizing when ambiguity is present: ambiguous questions can be handled by asking for clarification or enumerating plausible referents, while unambiguous questions should receive a direct answer. Current VLMs struggle with this distinction, often defaulting to a single interpretation. To investigate whether activation steering can serve as an inference-time mechanism to elicit such behaviors, we introduce AmbiSteer, a lightweight framework to selectively elicit ambiguity-aware behavior. Across three VLMs, we find that steering alone has limited and model-dependent effects, while prompting is generally more effective. Combining prompting and steering substantially increases clarification and enumeration behaviors, but also elicits them on unambiguous cases. AmbiSteer addresses this using a linear classifier to predict input ambiguity and condition the steering direction accordingly, increasing the proportion of inputs handled appropriately from 59.0% to 77.0% on average across models. Our work sets the ground for lightweight approaches to reduce model overconfidence in uncertain multimodal settings.

Zoom In, Scale Out: Metric Depth Models Mistake Digital Zoom for Moving Closer

Rabeya Noor, Khandoker Nosiba Arifin

cs.CV, https://github.com/rabeyanoor/zoom-equivariance

Many new AI models estimate how far away things are, in metres, from a single photo. To do this they must also work out what camera took the photo. We check whether they really do with a simple test: digital zoom, that is, cropping a photo and enlarging the crop back to full size. Zooming moves nothing in the scene, so a correct model should give the same distance for every point still in the picture. The test is free, exact and needs no labels. Two numbers describe a model's reaction: its depth changes like $s^{-\alpha}$ (ideal $\alpha=0$) and its focal-length estimate like $s^{\beta}$ (ideal $\beta=1$), for zoom factor $s$. On 800 images from KITTI, NYUv2 and DIODE, the widely used Depth Anything V2 has $\alpha=0.95$: it treats a zoomed photo almost as if the camera had moved closer, because it judges distance mainly from how large things look. Models that also predict the camera remove only part of this error ($\alpha\approx0.31$-$0.36$), because their focal estimate grows too little. Given the true camera, Depth Pro and DA3-Nested keep their depth ($\alpha\approx0$), so the error lies in their camera estimate. The reason: a zoomed photo is, pixel for pixel, a normal photo of a scene squashed in depth, so size alone cannot tell the two apart, and a focal estimator trained by regression stays close to the cameras it saw in training. Undoing this needs no labels or training and makes Depth Pro pass the test ($\alpha=0.04$) at almost no cost on normal photos. Controls, including a real optical zoom lens, rule out blur and resizing artefacts. Finally, label-free fine-tuning for self-consistency lowers $\alpha$ to 0.05 on KITTI and 0.07 on NYUv2 once a "blurry means zoomed" shortcut is removed; we report what this costs on normal and unseen photos.

Cracks in Embeddings: Robustness and Stability of JEPA-Based Chest X-Ray Representations

Zahid Mansur, Kalpana Yadav, and Deepak Mishra

cs.CV

Self-supervised Joint Embedding Predictive Architecture (JEPA) models are increasingly used for medical imaging, however there is a limited understanding about robustness of these models toward real-world image changes. We test four JEPA-based chest X-ray encoders, trained on 78,468 NIH ChestX-ray14 images, under six tests: two black-box adversarial attacks (SPSA, Boundary Attack), three image corruptions (Gaussian noise, brightness shift, contrast shift), and a label-scarcity test, measuring embedding change and classification AUROC. These perturbations cause severe, model-dependent damage: encoder-level Cosine Similarity drops as low as 0.33, and AUROC falls by as much as 0.12 in the worst case (I-JEPA under the Boundary Test). We test two ways of training the small predictor on top of each frozen encoder to address this. Adversarial fine-tuning, trained on clean and perturbed images together, raises embedding consistency above 0.97 in nearly every case and improves AUROC, relative to the same perturbed baseline, for most models, with the largest gain of $+0.10$ on I-JEPA. A Vector-Quantized codebook method, trained only on clean images and evaluated on perturbed test images for the first time, instead degrades under distortion for all four models, most noticeably for I-JEPA ($-0.15$ AUROC on average), showing that its discrete bottleneck alone does not provide the robustness that comes from training on perturbed data directly.

CardioTrace: Motion-Aware Cine Synthesis via Coupled Tracing and Segmentation

Zhi Chen, Le Zhang

cs.CV

Cardiac cine MRI provides time-resolved visualization of cardiac motion and is essential for evaluating ventricular function and anatomy. In routine clinical acquisition, however, cine sequences are frequently temporally sparse or degraded by arrhythmia, motion, and scan-time constraints, which can hinder reliable quantification and downstream analysis. We present CardioTrace, a motion-aware cine synthesis framework that generates a full cardiac cine sequence from limited input while explicitly modeling and tracing cardiac motion. CardioTrace couples two complementary pathways: a pixel-translation branch that directly predicts cine frames in a residual formulation, and a flow-field branch that estimates time-resolved deformation fields from a reference phase to provide interpretable motion correspondences across frames. To further preserve anatomical plausibility, a segmentation branch supervises clinically meaningful cardiac structures at key phases, and a cross-branch consistency objective encourages the synthesized appearance dynamics to follow the explicit motion guidance, mitigating temporal drift and unrealistic shape transitions over long sequences. The framework also supports prompt-style conditioning to incorporate auxiliary patient or acquisition information, improving robustness across heterogeneous multi-center data. Experiments on public cardiac cine MRI datasets demonstrate that CardioTrace improves perceptual quality and temporal coherence over representative GAN- and diffusion-based baselines, while better preserving ventricular morphology throughout the cardiac cycle. \textit{Our codes are available after review}.

Vessel2Brain: Edge-Aware Multi-Scale Flow Matching for 3D Brain Image Generation from Vessel Masks

Zhi Chen, Tianyang Zhang, Theodoros N. Arvanitis, and Le Zhang

cs.CV

Generating realistic 3D brain MRI volumes from sparse anatomical priors is a highly ill-posed yet clinically valuable problem, with important applications in data augmentation, simulation, and downstream neuroimaging analysis. In this work, we investigate the challenging task of synthesising full 3D brain MRI volumes conditioned solely on vascular label maps. While vascular structures provide strong anatomical and topological cues, they occupy only a small fraction of the brain volume and offer no direct information about surrounding tissue appearance. To address this challenge, we propose Vessel2Brain, an edge-aware multi-scale conditional flow matching framework that explicitly incorporates vascular structure information into the generative process. Our approach formulates vessel-guided brain image synthesis as a conditional continuous flow, enabling stable training and flexible generation while preserving structural correspondence. A multi-scale flow matching strategy is introduced to jointly model global anatomical coherence and fine-grained local details, with auxiliary velocity predictions providing additional supervision across spatial resolutions. Furthermore, we design an edge-aware objective that emphasises vascular boundaries during training, encouraging anatomically faithful tissue synthesis around vessel structures. We evaluate Vessel2Brain on two public 3D brain MRI datasets with paired vascular annotations. Extensive quantitative and qualitative experiments demonstrate that our method consistently outperforms state-of-the-art generative models in terms of reconstruction accuracy, structural consistency, and distributional realism.

Token2Tile: Token-Wise Sparse Attention with Tile Execution through a Low-Rank Space for Video DiTs

Xuecheng Wu, Aichen Feng, Jinyan Chen, Qingjie Zhao, Julien Lai

cs.CV

Attention is essential for modeling spatio-temporal dependencies in video diffusion transformers, but its quadratic cost makes it a major inference bottleneck. Its high sparsity offers an opportunity to accelerate generation by focusing computation on important interactions. However, accuracy and execution favor different granularities: each query attends to its own key tokens, whereas GPU kernels work on tiles. We present Token2Tile, a training-free sparse attention operator that combines per-query scoring and a per-query residual with tile execution. An online low-rank space, computed directly from queries and keys to preserve attention logits, makes clustering, scoring, and residual approximation efficient. Token2Tile unites query-specific selections to allocate exact work across query clusters, then packs the selected keys into GPU tiles. On MiniMax-H3 and Wan2.1-14B, Token2Tile delivers 1.48-1.54x end-to-end speedup over dense FlashAttention-3 with VBench scores within 0.5 points of dense. At comparable speeds on H3, it improves PSNR over SOL and SubBlock by 5.5-6.8 dB; on Wan, it exceeds SOL by 4.6-4.8 dB while running faster.

Domain Adaptation Based Pipeline for Character Classification and Handwritten Text Recognition

Florent Imbert, Simon Corbill\'e, Hui Han, Elisa H. Barney Smith

cs.CV, cs.LG, (IJDAR),, 2026

Character classification and handwritten text recognition, particularly in specialized tasks, often suffer from limited labeled data, while a large amount of unlabeled data remains unused. To address this challenge, we propose a novel unsupervised domain adaptation-based pipeline to reduce the distribution gap between source and target domains. Our pipeline follows three main steps. In the first step, we pretrained an expert model on source data, to learn a strong general representation. In the second step, we perform unsupervised domain adaptation to leverage unlabeled target data, adapting the model to the specific characteristics of the target domain. In the third step, we trained the expert model on source data in the target style. By aligning the feature distributions between the source and target, our method enables the model to generalize effectively across diverse datasets. The unsupervised training increases the computation time only during training not in inference. We evaluate the pipeline on both character-level and historical handwritten line recognition tasks, demonstrating its flexibility. We establish a new state-of-the-art on the Safran-MNIST-DLS dataset defined in Track 2 of the DAGECC competition with 76.17% Macro F1-score and achieve competitive results on each of the specific datasets of READ 2018 without using the labels of the target data. Code is available at https://github.com/simon-corbi/DRANet-for-classification-and-htr

nnAEU: a Simple and Efficient CNN-Autoencoder for Hyperspectral Unmixing

Edgard Dabier, Christophe Kervazo, Pietro Gori, Florence Tupin

cs.CV

Deep learning-based hyperspectral unmixing (HSU) has followed a trajectory of increasing architectural complexity, from simple autoencoders to transformers and, most recently, Mamba-based models, each claiming to advance the state of the art. Contrary to this trend, we propose a lightweight CNN-based autoencoder, coined "Not New Autoencoder Unmixing" (nnAEU). It is derived from the backbone of UNMamba (2025) through a pruning study that removes the Mamba component entirely. nnAEU uses less than 10 times fewer parameters than UNMamba, and far fewer than other transformer- and GAN-based methods, while outperforming them in accuracy. We provide a complete, minimal, reproducible code, available at https://gitlab.telecom-paris.fr/ring/nnAEU.git.

MacroGS: Macro-Pixel Shared Gaussian Primitives for Arbitrary-Scale Light Field Super-Resolutio

Zeqiang Wei, Angulia Yang, Min Xu

cs.CV

Arbitrary-scale light field spatial super-resolution reconstructs high-resolution views at any requested scale from low-resolution multi-view observations. Gaussian primitives enable continuous reconstruction, but generating separate primitive sets for each view redundantly encodes shared scene structure across views and causes the primitive count grow linearly with the number of views. To avoid such redundancy, we propose MacroGS, a feed-forward decoder that jointly predicts a single shared Gaussian set per macro pixel rather than generating separate sets for each view. Disparity-dependent replacement and angle-conditioned appearance allow each set to be reused across all output views, keeping the predicted primitive count independent of the number of views at each requested scale. The decoder iteratively predicts per-primitive disparities, retrieves the corresponding multi-view features, and updates the primitive states. Gaussian splatting and residual refinement then reconstruct the output light field. Despite being learned without geometric supervision, the predicted disparities strongly correlate with underlying scene geometry. Experiments across five benchmarks demonstrate that MacroGS integrates effectively with diverse light field encoders, improving reconstruction quality at fixed scales while achieving leading performance across most evaluated arbitrary-scale settings. The decoder also achieves speedups of approximately $2$-$10\times$ over state-of-the-art implicit light field decoders and $10$-$18\times$ over the view-wise Gaussian baseline.

From Images to Grounded Edits: A Real-World Benchmark for Chinese Grammatical Error Correction in Handwritten Essays

Weiru Zhang, Yuzhong Hong, Junwei Bao, Lemao Liu, Dejing Dou

cs.CV, cs.AI

Chinese Grammatical Error Correction (CGEC) is typically formulated as a text-to-text problem. While suitable for digital texts, this formulation falls short in real-world K-12 education, where AI-assisted corrections are expected directly on essay images, which are primarily handwritten and exhibit many irregularities. To bridge this gap, we introduce Grounded Chinese Grammatical Error Correction (G-CGEC), an image-to-grounded-edit task requiring systems to predict both corrections and their positions on essay images. We propose G-CGEC-Eval, a benchmark comprising real-world images of K-12 Chinese essays with fine-grained annotations. For evaluation, we adapt IoU-based metrics from object detection and propose a more tolerant alternative based on center-distance matching. Using this benchmark, we evaluate open-source and proprietary vision-language models under a direct single-pass setting, as well as settings with auxiliary components (OCR and character grounding) coordinated via pipelining or agentic harnessing. Results show that even top-tier proprietary models perform surprisingly poorly in the single-pass setting, while pipelining and agentic harnessing yield meaningful improvements. Notably, an error-type-based multi-agent workflow achieves the best performance, establishing it as the strongest baseline for future G-CGEC research. Through ablations and fine-grained analysis, we find the task remains far from solved, with linguistic error discovery and character-level grounding as the primary bottlenecks. This underscores the need for models that better integrate visual perception with linguistic reasoning and visual grounding.

RSSA: Robust Semantic and Spatial Aligner for Collaborative Perception

Zhihao Yang, Zhiyu Xiang, Peng Xu, Tianyu Pu, Kai Wang, Eryun Liu, Dongping Zhang, Yong Ding

cs.CV, https://github.com/Megalo-f/RSSA

V2X collaborative perception improves single-vehicle perception by aggregating multi-view features from collaborative agents. However, existing methods typically rely on simple spatial warping to align the collaborative features, which can cause semantic misalignment during fusion. Meanwhile, these deterministic warping-based methods are highly sensitive to the positional noises. In this paper, we propose a plug-in module called Robust Semantic and Spatial Aligner (RSSA), which performs semantic and probabilistic spatial transformations for robust feature alignment. Specifically, a Semantic Transformation (SemT) block modulates feature channels based on the observed relative pose to correct semantic inconsistencies. Subsequently, a Probabilistic Spatial Transformation (P-SpaT) block performs probabilistic warping by sampling from the Gaussian-modeled relative pose space. A Relative Transformation Augmentation (RT-Aug) strategy is further introduced to augment the relative poses and facilitate the training of the RSSA. Extensive experiments on both simulated and real-world datasets demonstrate that RSSA improves the state-of-the-art methods by a large margin under the configuration of both clean and noisy positions.

JaRS: Contrast-Free Localization of Memorized Regions in Diffusion Models via Jacobian Row Sums

Saisuchith Mahajan, Aditey Nandan, Rohit Singh, Nishanth Shetty, Chandra Sekhar Seelamantula

cs.CV, cs.LG

Diffusion models sometimes memorize and reproduce regions of their training images, raising privacy and copyright concerns. We aim to localize these regions. Existing contrast-based methods use the diagonal of the denoiser Jacobian, which reflects the posterior variance of each pixel. However, a small diagonal entry is ambiguous: it signals either memorization or averaging over many neighboring pixels in smooth or predictable regions. To resolve this ambiguity, these methods subtract a reference map, which requires a second evaluation with a null prompt or an earlier checkpoint. Unconditional models have no null prompt, and earlier checkpoints are unavailable in general. We show that Jacobian row sums (JaRS), unlike the diagonal, separate memorization from local averaging without a reference map. Pairing JaRS with an image-level detection score that uses the same statistic at an early denoising step yields JaRS+. Across Stable Diffusion v1.4 and v2.1, Realistic Vision v5.1, and an unconditional diffusion Transformer (DiT), JaRS+ outperforms contrast-based localization methods.

Multi-level latent-diffusion adaptation for cross-region point cloud segmentation of ancient terraces

Sina Mohammadi, Roderik Lindenbergh, Javier Ruiz-P\'erez, Marco Madella, Sebastian Fajardo

cs.CV

Automated lidar feature detection from lidar point clouds can support archaeological surveys, but models trained in one landscape may transfer poorly to another because of differences in terrain, vegetation, acquisition characteristics, and feature morphology. We address this problem for cross-region detection of ancient terraces using transductive unsupervised domain adaptation that exploits an unlabelled target survey. We propose MELD (Multi-level Embedding-space Latent Diffusion), which trains separate diffusion denoisers on target-domain representations at four hierarchical PointNet++ feature levels. These target distribution priors are then used to refine source representations during supervised training and target representations during inference, allowing adaptation to act directly on the multi-scale features consumed by the segmentation decoder. We additionally investigate local geometric descriptors derived from K-nearest-neighbour neighbourhoods, including surface normals, slope, and aspect. MELD is evaluated bidirectionally between the Teyuna and Miraflores lidar surveys in the Sierra Nevada de Santa Marta, Colombia, against a source-only model and nine configurations of established domain adaptation approaches. MELD raises F1 over the source-only model from 0.397 to 0.459 for Teyuna-to-Miraflores transfer and from 0.557 to 0.594 in the reverse direction, achieving the highest F1 among the evaluated adaptation methods in both directions. Adding local geometry further raises F1 to 0.542 and 0.606, respectively. Multi-level ablation shows that refining all four PointNet++ levels provides the strongest performance in both directions. These results show that explicit modelling of target feature distributions in hierarchical point cloud feature space, complemented by local geometry, provides an effective route to cross-region archaeological mapping without target labels.

InfiHall-Bench: A Benchmark for Hallucination Evaluation in Autoregressive Text-to-Image Generation

Yuxia Qiao, Xinhao Zhong, Junhao Li, Yi Sun, Jiaxin Hong, Shuoyang Sun, Bin Chen

cs.CV

Hallucination benchmarks for text-to-image generation are predominantly designed around diffusion models, limiting their diagnostic value for visual autoregressive (VAR) models and the development of mitigation methods. We identify two limitations in existing prompt construction paradigms: many prompts are no longer challenging for modern generators, while category-based construction relies on recurring object-attribute-relation templates and rarely tests interactions among multiple visual requirements within a single image. We introduce InfiHall-Bench, a controlled benchmark of 411 prompts constructed by systematically composing five foundational and visually verifiable skills: attribute binding, spatial relation, action relation, part consistency, and scene consistency. Each prompt covers 4.05 annotated skills on average, enabling the evaluation of hallucinations under diverse combinations of visual requirements. Using this benchmark, we find that hallucinations in VAR models exhibit a scale-wise persistence pattern: semantic errors can emerge at different autoregressive scales and are rarely corrected by subsequent fine-scale steps. Motivated by this observation, we propose Confidence-Masked Hallucination Mitigation (CMHM), which estimates the prediction confidence of target visual tokens at each autoregressive scale during teacher-forced fine-tuning. CMHM downweights reliably predicted tokens, allowing optimization to focus on uncertain predictions across scales. Experiments show that InfiHall-Bench distinguishes models that existing compositional benchmarks rate as comparable, while CMHM consistently outperforms uniform LoRA fine-tuning for VAR-based text-to-image generation.

SWAM: Saliency-Weighted Asymmetric Merging for Efficient Vision Transformers

Utkarsh Kumar Singh, Manish Das, Shiv Ram Dubey, Satish Kumar Singh

cs.CV

Vision Transformers (ViTs) achieve strong performance on image recognition tasks, yet their quadratic self-attention complexity over input tokens creates prohibitive computational costs for practical deployment. Token reduction methods offer a practical solution by progressively reducing the token sequence during the forward pass. We propose Saliency-Weighted Asymmetric Merging (SWAM), a training-based token merging framework that extends the Bipartite Soft Matching (BSM) with two saliency-aware mechanisms. We introduce a two-step matching formulation in which cosine similarity decides candidate pairs, while a multiplicative maximum saliency penalty regulates their selection for merging. We then further use asymmetric exponential saliency-weighted aggregation, which allows more salient tokens to contribute more strongly to the merged representation. A lightweight MLP-based saliency prediction module (75k parameters per stage, 1% of backbone parameters) supervised via attention rollout is trained end-to-end jointly with a DeiT-Small backbone using a composite cross-entropy, knowledge-distillation, and saliency-alignment objective. On CIFAR-100, TinyImageNet, and ImageNet-100, under a conservative schedule that reduces tokens from 196 to 100 patch tokens, with the CLS token retained throughout, 27.5% FLOPs reduction and 1.35 times speedup, SWAM matches or exceeds baseline across the reported results. Under an aggressive schedule (196 to 70 tokens with the CLS token kept throughout, 33.8% FLOPs reduction, and 1.46 times speedup), accuracy gains are achieved on ImageNet-100 (+0.23%) with minimal losses on CIFAR-100 and TinyImageNet.

TelePhysica: Agentic Reconstruction of Physically Interactive Scenes

Ruiqi Shu, Xufeng Zhan, Yuanzhi Liang, Haibin Huang, Chi Zhang, Xuelong Li

cs.CV, cs.AI

Direct image-to-video diffusion models entangle pixel synthesis with implicit latent motion, precluding persistent 3D mechanical equilibrium, mid-rollout intervention, and counterfactual force control. Conversely, single-pass programmatic 3D reconstruction from an uncalibrated photograph leaves monocular depth and support clearance underconstrained, causing reconstructed scenes to interpenetrate or collapse under gravity. We present TelePhysica, a closed-loop framework that reconstructs an executable, multi-regime physical world from a single photograph to drive stateful 3D interaction and trajectory-grounded video synthesis. A vision-language coding agent synthesizes separable visual meshes and declarative physical models within a two-stage verification loop that pairs pixel-aligned silhouette critique with a zero-force gravitational equilibrium preflight. The verified world compiles into a unified solver coupling rigid bodies, articulated chains, co-dimensional cloth shells, and 3D tetrahedral elastodynamics, whose simulated rollouts condition pretrained video diffusion models through pixel-aligned structural streams. On a 100-scene single-image benchmark, closed-loop execution raises equilibrium-verified world recovery from $0\%$ in single-pass synthesis to $81\%$. Controlled constitutive ablations show over an order-of-magnitude larger localized corner displacement and out-of-plane pleating in finite-element cloth than in matched rigid-plate proxies. Conditioning video diffusion on simulated trajectories increases dynamic motion-mask overlap by $2{\times}\text{--}5{\times}$ and preserves motion direction under counterfactual force reversals where direct image-to-video models fail.

AG-JEPA: Anatomically Guided Joint Embedding Predictive Architecture for Neurodegenerative Representation Learning

Azad Singh, Ayantika Das, Amog Rao, Aiman Farooq, Anirban Porya, Ananya Shukla, Sowbhagya Suresh, Srinivasan Iyengar, Mohit Jain, Mercy Prasanna Ranjit

cs.CV, cs.AI

Brain MRI can reveal early signs of Alzheimers disease (AD) before measurable cognitive decline becomes apparent. However, during the transition from cognitively normal (CN) to mild cognitive impairment (MCI), the disease-related signals are often subtle and confined to only a few brain regions. Existing Joint Embedding Predictive Architecture (JEPA) models learn general structural representations but do not explicitly capture the anatomical regions affected in AD. We propose Anatomically Guided JEPA (AG-JEPA), which introduces anatomical tokens that interact with patch tokens during pretraining to learn region-specific representations from brain regions relevant to neurodegeneration. Building on this representation space, we further propose Prog-AG-JEPA, which models disease progression trajectory as a conditioned latent displacement within this space. We evaluate AG-JEPA across five downstream tasks spanning Alzheimer's disease diagnosis and progression prediction. AG-JEPA improves diagnosis and MCI prognosis, achieving an 8.4 percentage point AUROC gain over the strongest baseline on CN/MCI classification and a 7.1 percentage point gain on out-of-distribution CN/AD classification on the MR-RATE dataset, highlighting its effectiveness across both early-stage detection and cross-cohort generalization. For disease progression modeling, Prog-AG-JEPA consistently outperforms all baselines on future diagnosis and brain volume change prediction, demonstrating the value of anatomically grounded neurodegeneration representations for diagnosis and prognosis.

EcoVGGT: Pinned-Key Inference for Efficient and Streamable Feed-Forward 3D Reconstruction from Long Videos

Hongsen Cao, Shanxin Yuan, Mona Jaber, Ahmed Sayed

cs.CV, cs.AI

Feed-forward multi-view transformers such as VGGT recover the cameras and geometry of a video in one pass, but the cost of their cross-frame attention grows quadratically with the frame count, and their accuracy drops on long videos. Most remedies approximate attention over all frames within a budget or select a few keys by their content. We hypothesize that the keys of a long video can be chosen without looking at its content, and our experiments on VGGT and $\pi^3$ support it: a few dozen evenly spread frames can serve as the keys of every frame. We present EcoVGGT, a training-free inference framework whose keys form a Pinned Key Set of 31 such frames. A pin pass stores their tokens in a Scene Cache, and the other frames read it in independent batches that reproduce joint inference with these keys exactly, at a constant cost per frame. An Online Stream updates the pinned frames during recording. Six middle global blocks align the frames and form a reusable VGGT module: the Alignment Score reads their attention to flag failures, and Alignment Refocusing reduces them by fine-tuning only these blocks (3.6 GPU hours). EcoVGGT ($\pi^3$) matches the success rate of $\pi^3$ on 1,000-frame ScanNet videos at 13.8 times its speed. With refocusing, EcoVGGT (VGGT) raises the success rate of VGGT from 70% to 82% at 8.5 times its speed on 500 frames. EcoVGGT reconstructs 4,000 frames on one GPU, and EcoVGGT ($\pi^3$) streams 1,000 frames at 18.6 frames per second with the lowest online absolute trajectory error.

KnowVis: Dual-View Evaluation and Diagnosis of World-Knowledge Grounding in Text-to-Image Models

Yonghang Yu, Ruoyu Wang, Haoyu Zhang, Lin Qi, Yawei Zhou, Changqian Yu

cs.CV, https://github.com/QuantumWhisper42/KnowVis

Text-to-image (T2I) models have made substantial progress in visual realism, aesthetic quality, and instruction following. However, real-world prompts often go beyond explicit visual descriptions and require implicit facts, structured knowledge, and domain-specific commonsense. Existing evaluations mainly focus on explicit prompt-to-image semantic alignment or specific knowledge subdomains, lacking unified evaluation and diagnosis of world-knowledge grounding in T2I models. We introduce KnowVis for dual-view evaluation and diagnosis of world-knowledge grounding in T2I generation. Built on a hierarchical knowledge taxonomy with case-level annotations, KnowVis combines two complementary benchmark views with a structured MLLM-based VQA judge protocol. The Skill-Tree Benchmark supports fine-grained atomic knowledge evaluation, while the Generalized Benchmark examines open-ended knowledge composition and expression. The protocol uses Required VQAs to assess atomic knowledge correctness in the Skill-Tree Benchmark, and combines Required, Optional, and dynamically discovered knowledge expressions with human-judge collaborative evaluation in the Generalized Benchmark. We validate the protocol's reliability on the Skill-Tree validation split, enabling scalable evaluation and diagnostic error attribution. Experiments show that KnowVis reveals notable gaps in world-knowledge grounding across existing T2I models and provides structured diagnostic signals for knowledge-intensive image generation.

DRA-HTR: Disentangling Representation and Adaptation for Handwritten Text Recognition

Gayan H. Pathirage, Florent Imbert, Denis Coquenet, Elisa H. Barney Smith

cs.CV

Handwritten text recognition (HTR), particularly when applied to historical documents, remains challenging due to the scarcity of annotated data and the substantial domain shifts caused by variations in handwriting styles, document degradation, acquisition conditions, and linguistic characteristics. While large amounts of unlabeled target-domain data are often available, effectively exploiting them requires adaptation strategies tailored to the specific requirements of HTR. We propose DRA-HTR, an unsupervised generative domain adaptation pipeline specifically designed for HTR and built upon the DRANet framework. The proposed approach combines source-domain pretraining, unsupervised adaptation to the visual characteristics of the target domain, relying on the generation of target-style source samples for task-specific training. We further investigate the integration of post-HTR correction to complement visual adaptation with language adaptation, relying on large language models, which we introduce as DRAL-HTR. The proposed framework is evaluated across seven HTR datasets spanning four languages, using a single set of hyperparameters for all source-target combinations. The recognition model achieves state-of-the-art performance on four datasets and competitive results on the remaining three. In addition, the proposed DRA-HTR establishes a new state-of-the-art for domain adaptation across all seven datasets and substantially improves overall robustness and training stability compared to the original DRANet framework: it reduces the Character Error Rate (CER) in average by 13.52 percentage points, and the average standard deviation decreases from 7.23 to 1.11 percentage points. Finally, post-HTR correction further reduces the CER by 2.16 percentage points.

ALoMa: Affine Correspondences Beyond Patches

David Nordstr\"om, Georg B\"okman, Viktor Larsson, Fredrik Kahl, Jonathan Ventura

cs.CV

Affine correspondences (ACs) enable robust and efficient solvers for multiple-view geometry by constraining the local geometry of point correspondences (PCs). Despite their utility in minimal solvers, AC estimation remains understudied. We revisit AC estimation beyond local image patches, extending it to the image and image-pair levels. Rather than estimating the components of the affine transformation separately, we learn the full 4-DoF matrix jointly. Building on this formulation, we introduce ALoMa, an AC estimator using LoMa keypoint descriptions, demonstrating consistent improvements across a wide range of downstream tasks. Code at https://github.com/davnords/loma.

STaG: Human-Annotation-Free Unsupervised Domain Adaptation for Text-Based Person Retrieval

Ji Yucheng, Bai Yang, Li Yang, Zhou Xinyu, Cao Min

cs.CV

Real-world deployment of Text-Based Person Retrieval (TBPR) is bottlenecked by the absence of textual annotations in new domains. Existing Unsupervised Domain Adaptation (UDA-TBPR) methods still assume access to target-domain human-written annotations (albeit unpaired with images), limiting their practicality. We consider a more practical setting: human-annotation-free UDA-TBPR, with labeled image-text data in the source domain and annotation-free, unlabeled images in the target domain. This setting introduces two core challenges: the absence of cross-modal alignment in the target domain, and visual domain shifts from source to target. To tackle these, we propose STaG, a dual-path framework comprising Style-Agnostic Text Alignment (SATA) and Heterogeneous Graph Memory Alignment (HGMA). Specifically, SATA leverages source-domain data to decouple identity-discriminative semantics from machine-generated stylistic artifacts, establishing robust cross-modal alignment for the target domain. Meanwhile, HGMA exploits cross-domain semantic neighborhoods via a memory-based graph to learn domain-invariant visual features. Ultimately, STaG achieves state-of-the-art performance across three benchmarks under this realistic setting.

SemRAG: Semantic-Guided Sentence-Level Retrieval-Augmented Sign Language Production

Xiao Liu, Shiwei Gan, Yafeng Yin, Peishuo Liu, Bowen Guo, Kuizhuang Liu, Cong Wang, Lei Xie

cs.CV

Sign language production (SLP) aims to generate sign pose sequences that faithfully express spoken-language text while preserving the rhythm and coordination patterns of signing. Common SLP methods learn text-to-pose mappings from training data but often suffer from semantic inaccuracies in long or complex sentences. Retrieval augmentation offers a way to introduce realistic signing priors to provide more explicit guidance for text-to-pose generation. However, existing retrieval-based SLP methods typically operate at the gloss or word level, relying on gloss-pose dictionaries or isolated sign segments that require costly annotations and fail to preserve continuous sentence-level dynamics. To bridge this gap, we propose SemRAG, a semantic-guided sentence-level retrieval-augmented framework that retrieves full-sentence signing templates as priors without constructing gloss/word-level sign dictionaries. While such templates preserve local motion patterns and global rhythm, they also introduce a semantic mismatch problem: irrelevant motions in the retrieved sentence may interfere with the target semantics. To address this issue, we introduce an SLT-guided template semantic localization module that identifies target-relevant temporal regions within the retrieved template while suppressing irrelevant signing content. To adaptively incorporate the template features into the generator, we further introduce layer-specific learnable gates that regulate their contribution across generator layers. Extensive experiments show that SemRAG improves semantic consistency and motion quality over baselines under both back-translation and motion accuracy metrics.

SCAR: Bridging Coarse Retrieval and Fine Localization through Candidate Reranking

Shuhan Dong, Tianyi Shang, Zhenyu Li

cs.CV

Natural-language point-cloud localization typically retrieves candidate map cells and then predicts a position inside each candidate, yet the final output is still determined by retrieval order. A retrieval score measures region-description relevance, not whether the coordinates a region yields are accurate: a relevant region may produce a displaced prediction, and a lower-ranked region may contain a successful one. We study the final selection problem after the candidate pool and Fine predictions are fixed---how to choose the coordinate most likely to localize successfully, and under which conditions the gain holds. We propose SCAR (Spatial Consistency-Aware Reranking). SCAR treats each cell--Fine-coordinate pair as a localization hypothesis, interprets directional relations as functions of position, and reads spatial support at the backbone's actual Fine point (Fine-conditioned Evidence Readout). It organizes this readout with center support, semantic presence, retrieval score, and geometric offsets into a 23-D representation, trains a shared scorer with localization-success labels, and at inference reranks existing hypotheses and returns their original coordinates. Experiments cover four public backbones and a corrected Des4Pos reimplementation across five domains. Excluding one protocol-reference configuration, exploratory TEST point estimates on the remaining 24 configurations show Top-1@5\,m above Original on 23 and Cell R@1 above Original on all 24. On development-set VAL, the Fine readout improves Top-1@5\,m by 5.77 percentage points (pp) over a center surrogate on corrected Des4Pos*KITTI, and the complete scoring scheme improves it by 13.98\,pp over FSR on MambaPlace$\times$nuScenesFull; the latter measures the complete scheme, not learned ranking alone.

Lens: Training-Free Semantic Search for Cross-Tile Object and Scene Retrieval in Satellite Imagery

Ata Akbari Asanjan, Anit Upadhyaya, Jeremy Kravitz, Subash Yeggina

cs.CV

We developed Lens, a training-free system for semantic object and scene search across large satellite mosaics that requires no detector trained for the class of interest. Lens represents each tile with a frozen, self-supervised vision transformer (DINOv3), removes the positional bias in its dense features by projecting them onto the subspace orthogonal to a positional basis estimated once from a constant image, and centers the debiased features on the mean feature computed across the whole area before comparison. Lens forms a query by averaging the centered features of a small neighborhood of patches around a chosen point, or of an entire tile for scene-level search, and matches it against the index by cosine similarity. It stores the centered patch features in a byte-quantized similarity index that supports real-time range search over a mosaic of thousands of patches, and a coarser whole-tile embedding index for scene-level retrieval. A guided upsampling network (AnyUp) runs once per tile to recover pixel-accurate boundaries beyond the native feature grid, and the result is cached so later queries reuse it. We evaluated Lens on the 5,128-tile DFC2020 test set and ran it as an interactive tool on five Sentinel-2 areas spanning agricultural, industrial, and desert terrain; the pipeline is not tied to a specific sensor. On DFC2020 a single click reaches 0.09 to 0.80 pixel F1 across eight land-cover classes at the per-class best threshold, with a mean F1 of 0.39, and it beats a pairwise INSID3 baseline on six of the eight classes at roughly four hundred times lower query cost. Indexing 1.31 million patches takes 169 s on one CPU thread, and a click returns a rendered overlay in 6 to 10 ms.

Deep Bundle Adjustment

Pak Hong Chui, Hector Andrade-Loarca, Daniel Cremers

cs.CV

Structural Self-Teaching for Compositional Generation in Unified Multimodal Models

Jaeyoo Park, Bohyung Han

cs.CV

We propose Structural Self-Teaching (SST), a novel framework that enhances compositional image generation in diffusion-based unified multimodal models (UMMs) by leveraging their internal understanding path as a source of structural supervision. The generation path of UMMs often struggles with dense compositional prompts, leading to missing objects, attribute binding failures, and spatial errors. In contrast, their understanding path localizes the same phrases in images that satisfy these prompts. SST converts this phrase-level grounding into training-time structural signals for the generation path. Specifically, we extract phrase-conditioned support masks from internal attention maps derived from the understanding path and employ them through two mechanisms. Phrase-local contrastive alignment uses these masks as pooling regions and aligns each generated phrase region with the same region of the target image. A structural scaffold builds a phrase-level layout from the same masks and modulates an intermediate spatial state of the generation path during training. The scaffold is phased out during training and removed at inference. SST requires no external structural annotations for training and no additional input at test time. Extensive experiments across multiple UMMs demonstrate that SST improves compositional fidelity over LoRA fine-tuning on the same data, with no inference-time cost. Project page is available at https://bellos1203.github.io/sst

Unlocking Cross-Attention in Label-Scarce Regimes: A Weakly Supervised Approach for Video Semantic Segmentation

Andrea Marelli, Alessandro Ardenghi, Giacomo Boracchi, Luca Magri

cs.CV, figures,, 7, tables

Weakly Supervised Video Semantic Segmentation (WS-VSS) aims to learn temporally consistent semantic object masks in videos using only video-level labels. While fully supervised models successfully learn temporal relationships by means of cross-attention, training these temporal weights under weak supervision is unstable and even pushes the model below its single-frame baseline. Therefore, existing WS-VSS methods exploit temporal consistency by relying on external priors, such as optical flow. Unfortunately these cues are non-semantic, computationally expensive and noisy, degrading the supervisory signal. Instead of supervising attention weights directly or using external priors, we propose MemCAM, a WS-VSS transformer framework that learns temporal cross-attention directly through feature-space self-supervision. MemCAM introduces a CAM Propagation Algorithm that synthesizes a supervisory signal by propagating past CAMs according to patch-level feature similarities in the latent space. Specifically, we constrain their induced CAMs through a consistency objective between predicted and propagated maps, stabilizing temporal attention learning. Experiments on the YouTube-Objects v2.2 and WS$^2$ datasets demonstrate that MemCAM consistently outperforms the temporal localization and segmentation performance of WS-VSS alternatives, showing that cross-attention can be effectively trained in label-scarce video settings through internal self-supervision.

DIRT: Depth-completed Image-based Rendering using Two-band Blending for Live Novel-view Synthesis

Mathias Harrer, Laura Fink, Noah Lewis, Paul Himmler, Tim Weyrich

cs.CV, cs.GR, material

State-of-the-art novel-view synthesis (NVS) methods commonly require per-scene optimization before rendering, limiting their applicability in live settings. Live or ad-hoc NVS, however, demands low-latency rendering during capture and strong generalization to new scenes. Some previous work tried to address this task by adapting training- and optimization-based methods, trading quality for low latency. We introduce a live NVS approach following traditional image-based rendering that operates on a posed set of depth sensor observations and images. Our method leverages explicit, sparse point-based geometry obtained from active sensors, resulting in a sparsely filled depth buffer that is completed in the (novel) target view's screen-space. Using this proxy, a posed set of images is projected into the target frame before fusing the resulting observations through a frequency-aware two-band blending strategy. The pipeline is entirely non-neural and optimization-free, requiring no training data or scene-specific optimization. As a result, our approach reduces the delay between capture and novel-view rendering and does not rely on favorable network generalization. We validate the method's applicability to heterogeneous sensor setups - including handheld RGB-D cameras, automotive LiDAR, and thermal cameras - as well as challenging scenarios, such as low light captures. Our experiments demonstrate that the approach outperforms prior methods in live reconstruction scenarios, while maintaining competitive performance in conventional NVS benchmarks.

MaskSCD: Bitemporal Mask Classification for Semantic Change Detection in Remote Sensing Images

Weikang Yu, Pedram Ghamisi

cs.CV

Semantic change detection (SCD) identifies where the land surface has changed and which land-cover transition has taken place. Most deep SCD networks predict the change map and the land-cover map of each date with separate per-pixel decoders. These outputs need not agree, so a pixel can be labeled as changed although it has the same class at both dates, and per-pixel decisions fragment objects. We present MaskSCD, which formulates SCD as the mask classification of a bitemporal label-pair image. Each bitemporal segment, a region of constant land-cover class pair, is predicted as one mask shared by both dates together with one class per date, and the change map is derived from the predicted class pair. This formulation keeps the change and semantic maps consistent and the label space linear in the number of classes. A bitemporal masked-attention transformer decoder implements it by reading each segment in both images and relating the two readings, and a bitemporal fusion pyramid provides per-date and change-aware features. MaskSCD is evaluated on SECOND, Landsat-SCD and Hi-UCD, which span 0.1 to 30 m resolution and four to nine land-cover classes, against fourteen recent SCD models re-trained under one protocol. MaskSCD achieves the best separated kappa, semantic change $F_1$ and transition $F_1$ on all three datasets and exceeds the strongest competitor by about five points in mean separated kappa. It produces no contradictory change pixels, which account for nearly 30% of the predicted change of some multi-task networks. The code is available at https://github.com/EricYu97/MaskSCD.

Adaptable Watermarking: Transforming Image Watermarking Models via Lightweight Adapters

Mehrdad Saberi, Vishal Asnani, Shruti Agarwal, Soheil Feizi, John Collomosse

cs.CV, cs.CR, cs.LG

We explore the adaptability of neural image watermarking models; the extent to which one watermarking method can be transformed into another through lightweight adaptation. We introduce a framework that enables encoder-decoder watermarking architectures to become modular and upgradable, supporting multiple watermarking styles without re-training full models. Adaptation is performed by attaching a compact parameter-efficient adapter and fine-tuning under a multi-domain residual alignment loss (spatial, gradient, and frequency) while preserving bit accuracy via a secret-loss term. We evaluate this approach across a suite of recent watermarking models, including TrustMark, InvisMark, RoSteALS, HiDDeN, and WAM, demonstrating that adapted encoders closely reproduce target residuals and that adapted decoders recover messages with near-optimal accuracy under standard augmentations and distortions. We show that cross-model adaptation, with applications in interoperable and upgradable watermarking, can be achieved with as few as 1-2M trainable parameters per component (e.g., the 2M decoder adapter used in our multi-decoder inference pipeline).

AI-Based Multi-View Echocardiographic Image Synthesis using Dynamic Shape Control

Jo\~ao Freitas, Anca Balinisteanu, Ahmed Youssef, Vitor Hugo Pereira, Jan D'hooge, Sandro Queir\'os

cs.CV, cs.AI, the, 2026, IEEE, International, Ultrasonics, Symposium, (IUS, 2026)

Although automated methods have improved echocardiographic workflows, progress remains constrained by the need for large, well-annotated datasets. As the development of clinical databases is often limited by ethical and logistical challenges, synthetic data generation has emerged as a promising alternative. However, existing approaches commonly rely on left ventricle shape-based supervision or are tailored to specific cardiac views, limiting the generation of additional standard and unconventional imaging planes and the preservation of multi-view consistency across cardiac chambers. To address these limitations, we propose a novel framework for shape-controlled synthesis of multi-view echocardiographic sequences. First, multi-view cross-sections are extracted from dynamic whole-heart meshes. Ultrasound-like appearance is then introduced through a two-stage AI-based pipeline. In Stage 1, an unpaired GAN-diffusion model translates these cross-sections into realistic echocardiographic frames. As this translation is performed independently at the frame level, Stage 2 employs masked video reconstruction to improve temporal coherence and cardiac motion. Evaluation demonstrated high frame-level realism for both stages, while the integration of Stage 2 substantially improved video-level motion quality and temporal consistency. Overall, the proposed framework enables the generation of realistic multi-view echocardiographic sequences from diverse cardiac models, providing a scalable source of annotated data for dataset augmentation and the development of multi-view cardiac assessment methods.

CoPre-MoE: Consolidating and Preserving MoE for Multimodal Continual Instruction Tuning

Tao Hu, Ling-Xiang Zhang, Da-Wei Zhou

cs.CV, cs.AI, cs.LG

Multimodal continual instruction tuning requires multimodal large language models to sequentially acquire new capabilities without catastrophic forgetting. Mixture-of-experts (MoE) architectures offer a promising way to mitigate task interference through expert specialization and combination. However, while existing MoE-based methods avoid overwriting shared parameters by allocating separate experts for each task, this progressive expansion incurs severe cross-task redundancy and linear parameter growth. Specifically, experts across tasks often learn overlapping functions alongside task-specific capabilities. For instance, ScienceQA and TextVQA both locate relevant image regions. To curb parameter redundancy without inducing forgetting, we propose Consolidating and Preserving MoE (CoPre-MoE). For each incoming task, CoPre-MoE first learns task-specific MoE to capture specialized capabilities. To eliminate redundancy and reduce parameter growth across tasks, we design a shared-and-private expert architecture. Specifically, we first evaluate functional overlap to selectively consolidate overlapping experts across tasks into a fixed-capacity generic expert pool. We then factorize the remaining task-specific experts into shared projection bases and compact task-specific cores within a private expert pool. To mitigate forgetting, we construct the router by pairing each expert with learnable retrieval keys, where historical keys remain frozen during continual updates while newly added keys are regularized with an orthogonality penalty. At inference, input-dependent routing dynamically combines both pools without test-time task labels. Extensive experiments demonstrate that CoPre-MoE achieves superior performance while substantially reducing parameter overhead.

Do Underwater Image Quality Metrics Agree with Human Perception? A Benchmark with Human Choices, Simple Edits and Generative Drift

Rishii Kumar Singh, Abhinav Rajput, Dinesh Kumar Vishwakarma

cs.CV, eess.IV, appendix

Underwater image enhancement is challenging to evaluate due to the lack of clean reference images, so researchers rely on no-reference image quality assessment (NR-IQA) metrics. However, it remains unclear whether these metrics agree with human judgments. We evaluate 73 handcrafted underwater, learned underwater, vision-language and general-purpose metrics against human preferences on five references: four published opinion-score sets and a new paired-comparison study. For each metric, we measure how often it selects the same image as people when comparing versions of the same scene. We further test whether simple image edits and small adversarial perturbations raise metric scores above the enhancement people preferred, and whether scores decrease as repeated generative edits add noise. Our results show that no metric consistently matches human preferences. The best worst-case Cohen's kappa over the five references is only 0.23 (Q-Align), while two raters in our study agree with a median kappa of 0.41. The widely used UCIQE often favors edits that people reject. On a Nano Banana Pro replica chain that should leave the image unchanged, only 22 of 73 scores decrease at every checkpoint, while noise grows by 2.5 times. We also introduce TrustUIQA, a benchmark that combines the human references, our study choices, the edited, attacked and generated images, and the scores of every metric on every image. The code is available at https://github.com/TrustUIQA/TrustUIQA and TrustUIQA at https://huggingface.co/datasets/TrustUIQA/TrustUIQA.

Toward On-Device Markerless 6DoF Tracking of a Physical Manipulative on a Mixed-Reality Headset: A Preliminary Study

Guangxi Feng, Philip Yasskin, Wei Yan

cs.CV, cs.HC

AR-Classroom uses physical models and virtual overlays to help learners explore spatial transformations. To align these overlays on a standalone headset, we developed a markerless six-degree-of-freedom(6DoF) tracker for a LEGO airplane on the Meta Quest 3. A compact YOLO model trained on synthetic Unity renders predicts 20 keypoints from dynamic RGB crops resized to 128 by 128 pixels. A perspective-n-point solver estimates the airplane's pose from these predictions and the corresponding 3D landmarks. In a paired evaluation on 1,000 synthetic validation images, cropping guided by ground-truth boxes increased the detection rate on annotated images from 32.5 to 46.2 percent. Mean keypoint F1 increased from 42.9 to 44.6 percent, with lower keypoint precision in the cropped view. In preliminary headset tests, 2 Hz was the highest tested tracking target at which the application maintained approximately 72 frames per second. A headset demonstration illustrates virtual overlay alignment with the physical airplane before and after a manual rotation. Quantitative 6DoF pose accuracy on real headset footage will be measured in future work.

MMFlash: Faster Speculative Decoding for Vision-Language Models

Sicheng Feng, Zigeng Chen, Song Wang, Gongfan Fang, Xinchao Wang

cs.CV

Speculative decoding accelerates generation by letting a lightweight drafter propose tokens for parallel verification by a target model. Diffusion drafters such as DFlash propose an entire token block in a single forward pass, but mainly focus on text-only tasks, leaving multimodal drafting underexplored. To study this setting, we first introduce a metric of token-level visual dependence based on the KL divergence between next-token distributions with and without visual input. We find that acceptance rates decrease from 46% in the lowest visual-dependence quartile to 22% in the highest, with this disparity persisting after multimodal training. Motivated by this observation, we propose MMFlash, a simple and effective framework that adaptively upweights visually dependent tokens that the drafter predicts poorly. For long video inputs, MMFlash further employs sparse attention to reduce redundant attention computation and improve drafting quality. Across various multimodal benchmarks, MMFlash achieves lossless decoding speedups of up to $3.58\times$, outperforming EAGLE-3 and DFlash by up to 195.4% and 17.1%, respectively.

StreamEmbed: Real-Time Indexing from Pixels for Screenshot Retrieval

Dayoon Ko, Yichuan Wang, Soyeong Jeong, Joseph E. Gonzalez, Gunhee Kim, Sewon Min

cs.CV, cs.IR, cs.LG

Screen history can serve as searchable visual memory for people and agents, but unlike an offline corpus, it must be encoded and indexed as each screenshot arrives. OCR-based indexing is fast but loses visual information; visual encoding takes over two seconds. Yet screens change incrementally: most pixels repeat within and across screenshots. This raises a question: do visual encoders need to recompute their representations from scratch for every new screenshot? We present StreamEmbed, a system for incremental visual embedding that exploits this redundancy. Guided solely by pixel differences, it combines spatial token pruning, which skips blocks that repeat a neighbor, with temporal KV reuse, which reuses cached keys and values for blocks unchanged across screenshots. A small learned adapter compensates for pruning and reuse to preserve fidelity to full-screenshot embeddings. On ScreenMemEval, our benchmark of 10,426 questions over 184 hours of screen histories from two humans and a computer-use agent, StreamEmbed sets a new accuracy-latency Pareto frontier on an M2 Pro GPU: it encodes up to 3.7x faster than full-screenshot encoding without sacrificing accuracy and improves accuracy over OCR-based indexing by up to 6.3 Recall@10 points at matched latency. A memory-efficient vector index stores a simulated year of screenshots in 0.13 GB of RAM. Together, these results make real-time indexing of screen history in its native visual form practical, laying a foundation for fully local visual assistants.

Box-conditional Hypothesis Testing for Out-of-Distribution Object Detection

Donghoon Lee, Jusung Park, Shinjin Kang

cs.CV

Detecting out-of-distribution (OOD) objects is essential for ensuring the reliability and safety of object detectors in real-world deployment. We treat the decision on a detected box as a hypothesis test with the null hypothesis that the box covers an object of an in-distribution (ID) class. The null distribution changes with the size of the box and its contact with the image border, and the detector observes both. The test can therefore rank a box only against ID boxes of the same size and border contact, which gives a distribution-free bound on the false-alarm rate for each of them and uncovers the separation that one null distribution hides. The power of the test also rests on the layer it reads. Layers built only for the ID classes have no reason to keep what sets an unknown object apart, so the test reads the features before them. We propose \textbf{PLUM}, a training-free score that pools the last backbone stage inside the box and ranks its distance to the ID classes among ID boxes of the same size and border contact. On the standard benchmark, PLUM reaches the highest AUROC among the compared methods without external models, with 91.5 and 94.5 on PASCAL-VOC and 95.1 and 97.5 on BDD100K. Code: https://github.com/DDOZMI/PLUM

Understanding Contextual Bias in Agricultural CNNs Through Hidden-Neuron Concept Induction

Abhilekha Dalal, Avishek Das, Samatha Ereshi Akkamahadevi

cs.CV, cs.LG

Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) can classify images using background-dominated patches that contain little or none of the target object, showing that they exploit contextual information. Previous studies detected this behavior through classification accuracy but say little about what CNN hidden layers represent. We investigate whether Concept Induction can reveal interpretable visual properties associated with hidden-neuron activations on contextual image patches. Using five agricultural datasets with high, moderate, and low contextual bias, we analyze four CNN architectures at three network depths and five patch locations. Quantitative image descriptors of each patch are encoded in a knowledge graph and used as background knowledge for Concept Induction, which derives logical expressions characterizing neuron activation. These expressions are tested on held-out images using the Mann-Whitney U test and Cliff's delta. Of 48,013 tested neuron-feature pairs, 13,164 (27.4%) were validated. Validation was most frequent at intermediate depths (mean 40.2% at mid layers versus 12.0% at deep layers), and no patch location dominated across datasets. Among the tested descriptors, edge, Gabor, and Tamura texture features had the highest validation rates. Stronger contextual bias did not correspond to higher neuron-feature validation rates: moderately biased Sugarcane had the highest rate (40.5%) and highly biased Cassava the lowest (19.2%). These results suggest that behavioral context dependence and the semantic interpretability of hidden neurons capture different aspects of CNN behavior, and that Concept Induction can complement patch-based bias detection.

JVT: Just Video Transformer makes pixel-space video diffusion simple

Jianjun Zhou, Hua Geng, Haoyu Guo

cs.CV

Direct diffusion modeling of high-dimensional raw video data is challenging, and existing pixel-space methods often rely on specialized architectures. We introduce JVT (Just Video Transformer), a simple large-patch DiT-style Transformer for pixel-space video diffusion, without a pretrained autoencoder, auxiliary detailers, cascaded stages, or U-Net-style skip connections. We systematically examine video patch embeddings, register tokens, and adaptive layer normalization to identify design choices that balance generation quality and computational efficiency. The resulting JVT design achieves generation quality competitive with latent diffusion baselines in both class-conditioned and unconditional settings. The pixel-space formulation of JVT also naturally supports joint generation of pixel-aligned modalities. We demonstrate this capability through camera-conditioned RGB-disparity generation, which improves camera following relative to RGB-only baselines and yields explicit scene geometry without an additional reconstruction model at inference. These results highlight the potential of simple DiT-style Transformers for pixel-space video diffusion, and we hope our findings offer useful insights and practical guidance for future research.

Restoring Diversity in Distilled Image and Video Generators by Probing Activations

Furkan Horoz, Ozgur Kara, Kaan Oktay, Adil Kaan Akan, Atakan Kara, Anand Bhattad, James Matthew Rehg

cs.CV

Distilling diffusion models into few-step student models substantially reduces sampling latency, but induces severe diversity collapse: samples generated from different random seeds show minimal variation. In this work, we investigate the root cause of this failure by probing the internal representations of student models relative to their teachers. We find that distilled students maintain total activation variance comparable to their teachers, but concentrate it into substantially fewer directions. This directional collapse is localized to an identifiable intermediate block at the initial denoising step. Consequently, diversity collapse stems from a misallocation of activation variance rather than an inherent loss of generative capacity. Guided by this finding, we propose ReDiverse to reinstate the missing variance directions. At training time, ReDiverse-FT fine-tunes a lightweight adapter that regresses student activations onto the teacher's, reviving the teacher's feature variance characteristic. At inference time, ReDiverse-TF introduces a training-free mechanism that steers activations along precomputed teacher principal directions. Across image and video distillation benchmarks, ReDiverse consistently restores sample diversity while preserving generation quality, further supported by the user study.

BAL: Bidirectional Autoregression in Latent Space for Learning Human Movement Representations

Genki Kinoshita, Ko Nishino

cs.CV, cs.LG, https://github.com/kyotovision-public/bal

Learning semantically meaningful human movement representations from 3D pose sequences is essential for human behavior modeling. Existing self-supervised methods derive supervision from artificial assumptions, e.g., invariance to augmentation within a certain range or recoverability of masked regions at a certain temporal scale, introducing heuristic hyperparameters that make the learned semantics sensitive to their settings. We propose BAL, a self-supervised framework that instead exploits supervision intrinsic to human movement: the natural ordering structure of everyday behavior. BAL autoregressively predicts in a semantically abstract latent space, analogous to how humans anticipate others' actions rather than their precise joint configurations. Bidirectional autoregressive prediction, both forward and backward in time, fully leverages these ordering constraints while enabling representation extraction using both past and future context at inference. To suppress representation collapse, BAL decomposes each prediction target into a global component capturing sequence-level semantics and a local component capturing context-independent movement. We prove that the local targets retain nontrivial angular diversity unless the representations are completely collapsed, preserving a meaningful training signal. Experiments on large-scale datasets demonstrate that BAL's representations are highly effective for motion captioning, forecasting, and interpolation, outperforming baselines on most evaluation metrics.

Alignment with Reflect-Reasoning: Learning How To Follow Principles

Henry Bell, Caroline Zhang, Dhaval Potdar, Mohammed Mobasserul Haque, Brandon Fain

cs.LG

The appropriate governing norms and principles for the alignment of Large Language Models (LLMs) often depend on cultural context and deployment application. However, most alignment techniques focus on training models to conform to human preferences or constitutional principles specified at training time and subsequently fixed. In practice, many deployers rely on system prompting to implement contextual norms and guardrails at inference time. Prior work has shown that post-generation reflective reasoning with critique and revision can improve conformance compared to simple system prompting, but such methods are limited to the reasoning abilities inherent in the trained model. We introduce Reflect-Reasoning, a training method that teaches models how to reason about alignment to principles, rather than simply which principles to follow. Across five models, Reflect-Reasoning consistently outperforms both system prompting and explicit prompting strategies for in-context reflective reasoning. These improvements extend and generalize to entirely out-of-distribution domains of medical and financial principles. We additionally demonstrate that Reflect-Reasoning reduces the occurrence of rare but serious principle violations. Finally, we show that Reflect-Reasoning does not erode the safety engineering of a model and is robust against jailbreaking attempts using adversarial constitutions. Overall, our results demonstrate that Reflect-Reasoning is a scalable method for training constitutional alignment as a generalizable reasoning capability, enabling models to adapt to novel principles and produce transparent reasoning traces without additional fine-tuning.

WETW: Learning Pair-Specific Weighted Euclidean Distances for Efficient DTW Search

Christos Panourgias, Qitong Wang, George Tzagkarakis, Themis Palpanas

cs.LG, cs.AI

Dynamic Time Warping (DTW) is the standard distance for similarity search over time series that are misaligned in time, but its cost is quadratic in the series length and lower bounds often prune few candidates, so search over large collections of long series is slow. Learned approximations embed the series to make comparisons cheap, yet compare every pair with the same fixed rule, usually the Euclidean distance, although DTW aligns each pair differently. We observe that, once a pair is aligned, DTW is a Euclidean distance over coordinates selected for that pair. Building on this view, we propose Weighted Euclidean Time Warping (WETW), a learned distance that adapts the comparison of fixed-length embeddings to each pair. A small symmetric network predicts one weight per embedding di- mension and pair without computing an alignment, which halves the error of the best static distance on the same embeddings. The embeddings come from the Weighted Euclidean SEries Encoder (WESEE), whose multi-scale Temporal Feature Extractor keeps the timing information that global pooling removes and raises recall@1 from 0.90 to 0.95, although WETW works with any encoder. We also show that fitting DTW distances is not enough for search, as regression alone misorders the closest neighbours, and that ranking regularization raises recall@1 from 0.79 to 0.95. Our filter-and-verify pipeline, WETW-FV, uses WETW to build a shortlist and exact DTW to check it, so the search is approximate but every returned distance is exact. Over eight datasets, WETW-FV is 11.4x faster than the fastest exact method among the UCR Suite, lower bounds and MESSI-DTW, at a mean recall@10 of 0.93, while the best learned baseline reaches 0.59 at the same speed. On 48K-point series, WETW- FV remains 40x faster than the UCR Suite at a recall@10 of 0.91.

Classification with Large-Step Gradient Descent: Unifying Self-Stabilization and Convergence Rates

Salma Tarmoun, Lachlan Ewen MacDonald, Konstantinos Emmanouilidis, Ziqing Xu, Ren\'e Vidal

cs.LG, math.OC

Neural networks are often trained with gradient descent (GD) using step sizes that exceed classical stability thresholds, a regime where standard convergence theory no longer applies. Despite exhibiting non-monotonic loss and oscillatory dynamics, this regime frequently leads to faster convergence and better generalization. Existing theoretical perspectives offer complementary but incomplete insights: one identifies a self-stabilization mechanism that drives GD toward flat regions but does not yield convergence rates; another derives rigorous rates for specific losses but does not explain the underlying stabilization mechanism; and a third unifies these views for overparameterized least squares, but relies on the existence of a minimizer manifold, which is absent in classification settings. In this work, we extend this unified geometric perspective to exponential-tail losses, including logistic and cross-entropy losses in separable linear classification, where the loss infimum is not attained at any finite parameter. We identify a curvature-defined reference subspace that replaces the role of the minimizer manifold, yielding a coupled dynamical system in which orthogonal oscillations are damped by parallel progress along directions of decreasing sharpness. Under regularity conditions, which we verify for logistic and multiclass cross-entropy losses on symmetric data models, we derive explicit bounds for the iterates, not just the loss, covering both the unstable and stable regimes, and revealing a three-phase damping structure in the latter. Our analysis further uncovers a transient implicit bias induced by large step sizes: although GD converges asymptotically to the max-margin direction, large steps can leave persistent orthogonal residuals and, in certain geometries, amplify them, affecting finite-time alignment with the max-margin direction.

Unfinished-Bench: Measuring Rule Violations by Persistent Agents in Realistic Work Environments

Seunghyun Lee, Jaehui Hwang, Dongyoon Han, Sangdoo Yun

cs.LG, cs.CL

AI agents are increasingly equipped with persistence toward task completion to run delegated work autonomously for long periods, under many unstated constraints from internal documents and other people's authority. When completion requires violating a rule, the agent must decide alone, yet benchmarks of instruction-rule conflicts state the rule in the prompt or keep it hidden and do not evaluate persistence. We present Unfinished-Bench, a benchmark that gives models persistence in autonomous runs without a user, in realistic work environments with embedded rules. In each of its 97 scenarios across 7 organizations and 7 risk categories, the goal and the rule cannot both hold. We find that persistence raised violations of a rule in an internal document from 32.2% to 43.6%, five times the 8.5% for the same rule stated by the user. Of the document-rule violations, 98.3% occurred after the rule was displayed, and in 89% of a judged sample the agent first wrote down the rule. Violations of rules placed in the environment without user oversight can lead to serious incidents, so persistence-driven risks need to be evaluated. Benchmark documentation: https://francis21c.github.io/unfinished-bench/

Diffusion Tree Sampling for Inference Time Adaptation of Crystal Generative Models

Daniel T. Levy, Vineet Jain, Tara Akhound-Sadegh, S\'ekou-Oumar Kaba, Siamak Ravanbakhsh

cs.LG, cond-mat.mtrl-sci, cs.AI

Diffusion-based generative models have become strong priors over plausible crystal structures, but turning that prior into promising candidates requires steering toward several conflicting properties at once. Fine-tuning is expensive and produces a new model for every new objective, while differentiable guidance is not applicable to many real-world tasks. We instead adapt these generative models at inference time using Diffusion Tree Sampling (DTS), a Monte Carlo tree search procedure over the denoising trajectory. We make two technical contributions on top of DTS that are particularly relevant for AI-for-science applications. First, we show how to use existing property-conditional generative models (e.g., models fine-tuned for classifier-free guidance) as guided proposals inside DTS, without distorting the target distribution. Second, we extend DTS to multi-objective sampling, so that a single search produces samples spanning an entire Pareto front. We instantiate both adaptations on de novo crystal generation: improving the stability of crystals generated by different models, using pretrained conditional models to generate crystals belonging to rare space groups or with high bulk modulus, and generating crystals along a Pareto front of high dielectric constant and high bandgap materials.
