Klaus Weinbauer, Peter F. Stadler, Thomas G\"artner, Sagar Malhotra

cs.LG

We formally characterize the conditions under which sufficient explanations exist for classifiers on structured data such as graphs and strings. We focus on post-hoc explainers that select parts of the input as sufficient explanations. An explanation is sufficient if the selected part alone ensures the predicted label. Sufficient explanations have been widely adopted across data modalities, including graphs, strings, and images. However, a unified formal analysis of sufficient explanations remains missing. We bridge this gap with four main contributions: (i) We define a general explanation formalism consisting of an instance space, a classifier, and a select relation. The select relation specifies which potential sub-instances or feature subsets can be selected as explanations from each instance; (ii) We show that the existence of sufficient explainers is fully characterized by the interplay between the classifier and the select relation; (iii) We extend this analysis to practically relevant variants of sufficiency, including probabilistic sufficiency and deletion sufficiency; (iv) Based on the formal analysis, we devise statistics that bound the maximum achievable sufficiency for a given task. We evaluate these statistics on language, protein, and molecular classification datasets and find that full sufficiency is unattainable on most of them. Since these bounds hold for every explainer, they provide a task-level reference against which any explainer's sufficiency can be judged in absolute terms.

MarathonTS: a benchmark of long synthetic time series from window assembly and long-series generators

Ilyas Varshavskiy, Sergey Shulgin, Shuhrat Khalilbekov, Bonu Boboeva, Azizjon Azimi

cs.LG

Many applications of synthetic time series require a series that is much longer than the windows on which a generative model is trained. Such a series can be assembled from a pool of short generated windows, which requires deciding which windows are taken, in what order, and how their seams are processed, or it can be produced directly by a long-series generator. This study presents MarathonTS, to our knowledge the first open benchmark that compares both families under one protocol. Every method produces a series of the same length on 9 open hourly datasets with 10 seeds. The series is evaluated by its fidelity to the real data (83 metrics in 12 groups, against three real references: train, validation and an out-of-time split), by its utility (models are trained on the synthetic series only and tested on real data, TSTR against TRTR, in 14 classification and regression tasks), by its memorisation of the training data and by its computational cost. The statistical analysis is carried out as a separate stage that reads the published tables and gives the rankings of every method in every slice, the answers to the research questions with tests and uncertainty, and the results of a pre-specified confirmatory family. In the main run, the methods differ in both confirmatory cells; the choice of windows matters more than the processing of their seams, which does not improve the series, and no method trains the downstream models as well as the real series. The top tier of the leaderboard is reproduced on other hardware, whereas the exact places within it are not. For the reproducibility of the study, the code, the data hashes and all results are publicly available (links below).

T2ST: Data-Efficient Spatio-Temporal Foundation Models via Temporal Data and Knowledge Transfer

Yue Xu, Kaiyun Wei, Wenying Duan, Zichen Xu, Shuai Ma, Xiaoxi He

cs.LG, cs.AI

Spatio-temporal foundation models (STFMs) require large-scale training corpora, yet synchronized observations and spatial metadata are costly to acquire and curate, limiting the availability of suitable data. By contrast, time-series data are abundant and easier to collect. Since observations at each location form a time series, this motivates a natural question: can abundant time-series data enhance STFMs when spatio-temporal observations are scarce? To bridge this gap, we propose T2ST, a data-efficient training framework that augments limited spatio-temporal observations with selected auxiliary time series and transfers knowledge from a frozen time-series foundation model. Our key insight is that temporal pattern learning can benefit from abundant external time series, while spatial dependency learning remains grounded in real spatio-temporal observations. Guided by this insight, T2ST retrieves matched sequences to reinforce sparsely observed temporal patterns and selects reliable complementary sequences to broaden temporal coverage. Temporal relation distillation aligns relationships among historical patches, while multi-scale prediction distillation transfers forecasting behavior at fine and coarse temporal resolutions, enabling knowledge transfer across heterogeneous architectures. Using only 10\% of the original spatio-temporal data, T2ST enables STFMs to achieve overall forecasting performance competitive with or superior to their full-data counterparts across six long-term and twelve short-term forecasting datasets. These results establish a new paradigm for data-efficient training of spatio-temporal foundation models. Code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/T2ST-65F3/.

NeoResearch: Literature- and Experience-Guided Autonomous Time Series Model Discovery

Ze Guo, Mingyue Cheng, Xiaoyu Tao, Shuo Yu, Shangtong Ye

cs.LG

Developing effective forecasting models for diverse time-series domains requires substantial expert knowledge, evidence from scientific literature on the web, and iterative experimentation. However, translating this knowledge and research experience into model designs requires preserving source context and testing applicability under task-specific assumptions. In this paper, we propose NeoResearch, a literature- and experience-guided autonomous research framework that leverages large language models (LLMs) as researchers to discover lightweight, domain-specific forecasting models. Specifically, NeoResearch establishes an expert-inspired dual-loop research workflow, coupling hypothesis-driven architecture exploration with iterative training and hyperparameter optimization through experimental feedback. To enable knowledge-grounded discovery, the agent autonomously mines scientific literature from the Web, extracting modeling mechanisms, empirical evidence, and applicability conditions to formulate task-grounded, testable architecture hypotheses. Furthermore, a hierarchical experience memory links literature findings to experimental outcomes and consolidates conditional lessons, guiding architecture discovery and training optimization while supporting reuse across forecasting tasks. Experiments on ten datasets show competitive performance and support dual loops, literature extraction, and memory reuse. Our code is available.

Small Is Not Dead: Distance-Aware Cluster Revival for e-means

Eyal Weiss

cs.LG, stat.ML

e-means (Bessa et al., 2026) is a recent mini-batch k-means algorithm, now part of the FAISS library, that reaches the error of Lloyd's algorithm about an order of magnitude faster on large embedding datasets. Every five mini-batches, it removes every cluster whose running point count falls below a tenth of the average and reuses its centre to split a large cluster. In the authors' ablation, removing this mechanism, called dead-cluster revival, raises the error more than removing any other component added to the moving-average update. The rule, however, considers only cluster size: it cannot distinguish a cluster that is small because it is redundant from one that covers a rare, isolated group of points, and on data with such groups it repeatedly removes centres whose removal is costly. We add a second condition, based on the distance between centres: a small cluster is removed only if its centre is close to another centre, relative to a running estimate of the mean squared error. When a centre equals the mean of its points, this squared distance is an upper bound on the average increase in squared error, per point of the cluster, caused by removing the centre. The condition costs one nearest-centre search for the small clusters every five mini-batches and adds no measurable running time. On the e-means paper's embedding benchmarks, it never prevents a removal, so the results are identical to those of FAISS. On datasets with small isolated groups not used during development, it reduces the test error after two epochs by roughly 10% to 80% relative to count-based revival, and it preserves the advantage of k-means++ initialization; on the other evaluation datasets, it stays within about 1% of the better of revival enabled and disabled. We also report two failure modes that the analysis predicts. This is a CPU proof of concept; GPU implementations are left to future work.

Neural Classifiers as Error-Correcting Codes

Sergey Salishev, Anton Makarov, Oleg Granichin, Renata Avros, Zeev Volkovich

cs.LG, cs.IT, math.IT, Sciences, 766, (2027), 124394

A unified framework is proposed that characterizes supervised deep classification as the decoding of error-correcting codes in the Shannon sense. Cross-entropy optimization is shown to align softmax classifiers with maximum-likelihood decoding, where logits map to Hamming distances to class-specific codewords. This correspondence explains the emergence of standard divergence objectives (e.g., Kullback-Leibler, Jeffreys) and provides a geometric basis for the stability of decision boundaries. Furthermore, the geometries of Neural Collapse and LeJEPA are identified as optimal constellations for decoding under isotropic noise. Unlike classical Error-Correcting Output Codes, deep networks are shown to learn effective codebooks in the feature space implicitly. This insight is leveraged to derive novel criteria for lossless quantization, improved knowledge distillation, and architectural regularization based on code redundancy. Finally, this perspective is extended to self-supervised encoders, offering a cohesive explanation for the geometry of latent representations in modern neural models.

ParetoM$^3$: Learning on the Pareto Set under Preference Guidance via Min-Max-Min Optimization

Pei Tang, Songtao Lu

cs.LG, cs.AI, math.OC, Processing, Systems, (NeurIPS, 2026)

Learning across multiple inherently conflicting objectives requires selecting desirable trade-offs from the Pareto set. In this work, we study preference-guided multi-objective optimization, where a predefined preference function is minimized over the weak Pareto set of multiple nonconvex objectives. Leveraging a merit function that vanishes on the weak Pareto set, we propose a penalty-based min-max-min formulation, termed the M$^3$ problem, which unifies preference optimization and the enforcement of weak Pareto optimality in a single objective. We further develop ParetoM$^3$, a single-loop first-order algorithm for computing stationary solutions of the resulting M$^3$ problem. Under a local Kurdyka-Lojasiewicz assumption, we prove that ParetoM$^3$ finds an $\epsilon$-stationary point of the M$^3$ problem that is also approximately weakly Pareto optimal for the original multi-objective problem when the penalty parameter is sufficiently large, with explicit non-asymptotic convergence rates. We also show that this stationarity notion recovers existing optimality criteria, including approximate preference stationarity and approximate Karush-Kuhn-Tucker conditions, under the same assumptions used in prior works. Experiments on a synthetic benchmark, image classification, regression, and math reasoning demonstrate that ParetoM$^3$ reliably attains preferred weak Pareto optimal solutions while achieving strong practical performance. Code is available at https://github.com/tpgoodboy/ParetoM3.

Right Token, Right Time: From Per-Token Gating to Trajectory-Aware Activation Steering

Nima Eshraghi, Lovedeep Gondara, Sagarika Suresh, Xiaotong Xu, Jithin Pradeep, Fanny Chevalier

cs.LG, cs.AI

Activation steering via sparse autoencoders (SAEs) enables behavioral control of large language models without task-specific fine-tuning. Yet, standard methods apply the steering signal at every generated token, displacing representations from the distribution the model was trained on and eroding generation quality. We show that \emph{when} along a generation the intervention is applied is a decisive axis of control. We cast steering as a per-token allocation problem under a fixed budget of steerable tokens, so that methods differ only in which tokens they select, making allocation the sole variable. Within this framework we introduce two gates. The first, Contrastive Feature Projection (CFP), is memoryless and selects the tokens least aligned with the target behavior through a sparse, interpretable feature projection. The second, Closed-Loop Adaptive Bernoulli Steering (CLABS), is trajectory aware. It treats allocation as closed-loop feedback control, tracking the alignment a generation has accumulated so far and spending budget where the trajectory falls behind. On a linear steering-response model we derive an explicit gain region that guarantees closed-loop stability of CLABS and show that its integral term reduces the steady-state tracking offset. Empirically, CFP and CLABS achieve the largest behavioral shift among budgeted gates at matched budgets, with CLABS strongest under the tight budgets where allocation matters most, while best preserving fluency.

Clean Tickets, Wrong Orders: Catching Silent Errors with a Language Model in Your Browser Tab

Umar Ahmed, Maxim Dunaev, Mohammad Nour Al Awad, Sergey Ivanov, Ivan Khodnenko

cs.LG, cs.CL, cs.HC, Companion, paper:, arXiv:2611.17169

A small language model that turns a typed order into structure can fail in two ways. It can print something broken, which any system rejects. Or it can print a clean, well formed order that nobody placed. Our companion work calls the second kind a silent error, and it is the dangerous one. We present a web demo in which a fine-tuned 0.8B parameter model runs entirely inside the visitor's browser tab, through llama.cpp compiled to WebAssembly. No order text leaves the device. The page parses orders as the visitor types, scores every ticket with the PIZZA benchmark's own matcher ported to TypeScript, refuses anything off the menu with "?" so the model can face the open web, and lets visitors hunt for silent errors or try to cause one. Run in the browser on all 1,357 PIZZA test orders, the deployed model writes the same output as the server evaluation for 99.0% of them and reaches the same exact match (0.882); we also report its latency on 3 consumer laptops. Demo: https://wrong.cuyor.com

Emulating physics-based XCO2 retrievals across OCO-2 viewing modes with compact neural networks

Xianlei San, Chengxing Zhai, Hui Su, Kuai Dai, Shuangchen Du

cs.LG

Operational full-physics retrieval of the column-averaged dry-air mole fraction of carbon dioxide from satellite spectra is computationally demanding. We use singular value decomposition to compress spectra from the Orbiting Carbon Observatory-2 separately by band. Compact multilayer perceptrons use the resulting spectral scores, prior information, and viewing geometry to predict the difference between the full-physics retrieval and its operational prior. Adding the predicted difference to the prior yields an estimate of the retrieved mole fraction. The models were trained on 2015-2019 observations and validated on 2020 observations. In 2021-2025 tests, the mean annual root-mean-square errors relative to the Atmospheric Carbon Dioxide Observations from Space retrieval product were 0.89, 1.35, and 1.43 parts per million for ocean glint, land glint, and nadir observations, respectively. On East Asian nadir observations screened using the published criteria for the spectral Transformer, our model yielded a lower mean annual error than reported for that model in 2020-2022. These results show that compact neural networks using compressed spectra can efficiently emulate the full-physics retrieval across multiple viewing modes and years.

DECODE: Decomposable Environments for COntrolled Sequential-Decision Evaluation

Diego Silva Prado, Bruno Caldas de Souza, Tiago Amaral Penteado de Jesus, Ricardo Guimar\~aes Herrmann, Cristiano Breuel, Cristiano Bevitori Maffia De Oliveira

cs.LG, q-fin.RM, stat.ML, https://github.com/alpha-synthesis/decode-arxiv., Archived, release:, https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.24200270

When a sequential decision system falls short of an optimum, its score does not reveal which modifications would improve its decisions. DECODE is a benchmark for testing such modifications through controlled experiments on planning and available information. Its consumer-credit collections generator provides five partially observed environments that share known dynamics matrices and vary a memory parameter and process noise. The experiments change selected inputs or planning rules while keeping the rest of the solver fixed. Each comparison specifies the solver's state inputs, response inputs and planning horizon. On the deterministic base environment, which was selected for a large gap between six-month and one-month planning with the hidden state, extending planning from one to six months increases the scores of two gradient-boosted solvers by 15 to 17 percentage points of the exact optimum, computed with the hidden state and borrower traits. Even at this horizon, the best graded solver, whose repayment response is fitted on logs, remains 15.6 points below that optimum. In a specified intervention sequence, three changes to its state inputs and their evaluation during search lower its score by 6.3 points, and replacing its fitted repayment model with the true response, evaluated with hidden borrower traits, then raises it by 15.6, a net recovery of nine of those points. Because the intermediate effects are conditional on the repayment model and the order of the changes, they do not uniquely assign the gap to separate causes. Within these limits, DECODE makes hypotheses about solver performance testable through specified interventions under known dynamics and explicit information conditions.

f-Divergence Self-Play for Tabular Anomaly Detection via Large Language Models

Hoang Tran Vuong, Linh Ngo Van, Dang Nguyen, Thin Nguyen, Phuoc Nguyen, Mehrtash Harandi, Trung Le

cs.LG, cs.CL

Anomaly detection in tabular data poses significant challenges due to heterogeneous feature types-mixing numerical, categorical, and textual attributes, which complicate learning meaningful representations of normality. Recent work has applied large language models (LLMs) to this problem by serializing table rows as text sequences, yet these approaches rely on one-shot supervised fine-tuning that offers limited signal to tighten the model's description of normality. We propose DiSPaT, a self-play fine-tuning framework that strengthens the model's understanding of normal data. Building on the theoretical foundation of f-divergence minimization, we derive a tight approximation connecting our training objective to reducing the distributional gap between real normal data and model-generated samples. DiSPaT operates through an alternating optimization: at each iteration, the current policy generates synthetic samples that serve as pseudo-anomalies, while a critic discriminator learns to distinguish these from real normal samples; this signal drives policy updates that progressively align the model distribution with the true normal-data distribution. Extensive experiments on diverse benchmarks demonstrate that DiSPaT consistently outperforms prior LLM-based methods, deep learning approaches, and classical unsupervised detectors for tabular anomaly detection.

Where Tree Ensembles Decide: Structural Local Explanation with Decision Predicate Graphs

Sylvio Barbon Junior, Luca Longo

cs.LG, Supplementary, material, attached, as, an, ancillary, file

Tree ensembles are among the most accurate models for tabular data, yet the computation behind a single prediction is spread over hundreds of decision rules. Feature attributions and counterfactuals explain such predictions through the influence of features or a change of the input, leaving open which of the thresholds applied by the trees a prediction responds to, while structural explanations compress these rules and lose the trees behind them. To address this gap, we propose a local Decision Predicate Graph (local DPG), which gathers the rules executed for a sample into a verifiable record of the computation, with a class support that reproduces every explained prediction. From these rules we select the critical predicate, the executed predicate whose crossing is estimated to move the model most strongly toward a chosen class, which offers the user a concrete threshold and the trees that share it, without an external explainer. Across 16 datasets and four ensemble families, it separates differently predicted neighbours better than random and nearest-threshold predicates. A single crossing recovers the true class in 46-69% of wrong predictions, against 29-50% when the same rule is applied to the feature ranked first by TreeSHAP, a measure close to the selection criterion, and on planted faults it finds the faulty threshold in 46-71% of the resulting errors, against 6-21% at random. Although training-free edits of these predicates did not reduce the error on unseen data, the critical predicate complements attributions and counterfactuals by naming where a prediction can be changed.

WeatherDreamer: Learning a World Model from Multimodal Earth Observations

Kate Duffy, Elise Penn, Tristan Ballard, August Posch, Thomas J. Vandal

cs.LG, physics.ao-ph

Machine learning weather models now rival operational global forecasts but still start from analyses produced by separate, expensive data assimilation (DA) systems, which for global models are typically updated every six hours. Observation-to-observation models avoid this dependence, but most omit the recursive filtering cycle that lets DA systems carry a background state through sparse, irregular observations. We introduce WeatherDreamer, a world model that learns this cycle end-to-end from 18 global observing systems, without reanalysis. It infers a latent state from observations, carries it forward hour by hour with learned dynamics, corrects it each hour with new observations, and can predict what any of its observing systems would measure at any location. At held-out stations, its ensembles beat NOAA's Global Ensemble Forecast System (GEFS) for temperature, dew point and wind out to 12 h and are better calibrated than GEFS and the ECMWF ensemble. Precipitation, cloud-top height and solar irradiance, never inputs at inference, are inferred with skill matching or exceeding operational products at short leads. Against the first guess of NOAA's Gridpoint Statistical Interpolation (GSI) DA system, WeatherDreamer's short-range departures are within 10% of GSI's at surface stations and within 25% for moisture-sensitive microwave channels, but up to five times larger for temperature-sounding channels. Never given observation errors, its learned update assumes errors close to known instrument and retrieval errors of microwave sounders and scatterometer winds. WeatherDreamer also separates viewing geometry from atmospheric state, recovering 83-96% of observed limb darkening and producing seamless nadir-view imagery. Producing an hourly global analysis in about 1.5 minutes on one GPU, WeatherDreamer shows that assimilation and forecasting can be learned together directly from observations.

Deep Neural Networks Training Efficiency: Does Grokking Modify its Dynamics?

Eduardo Cueto-Mendoza, John D. Kelleher

cs.LG

We study whether grokking, the delayed emergence of out-of-distribution generalization after apparent overfitting, alters training efficiency in deep neural networks, where efficiency is measured as accuracy per unit energy. Using two canonical settings, modular arithmetic and MNIST, we analyze two neural network models under 16-, 32-, and 64-bit precision and use Orthograd to test whether grokking produces noticeable deviations from the typically decreasing dynamics of training efficiency. We consider both short training runs of up to 500 epochs and long training runs of up to 20,000 epochs, and in both cases identify grokking as a sharp improvement in test accuracy after a sustained train-test gap. In the short-horizon experiments, applying Orthograd at progressively stronger levels during early training does not induce grokking, and efficiency remains smoothly decreasing. In the long-horizon experiments, Orthograd induces grokking, but the efficiency curves remain approximately monotonic and show no appreciable local increase at the grokking transition. These results suggest that, in our setting, grokking does not materially alter overall training-efficiency dynamics.

Fuse-PINN: Combining Finite Element Methods and Physics-Informed Neural Networks to enable massively parallel simulations of non-linear PDEs

Stanislas Pamela, Nicolas Pailliez, Emmanuel Franck, Victor Michel-Dansac, Laurent Navoret, Vignesh Gopakumar

cs.LG, physics.plasm-ph

Research in the domain of Physics-Informed Neural Networks applied to PDE modelling has advanced significantly in recent years, providing a wide ecosystem of novel methods. However, one frequent limitation is the inability of PINNs to scale out on High-Performance Computing architectures to address more ambitious challenges, particularly non-linear physics models with turbulent dynamics in large spatial domains. Typical studies restrain demonstrations to toy models with simple physics equations, few variables, and rarely any complex non-linear dynamics evolved over long timescales. Part of the AI community has even adopted the idea that "PINNs don't scale". This work proves otherwise. We present a mathematically robust formulation of Finite-Element-Method for Neural Networks (FEM-NN) implemented into a comprehensive, modular framework: Fuse-PINN. This formulation provides the basis for a sclable parallelisation of Physics-Informed Neural Networks on GPU-based HPC systems. Furthermore, an Energy Natural Gradient Descent optimiser is implemented to allow high-precision convergence. Fuse-PINN is demonstrated on physics models relevant to Magnetic Confinement Fusion, including non-linear convective models with fine-scale dynamics. Near-perfect scaling is achieved on multi-GPU runs with increasing problem size.

PACT: A Decoder-Confidence-Guided Two-Round Receiver for Joint Channel and Phase-Noise Estimation in Sub-THz OFDM

Ruilin Wang, Xiaodai Dong

cs.LG, eess.SP

Frequency-selective fading and oscillator phase noise (PN) jointly impair coded sub-THz OFDM reception. We propose a decoder-confidence-guided two-round receiver combining a {pilot-aware complex transformer} (PACT) estimator, banded linear minimum mean-square error equalization, and neural normalized min-sum decoding. PACT jointly estimates the frequency-domain channel and effective time-domain PN through interacting complex-valued branches. Decoder-derived pseudo-pilots and continuous constellation-aware confidence guide second-round refinement through PACT inputs and attention, with parameters shared across rounds. At $E_b/N_0=30$~dB, the PACT receiver achieves approximately $70\%$ and $57\%$ lower code-block error rates than the best-performing learning-based baseline in the two benchmark settings, respectively. Frozen-weight tests further demonstrate applicability across channel/PN models, carrier frequencies, and FFT/bandwidth configurations.

Black-Box Uncertainty Quantification for Large Language Models via Ensemble-of-Ensembles

Wang Ma, Debarun Bhattacharjya, Junkyu Lee, Nhan H Pham, Harsha Kokel, Qiang Ji

cs.LG, cs.AI, 38, tables., Code:, https://github.com/wma17/Black-box_UQ_for_LLMs_Ensemble_of_Ensemble

Reliable use of large language models (LLMs) requires uncertainty estimates that separate ambiguity intrinsic to a prompt from genuine knowledge gaps. Bayesian and deep-ensemble methods deliver this aleatoric/epistemic decomposition in principle, but are computationally prohibitive at LLM scale and require white-box access. Existing black-box methods, in contrast, summarize to a single scalar that cannot make the distinction. We introduce a two-level ensemble that bridges this gap. Stochastic decoding of a fixed prompt forms the inner ensemble; meaning-preserving semantic perturbations of the prompt form the outer ensemble. Variability is measured in a continuous embedding space, and the law of total covariance gives an exact within-/between-perturbation decomposition to estimate aleatoric and epistemic uncertainty (AU/EU); it requires only black-box sampling access. On top of this estimator, we contribute three theoretical results that ground the framework: (i) a finite-sample bias identity for nested Monte Carlo with a closed-form bias-corrected epistemic estimator; (ii) a Bayesian bridge bounding the gap between perturbation-population AU/EU and Bayesian targets; and (iii) a separation result identifying a class of failure modes that any consistency-only black-box method provably misses while perturbation-based EU detects. Across five short QA benchmarks and four instruction-tuned LLMs spanning two families and two scales, our estimator is competitive with strong white-box baselines, and at a matched generation budget it matches leading black-box methods on error ranking while decomposing AU and EU; controlled paired interventions show that AU and EU respond selectively to distinct, actionable sources of failure: prompt ambiguity and model knowledge gaps; and a long-form pilot on the NQ long-answer split shows the framework extends to claim-level factuality without retraining.

Sample-efficient prediction of high-entropy alloy strength through outcome-aware robust sequential learning

Ahmed Shoyeb Raihan, Md Habibor Rahman, Ashish Nimbarte, Imtiaz Ahmed

cs.LG

Accurate prediction of high-entropy alloy (HEA) strength can guide composition and processing decisions; however, obtaining tensile-test data for HEAs can be costly and experimentally demanding. This study proposes Robust Outcome-aware Sequential Experimentation for High-Entropy Alloys (ROSE-HEA), a sample-efficient framework for predicting ultimate tensile strength (UTS) from alloy composition and processing conditions. Its acquisition policy anticipates how possible UTS outcomes update predictions and the support for complementary Gaussian process (GP) descriptions. It then reassesses the worst-case expected predictive risk remaining across a reference domain to select informative candidates. A retrospective evaluation on 501 HEA records compares ROSE-HEA with expected-improvement (EI) and upper-confidence-bound (UCB) Bayesian optimization (BO), random GP sampling, and eight conventional regressors across 30 matched runs with shared initial observations and test conditions. Starting from 25 initial observations and acquiring 100 additional responses, ROSE-HEA achieves the lowest mean root-mean-square error (RMSE), at 152.84 MPa, and the highest mean R^2, at 0.7443. Mean RMSE is reduced by 6.16% and 6.37% relative to EI and UCB, respectively. The selected observations improve prediction across six regressor families and provide broader design-space coverage. ROSE-HEA also surpasses the strongest conventional regressor's mean predictive performance using 125 rather than 185 observations, corresponding to 32.4% fewer training observations. These findings demonstrate the potential of outcome-aware robust sequential learning to improve UTS prediction in HEAs under constrained training budgets.

Asymptotic Margins and Feature Confinement in Conic Classification Heads under Softmax Cross-Entropy

Omer Nezih Gerek

cs.LG, eess.SP, https://github.com/ongerek/Conic

Classification heads built on conic functions give each class a bounded region of the feature space, and such regions are used to reject samples of unseen classes. We ask whether these regions also keep the features bounded when the head is trained with softmax cross-entropy. For a head whose class scores combine a linear term with a Euclidean norm, we first show that the region of each class is bounded by a quadric of revolution, whose eccentricity is the reciprocal of one learned ratio. We then attach to each class an asymptotic margin, which compares the growth of its score with the growth of all the other scores and can be computed by a second-order cone program. The loss of a class has infimum zero exactly when its margin is positive, and it then approaches zero only along diverging features. When the margin is negative, the loss is coercive and its minimizers lie in an explicit ball. Since softmax depends only on the differences between scores, a class with a bounded region can still have a positive margin, so bounded regions do not confine the features. The result extends to other norms, including the polyhedral conic functions. On CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100, all 1210 cells of 67 trained conic heads have positive margins, whereas a prototype head keeps nine of its ten cells bounded, as the theory predicts.

On numerical issues of divergences between nearly equal distributions

Brandon Amos

cs.LG, cs.NA, math.NA, stat.ML

Barron et al. (2026) recently identified numerical issues in the softmax and soft cross-entropy loss. We show that similar issues are present in standard divergence operations between categorical distributions. This includes the forward and reverse Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence and generalized Jensen-Shannon divergence (JSD), as well as Monte Carlo KL estimates and entropy. When the distributions are nearly equal, rounding and cancellation issues affect the forward and gradient computations. These issues can affect settings using divergences in optimization objectives, such as knowledge distillation, reinforcement learning, and proximal methods. We propose numerically stable reformulations and provide example implementations for each setting, validated by property-based tests.

WeightBench: Evaluating Language Models as Weight-Space Programmers

Aayush Marasini, Barsat Khadka, Nick Rahimi

cs.LG, cs.AI, https://github.com/Aayush-Marasini/WeightBench

Recent work shows that large language models (LLMs) can operate directly on the parameters of other neural networks. They can read what a network was trained to do, fix a network that fails, and write working networks from scratch, all without gradient descent. This capability has been termed Weight-Space Programming (WSP). Exploring this new paradigm systematically requires a robust benchmark. We introduce WEIGHTBENCH, a benchmark that evaluates LLMs of increasing capability on 42 WSP tasks across five tracks: reading, fixing and writing weights, recognizing edits that are impossible, and data-free compression. The networks whose weights the LLMs read and edit range from 2K-parameter control policies to 7B-parameter language models. Experimental benchmark results show that frontier agents can already read small networks and write working control policies without ever running them, but cannot yet patch language models, compress them without data, or tell an impossible edit from a hard one. On all 42 tasks, Claude Opus 5.5 reaches a macro score of 0.535 (Sonnet 5.5 0.466, Haiku 5.5 0.402, DeepSeek v4-flash 0.215); on the 38 tasks it was run on, GPT-6.1-sol reaches 0.568 against Opus's 0.546. Code, the task registry, our OS-level sandbox harness, and the live leaderboard are publicly available at https://github.com/Aayush-Marasini/WeightBench.

Pairing AIS with RF: Analysis of a Markovian Approach for Maritime Data Fusion

Daniel Grimm, Kyra Jongman, Renato Cordeiro Ferreira, Giuseppe Cascavilla, Alfredo Cuzzocrea, Willem-Jan Van Den Heuvel

cs.LG, cs.CR, Privacy, and, Security, of, Big, Data, of, IEEE, BigData, 2026

Monitoring maritime activities is important for safety and security, particularly for detecting illegal activities, but remains challenging due to the size and complexity of the maritime domain. The Automatic Identification System (AIS) is commonly used for this purpose, despite its known vulnerabilities. In contrast, space-based radio frequency (RF) observations provide an independent source of vessel activity but lack ship identification and therefore cannot identify vessels on their own. Following the CRISP-DM process, this study applies a Pair Hidden Markov Model (PHMM) to align individual RF detections with AIS trajectories, using temporal and spatial information while modeling noise and timing discrepancies. We evaluate it on NOAA AIS data with synthetic RF detections against nearest-neighbor baselines and analyze its errors with bias tests on trajectory length, sampling density, stationarity and AIS--RF distance. The length-corrected Forward score reaches a top-1 precision of $0.7776$ on the validation split of multi-candidate detections, where errors are possible, and an AUC of $0.637$ for detecting dark vessels among the detections that pass the prefilter. With a single RF detection, the score mostly reflects the AIS trajectory itself, and its errors favor longer and more stationary trajectories. A log-odds score, which compares the full model with a null model without match transitions, raises precision to $0.8580$ and AUC to $0.852$. Its length-corrected variant no longer shows a significant bias towards stationary trajectories and short inter-message distances, but still favors candidates farther from the RF detection. A time-weighted nearest-neighbor baseline without fitting remains ahead (precision $0.9013$, AUC $0.904$), so this single-detection, hand-designed PHMM formulation approaches but does not beat it.

Only as Safe as the Weakest Agent: A Break That Appears Only When the System Is Multi-Agent

Keegan Wang, Anantika Mannby

cs.MA, cs.AI, Agents, that, Manage, Agents, (Meta-Agents)

Major industry multi-agent systems like Codex and Claude Code can be broken by attacks that have long been unsuccessful against modern frontier models. AI Safety as a discipline has largely focused on finding the bounds and vulnerabilities of AI models used in isolation. Yet as humans increasingly deploy these models as agents and multi-agent systems, the same attacks that had been thought to be patched when using a model in isolation can break these same models under specific circumstances. We show that these system breaks are not purely theoretical, but can break Claude Code, Codex, and a reference harness, all unmodified and used with their own sub-agent mechanisms. We show the attack by asking agents to answer questions from a store of files on disk in which we have planted a forged record. Searching the store themselves, the strong model orchestrators commit almost zero harmful revisions in over 1,000 trials. However, if that same orchestrator model delegates to a cheaper sub-agent, which is what these harnesses are built to do, the same models on the same facts fail on 20/180, 44/180, 75/180 and 553/900. Thus, even when the system is driven by a strong model, the system's safety lands near the weak reader's, not the orchestrator's. Simulations over 18 models show the same effect in the delivery channel where five aggregators, who on their own were almost never unsafe, repeat a corrupted sub-agent's error in 751/757 trials. The safety of real-world agentic systems that use strong orchestrator agents and weaker sub-agents, and multi-agent systems in general, is defined not by the safety of the strong agent but by the safety of the weakest agent.

When Should a Virtual Patient Ask? RISP-Guided Proactive Information Seeking in Multi-Agent Patient Simulation

Jingyao Cai, Shuaijun Liu, Abdul Rehman, Xiaowan Li, Qintian, Thomas Dolby, Sue M Green, Xiaosong Yang

cs.MA

Virtual patient (VP) systems support nursing communication training by providing simulated encounters in which learners practise explaining care and responding to patient concerns. Although large language models (LLMs) support flexible dialogue, current approaches give less explicit attention to how patients' information needs develop and guide the content and timing of their questions. We draw on the Risk Information Seeking and Processing (RISP) model, which relates information seeking to perceived risk and the gap between perceived knowledge and desired understanding. Guided by this perspective, we propose the RISP Multi-Agent System (RISP-MAS), a VP system for patient-initiated information seeking. The system links ongoing assessment of the patient's perceptions and information needs to coordinated decisions about whether to seek information, when to ask and which uncertainty to address. Evaluation of nursing student-VP interactions suggested that RISP-MAS exhibited plausible patient information-seeking behaviour, as indicated by expert assessments, while student feedback reflected positive interaction experiences. This work extends patient simulation by connecting a psychological account of information needs to explicit decisions about question initiation. The approach offers nursing students opportunities to practise recognising unresolved patient concerns and tailoring explanations to patients' information needs.

Collective Tree Exploration with Predictions

Romain Cosson, Yichen Huang, Michael Mitzenmacher, Ali Vakilian

cs.DS, cs.MA

Autonomous trading agents need controls that prevent problematic orders without making legitimate tasks impractical. We study this aspect of agentic compliance with TradeGauntlet, a replayable limit-order-book simulator that separates detection, prevention, and execution cost. In 1,680 simulated sessions with Claude Haiku 5.5 and Sonnet 5.5, all conduct alerts were self-matches. Gates eliminated those alerts by rejecting self-crossing orders, while the aggregate-gate condition reduced Sonnet's two-sided fills by about 15%. A conduct reminder reduced Sonnet's alert rate but reduced Haiku's fills without a detectable alert benefit. These results show why zero alerts alone do not establish an agent's compliance. Scripted probes and two search methods test weaknesses beyond those observed in the LLM runs. The searches find split-order schedules that evade the implemented conduct rules; a learned baseline catches all 2,400 tested searched episodes but also flags 15% of a large maker's sessions. Aggregate caps reduce the response-dependent gain unevenly, with smaller reductions when only near-touch depth affects prices. A response fitted to five Nasdaq stocks and a separate ABIDES experiment expose the dependence of these estimates on market assumptions. A distinct adversarial prompt elicited refusals from Sonnet in all 240 rounds, yielding no candidate strategies to evaluate. We argue that this corollary fails in security: decisions there hinge on anomalous inputs, which are long-tailed (e.g., zero-day attacks) and therefore poorly served by likelihood fitting. Worse still, the capability brought about by scale (e.g., inferring the intended whole from a corrupted part) leads a LLM silently \emph{repair} the anomaly before any downstream security head ever sees it. We formalize this as a tradeoff between two quantities: the cognitive capability $K(W)=I(W;P)$ that a model holds about the world distribution, and the security-information retention $R(W)=\frac{I(Z;S)}{I(A;S)}$ that survives in its intermediate representation. We derive three information-theoretic bounds to connect them: an upper bound on restoration accuracy in terms of $K(W)$, an upper bound on $R(W)$ in terms of the restoration error, and a Fano lower bound on security-task error in terms of $R(W)$. These theorems approximately imply that usable security information is a product of how much security information survives and how well it can be extracted, indicating that optimal usable security information can be achieved at a certain model size. We state this as the \emph{condensing law of security information}: usable security information is unimodal in model size, so security generalization error is U-shaped and is minimized at a finite $N^{\star}$ rather than decreasing without bound. We develop a principal-agent test for when algorithmic decision-making should trigger disclosure obligations even when concerns about gaming or trade secrecy exist. The test, which is anchored in decision quality, relies on two diagnostics that can be disclosed without enabling gaming or revealing trade secrets: the error profile (false positive and false negative rates, overall and for each group) and the distribution of decision outcomes across groups. These diagnostics make visible whether decision-makers align design and implementation with the public interest or instead use opacity to obscure bias, inefficiency, or strategic shirking. Strong disparities across social groups, and asymmetries in the distribution of decision errors across outcome types, should trigger rebuttable presumptions favoring further disclosure. Where ground-truth error rates are unavailable, outcome distributions across groups can support the same presumption, and a decision-maker's inability to produce an error profile is itself diagnostic. The test thus treats fairness metrics as triggers for further disclosure and extends to generative and agentic systems through analogous metrics. The framework informs differentiated disclosure regimes (staged, partial, mediated, or audience-segmented). This worst-case result raises fundamental questions about the trainability of quantum AI models. We present a major advance towards resolving this trainability question by answering whether a given quantum circuit has a barren plateau. We do this using an analytical framework that bounds the gradient variance for arbitrary input states $\rho$ and observables $\mathcal{M}$ by decomposing the unitary representation of the associated compact Lie group. When the circuit segments on both sides of a gate form $2$-designs, we obtain the variance exactly and prove a sufficient condition for trainability: if $\rho^{\otimes 2}$ and $\mathcal{M}^{\otimes 2}$ are sufficiently concentrated on the same irreducible invariant subspace of polynomial dimension, and the differentiated gate moves this subspace by an inverse-polynomial amount, then the gradient variance is at least inverse-polynomial. We further present a sampling algorithm that estimates the gradient variance from runs of the ansatz, without constructing the representation-theoretic projectors. Our algorithm decides whether the variance exceeds a tolerance $\tau$ with the number of runs polynomial in $1/\tau$, including cases in which the bounds are inconclusive. The parameter $L$ plays the role of a Lipschitz constraint and is viewed as a regularization parameter: small values of $L$ enforce a more geometry-aware discriminator, while as $L$ increases the geometric regularization is progressively relaxed. It is a known result [3] that such divergences converge to the KL divergence as $L\to\infty$. In this paper, we characterize the asymptotic behavior of the optimal discriminators as $L\to\infty$ in different regimes: Firstly, under $Q\ll P$ and the Radon-Nikodym derivative $\frac{dQ}{dP}$ is bounded from above and bounded away from zero. On an edge FPGA, this state can exceed the on-chip memory and must be paged to external memory. This paper asks whether such paging dominates the cost of decoding (generating tokens). We built a hardware tile that computes one complete layer of a custom 369-million-parameter hybrid Gated DeltaNet model on an Agilex 5 FPGA and pages its recurrent state. The model state needs 6.2 MiB, whereas the device has 0.87 MiB of block memory. Paging adds 26.0 MB of memory traffic per decoded token, at most 11.2 percent of the calculated total. On the board at 75 MHz, with synthetic weights, one token takes 11.15 ms in this layer. The matrix-vector products with the weights take about 62 percent of this time and the state phase, including the paging, 13 percent. The tile is one node of a multicast mesh of tri-mode processing elements. We measure the error of the fixed-point arithmetic against floating point and check that the hardware agrees bit for bit with a fixed-point reference. In simulation, the state logic agrees with the reference on all 18 recurrent layers. On the board, six builds pass all 30 runs of 64 tokens and two runs of 1,024 tokens. For this model and memory interface, the delivery of the weights limits the decoding performance, and the recurrent state does not. State paging is therefore practical for such a model on an edge FPGA. arXiv:2611.18459 (*cross-listing*) Date: Fri, 13 Nov 2026 17:28:37 GMT (1037kb) Title: Callo: Serving Cache-Miss Requests with Speculative Decoding on Invalidated KV under Prefill-Decode Disaggregation Authors: Jianjun Ou, Matthew Lentz Categories: cs.DC cs.LG Comments: 24 pages ------------------------------------------------------------------------------ A diffusion equation can be rewritten as a transport equation driven by the score function of the evolving density. Code: https://github.com/chris-mrn/ScoreCLR_code ------------------------------------------------------------------------------ We study feedback-driven black-box processes across software engineering and machine learning. While many policies are developed under structural assumptions on the underlying systems, many real-world systems operate without such knowledge, necessitating black-box policies that lack a unified evaluation framework. A finite representation can succeed without recording an infinite environment in full when those asks share collective structure, rather than demand unrelated facts scattered arbitrarily across it. We formalize this phenomenon through future-task information, the minimum retained information needed to preserve decision value relative to the available evidence. A joint decision-regret state records posterior comparisons among actions for every permitted task. Under compactness assumptions, every retained representation admits a calibrated replacement based on this state, preserving all task regrets without increasing information; the resulting information-regret curve equals an operational coding rate. Two exact boundaries govern finite preservation: weak-star closure for a fixed action count, and the singular mass of the source-evidence law when all finite action menus are admitted.
